INTRODUCTION

fourteenth century, and the modern

reader may be inclined to object to
some features of its arrangement, as well as
to a certain leisureliness in the style, both
of which things savour of the period of its
composition. Let him not be discouraged,
however, by these unessential details. He is
not to expect a flood of eloquence, nor even
a collection of pious considerations calculated
to foster sensible devotion, for Hilton lays
claim to none of these things. He has a
higher aim in view. Expressing himself in
a very simple, almost catechetical manner,
in short chapters, reinforced by well-selected
quotations from Holy Scripture, he lays
down the main principles of a perfect life,
at the same time placing it within reach of
all souls of goodwill. He seeks only to be
lucid, to encourage, and to convince. Prac-
tical before everything, he deliberately avoids
hard terms, hyperbole or theoretical argu-
ment, and does not shrink from employing
homely expressions for the sake of clearness.

v

T Scala Perfectionis dates from the

INTRODUCTION

Anyone who uses this book for the purpose
for which it was intended, for instruction
and self-improvement, will quickly discover
its real value. Moreover, the lucidity of
the style, the masterly fashion in which the -
English language, while yet in its infancy, is
manipulated in order to give expression to
abstract and psychological ideas, together
with the gentle gravity of tone and the pic-
turesque imagery which lend so much life
and charm to his writings—all these win for
Hilton a special place among masters of
English prose, as well as among authorities
on the interior life.

There is no doubt that it was by contact
with the Fathers and Doctors of the Church
that Hilton’s mind and pen alike were trained.
Even if the very peculiarities of his style and
his use of the syllogistic method of explaining
by question and answer did not show him to
be a man well-versed in the arguments
of the Schools, it would still be easy to
recognise in him a theologian nourished upon
tradition. In several manuscripts, too, he
is spoken of as Master, a title at that time
reserved for theological doctors, and one of
them expressly calls him a doctor of .the
University of Paris, with which little or
nothing in his ideas or opinions would be
inconsistent. He well deserves the praise
bestowed on him in the Harleian MS. (6579);

vi

INTRODUCTION

homo venerabilis scientia . . . composuit librum
plenum catholica doctrina et aedificatione. It
may be added that Hilton’s opinions are,
generally speaking, those of the Augustinians
of Paris in the second half of the fourteenth
century; while accepting much of Saint
Thomas’ doctrine, he still preserves a certain
number of ideas peculiar to Saint Augustine.
These last details are among the rare indica-
tions to be found regarding the life and person
of our author.: They are gleaned, like the
rest:of the information we possess, from the
internal evidence of the manuscripts. Cer-
tain of these, and among them the oldest,
were written in Yorkshire dialect; it is,
therefore, supposed that Hilton himself must
have been a native of that county, and a
member of one of the noble families of the
time. In this case.he must have met with
adversaries among his own kin, for Sir
Reginald de Hilton was an ardent disciple
of Wycliffe’s notions. But although the
argument from the manuscripts carries
weight, it is not conclusive as to the writer’s
actual family, for the name Hilton was
then common in England and especially in
Yorkshire:

We learn at least from the copyists that
Walter Hilton was a Canon Regular of
Saint Augustine and spent part of his’ re-
ligious life at the Augustinian Priory of

Vii

INTRODUCTION

Saint Peter at Thurgarton in Nottingham-
shire, where he died, March 24,1396. Apart
from the evidence of a single manuscript
in which he is styled governor, it would
not seem that he was ever superior of his -
monastery; his name does not occur in the
list of priors. Existing remains of the Pre-
Reformation buildings show that Thurgarton
possessed a dignified beauty fully worthy of
the Order which raised Dorchester Abbey,
and of the county which could boast of
Southwell Minster among its religious
houses.

Hilton was the author of other works |
besides the Scala Perfectionis. Among them
are two small treatises generally considered
authentic, which have been published—e.g.,
The Song of Angels and the Letter to a Devout
Man of Secular Estate, teaching him how to
lead a Spiritual Life therein. Other of his
writings remain unpublished, such as the
Letter on the Origin and Prerogatives of the
Religious Life and the Treatise on Images
against the Heretics. Although it is not
certain that all the writings ascribed to Hilton
are from his own pen, the very fact of their
being attributed to him proves that he was
looked upon as the head of a School, and to
such a one the works of his followers would
commonly be assigned. He was famous
enough to have been supposed the author

Vili

INTRODUCTION

of the first three books of the Imitation of
Christ when it was first current in England,
under the title of the Musica Ecclesiastica,
an idea which still finds supporters at the
present day. But the likeness between the
two works is sufficiently explained by the fact
that they both deal with the same subjects
and draw upon the same sources.

The date of Hilton’s death calls for
mention as showing that special interest
attaches to the historical period covered by
his lifetime. He lived under Edward III
and Richard II, and although he did not
survive until the latter’s deposition in 1399,
he must have seen the revolutionary move-
ment which resulted in the taking of London
by Wat Tyler in 1381, besides being one of
Wycliffe’s contemporaries. The Scala Per-
fectionis must have been written in the midst
of the Hundred Years War, during the great
Schism of the West, amid the general up-
heavals of thought and doctrine caused by
the preaching of the Lollards, and still more
by the popular risings, labourers’ strikes and
other conflicts, religious, political and social,
which made this period one of the most
turbulent and anxious in the-whole of English
history. Although all these troubles had
not stifled faith and devotion in the nation
at large, it must be admitted that the cultiva-
tion of mystical theology appears to have

ix A2

INTRODUCTION

met with scant encouragement in many of
the older monasteries, preoccupied as they
were with temporal concerns; and the same
applies to the Mendicant Orders which were
very prosperous at this time and much -
involved in worldly business. Driven out
everywhere else, mysticism found a refuge
with the solitaries who, whether living as
hermits in the open country, or as recluses
shut into some cell adjoining a church, still
remained numerous in fourteenth-century
England. The treatises compiled either by
the anchorites themselves, or for their use,
form a unique group of writings, and con-
stitute what may be termed the School of
English Fourteenth Century Mystics. It
is to this school that Hilton belongs.
Although nothing warrants the supposition
that he himself led the life of a solitary, he
is evidently well acquainted with the needs
and aspirations of such a state. His work
is addressed to a recluse, and although
nothing is known regarding her, it is unlikely
that in this he was merely employing a
literary device. She seems to have known
the author well and could call his face to
mind (Bk. I, chap. Ixxxi). She is obliged
by her rule to recite the Breviary (Bk. I,
chap. xxvii), which was not generally the
case among anchoresses. She is recom-
mended to read the Holy Scriptures (Bk. I,
X s

INTRODUCTION

chap. xv), and she understands the psalms
which she repeats; she must therefore have
known Latin. These particulars justify us
in believing that she belonged to some
religious order. This was often the case,
and many Benedictine Abbeys, for example,
possessed cells—reclusorium, anchoragium—
where those nuns retired who were eager for
greater perfection and called to the solitary
state. Another striking feature of Hilton’s
book may be noted in this connection. He
presupposes a much higher moral tone,
especially regarding temperance and chastity,
than do the older rules written for the anchor-
esses. It is certain, however, that the Scala
Perfectionis was not written solely for them,
and when this is realised, its lofty spirituality
becomes all the more remarkable. The
circulation of such a work proves that the
religious life of fourteenth-century England
is not to be judged by the accounts of the
satirists, nor yet by the invectives of Wycliffe,
a conclusion which gains weight from a
study of other writers belonging to the same
school.

That the mystics present many features
in common is beyond dispute, and to a
certain extent they supplement and complete
each other. Richard Rolle, the Hermit of
Hampole (f 1349), the first in order of date,
is the most prolific of them all, but being

Xi

INTRODUCTION

endowed with a remarkably original mind
and great lyrical gifts, he gave rein to the
impulse of his peculiar genius, and, even
when he reasons or teaches, he hardly does
more than present us with the reflection of `
his own temperament. Hilton draws largely
upon his works and makes it clear that he
does so, without, however, employing actual
quotations, according to the usual medieval
custom. In the same way he makes use of
the Cloud of Unknowing and the letters
annexed to that treatise in the manuscripts—
anonymous works dating from the middle of
the fourteenth century, and perhaps the
most daringly original of the writings of
all this group of mystics, although they deal
only with certain particular points regarding
contemplation. Hilton knows how to give
the pith of their doctrine while stripping it
of everything that might appear too difficult,
as, for instance, of the passages recalling
Denys the Areopagite. Finally there exists
so strong an affinity between Hilton’s work
and the Revelations of Dame Julian of
Norwich that some have maintained that
she must have read the Scala Perfectionis.
This remains to be proved; we shall refer to
the point later. In any case it is impossible
to deny the close parallel in the spiritual
outlook of the two writers—a parallel which
frequently extends to the very language they
xii .

INTRODUCTION

employ. But Hilton alone, among those
enumerated, wrote a fully systematised
treatise for the purpose of instruction, epito-
mising the age in which he lived, while
borrowing whatever is best from the works
of those who preceded him. Although he
abstains from controversy and is content to
place things in their just light by a sober
exposition of the truth, he shows himself
thoroughly acquainted with the mind of his
age and carefully warns his readers against
contemporary error. ‘They are placed on
their guard in this way, against the new
notions disseminated by the Lollards, as well
as against whatever is too personal in Richard
Rolle. He strongly upholds the traditional
principles of the old spirituality against. the
fierce attacks which were then being made
upon them, and here he is quite in accord-
ance with his predecessors including Rolle
himself. He clearly envisages the new trend
of religious thought to which Wycliffe had
lent the support of his word, but which he
did not create, for it is recognisable at a
considerably earlier date, and was consoli-
dated by later Protestant writers.

Hilton himself is as faithful to the old
spirituality as he is submissive to the teaching
of the Church. Beyond Richard Rolle,
nay, beyond the masters of the School of
Saint Victor who inspired him as well as all

xiii

INTRODUCTION

his contemporaries—though he knew how to
simplify their teaching—he reaches back to
Saint Bernard, Saint Anselm, and the main
stream of monastic spirituality which in-
fluenced England so profoundly down to
the thirteenth century. Through the Anglo-
Norman monks he is directly linked up with
Saint Gregory, Saint Denys, Saint Augustine,
and the Fathers of the Desert upon whom he
was bred up, and whom he simply styles
the Fathers when quoting fromthem. Hilton
is indeed among the clearest witnesses in a
great line of tradition, and it is not one of
the least of his virtues to have presented the
old spirituality in synthetic form just when
it was being attacked and distorted. Well
might Hilton’s first publishers inform their
readers at the conclusion of this treatise that
the Scala Perfectionis contains notable teach-
ing, worthy of all acceptance. It is not a
question of Hilton’s doctrine alone, but that
of all Christian antiquity, of which he merely
hands down the torch. The brief analysis
of the work which follows will show this, as
well as providing an opportunity of explain-
ing certain aspects of Hilton’s thought and
style which may not be quite clear to his
twentieth-century readers.

The Scala Perfectionis, as its title shows,
treats of the means by which the soul is
Xiv ;

INTRODUCTION

led upwards towards perfection—1z.e., union
with God. This union is only to be reached
by charity. Such are the premises which
the author assumes as known and unques-
tioned, and upon which therefore he does
not enlarge. But all those who seek union
with God do not follow the same path.
Some manifest their charity chiefly in works
of mercy towards their neighbour: whether
corporal, such as caring for the poor or the
sick, or spiritual, such as preaching or the
charge of souls (Bk. I, chap. ii). Others,
on the contrary, “ for love of God forsake
all worldly riches, worship, and outward .
business, and wholly give themselves, body
and soul, after their might and cunning, to
the service of God by ghostly occupation’
(Bk. I, chap. iii). Those in the first category
lead the active life, the second are contem-
platives. Although Hilton describes rather
than defines these two classes, he says enough
to show that he is in entire agreement with
the traditional teaching on the subject, as
it is found, for instance, in Saint Thomas,*
and thus he calls the active life one which
is mainly devoted to external works, whether
spiritual or corporal, and the contemplative
that which has for its object the contempla-
tion of divine truth.

However elementary these ideas may

* JJ da TI dae, Q. CLXXIX—CLXXXII.
XV

INTRODUCTION

seem, they possess an importance in Hilton’s
teaching which must not be overlooked. He
speaks ofa life and a state (Bk. I, chaps..i, xvi),
and for either a life or a state to exist, properly
speaking, it is not enough to have a series”
of acts or even habits, for habits may be
superficial and unproductive of any vital
modification of character. It is necessary
that the occupation which gives its name to
the life or state should take the principal
place therein, and that it should be set in
relation to that occupation as to its aim and
object. There must be a series of operations
. sufficiently important to control the whole
field of activity and sufficiently habitual to
become easy, natural, and even in some sort
pleasurable. ‘There must be frequent acts,
but they must be mainly such as dispose
the soul towards a given end. Doubtless
this direction of spiritual activity is not
strictly exclusive; an active soul may and can
practise contemplation, a contemplative will
have to perform some external works. But
even so—and this is the constant teaching
of the Summa—one of these two kinds of
life must necessarily predominate and control
the general tendency of every act performed.
Hilton’s mind on these points is quite
unmistakable, and in order to understand
it clearly, the word life must be taken in its
fullest: possible sense. He does not lose
xvi ;

INTRODUCTION

time, moreover, in drawing comparisons
between the two lives. Bent only upon
pointing out the way of perfection to his
followers, he begins at once to speak to them
of that contemplative life which tradition
has always recognised as the better and more
meritorious of the two. He addresses him-
self to those who have turned wholly to God,
renouncing all preoccupation with exterior
objects. To lead them to their goal, he does
not simply recommend pious practices or a
method of prayer to his disciples; he offers
them a particular kind of life, and herein
lies the whole gist of his book.

Before proceeding to enunciate the laws
of this mode of life, it is necessary first to
describe its principal activity, that which
gives it its name and determines its object.
Here again our author does not disappoint
us, for he devotes several very condensed
chapters to this subject (Bk. I, chaps. iv-x).
Once more it may prove useful to emphasise
ideas which were quite familiar to devout
persons of Hilton’s age, but which stand out
in striking contrast to the vagueness and
inaccuracy of modern devotional termin-
ology. When once it is liberated from the
shackles of corporeal imagery—and Hilton
places his readers on their guard against a
type of metaphor so open to misconstruction
—contemplation becomes essentially the

xvii

INTRODUCTION

“ simple act ” of the Schoolmen, or, accord-
ing to our author, “a knowledge and a
viewing ” of the intellect which is turned to
God. It is, therefore, neither pious fervour
(Bk. I, chaps. ix, lxxv; Bk. II, chap. xxix), ©
nor a process of the imaginative faculty by
which the scenes of our Lord’s life, for
example, are called up; for this last is the
function of meditation (Bk. I, chaps. v, xxv).
Nor is it to be confounded with certain
sensible phenomena which may sometimes
accompany it (Bk. I, chaps. x-xi; Bk. II,
chap. xli). But, on the other hand, it would
imply an utter misapprehension of the
nature of Christian contemplation to make it
an act of the intellect alone, and Hilton
hastens to point out that the contemplation
of learned men, exercised almost wholly
through mental effort and by the light of
reason, is in itself of no value and “‘ tasteless
as water ” (Bk. I, chap. iv). It lacks one
essential element, the supernatural. Setting
aside, therefore, this first kind of contem-
plation, he distinguishes two other types
according to the light by which the mind is
enlightened, which we may call the second
and third.

The third and highest kind “which. . .
lieth both in cognition and in affection ” is
not strictly defined as extraordinary con-
templation, the name it often bears to-day;

xviii ;

INTRODUCTION

but, to use the more sober language of the
Scala, it is the fruit of a special grace. It
is an infused contemplation, and the soul
illumined by the gifts of wisdom and under-
standing is uplifted by it to a knowledge of
God and spiritual things. It is a grace of
which God grants more or less, to whom He
wills, but generally it is confined, especially
in its fulness, to solitaries and to those
leading the contemplative life (Bk. I, chaps.
viii, ix).

As for the second kind it “ lieth principally
in affection without light of understanding
of ghostly things,” and is thus specifically
distinguished from the third kind. Although
it is equally a grace, and a great one, there is
nothing intrinsically exceptional about it.
In its lower forms it is bestowed on many
souls, and even in its highest stages it can be
regarded as the normal outcome of the con-
templative life faithfully pursued, the usual
consequence of a way of living sanctioned
and organised by the Church and never
barren of result for those who are called to
it by God. It may indeed take on different
aspects, presenting a diversity which has
puzzled certain writers, but the same formal
reason underlies them all; in all contempla-
tion is practised by the light of faith (Bk. I,
chaps. vi, XXV, XXVIi, etc.).

Hilton condenses this preliminary analysis

xix

INTRODUCTION

of his subject, for he will have frequent
occasion to revert to it throughout the
remainder of his work. Later we shall find
him characterising the deeper elements of
that mystical life whose course of develop-
ment does not always coincide with that of
contemplation (Bk. I, chap. xcii; Bk. II,
chaps. xxix, xxxi); here he contents himself
with pointing out to the soul what she will
verify by her own experience. As long as
the intellect is exercised in the obscurity of
faith, the soul will be especially sensible of
the happiness, recollection, and devotion
resulting from her adherence to God. Little `
by little the emotional part of this contem-
plation subsides (Bk. I, chap. xxxi); recollec-
tion increases together with the facility for
keeping an ever closer hold upon God, and
the soul makes her home in a higher region
where she feels herself to be at peace and
fortified against trials (Bk. I, chaps. vii, xxxi);
in a word she may attain the highest stage in
this second kind of contemplation, without,
however, having experienced any extraordin-
ary phenomenon. It is necessary to read the
account of this gradual process in the text
itself, noting wherein the second kind of
contemplation differs from the third, to
which, however, little by little it leads up and
prepares the way. But, after placing them
against each other in careful contrast, Hilton
XX ;

INTRODUCTION

seems, in the end, hardly to separate them
at all, proposing both kinds simultaneously—
though with some needful warnings—before
the holy ambition of a soul sufficiently
resolute to climb the ladder of perfection
(Bk. I, chap. vii).

When once the goal is in sight, it becomes
a question of knowing by what means it is
to be reached. Contemplation is, indeed,
a grace, but it is necessary that we, on our
side, should place ourselves in favourable
conditions to receive that grace and make
good use of it. Otherwise we could not
lay claim to the gift which would then come
to be rightly described as extraordinary and
exceptional in character (Bk. II, chap. xx).
Hilton, therefore, goes on to teach us the
conditions under which this preparation is
to be made (Bk. I, chap. iii). The first of
these belongs purely to the natural order
and is one that was not unfamiliar to the
Neo-Platonists. Hilton mentions it at the
outset (Bk. I, chap. i), without, however,
pausing to work out the point, since it was
a condition already fulfilled by the recluse
to whom he wrote. It consists in withdraw-
ing as far as possible from outward occupa-
tions and preoccupations, and living in great
tranquillity of body and mind (Bk. I, chap. vii).
It is obvious that those who concern them-

XXi

INTRODUCTION

selves with external matters, particularly
if they do so at their own choice, run small
chance of arriving at this habitual and lofty
state of contemplation (Bk. I, chap. xxii and
passim). To this first condition others are
superadded. There are some practices,
employments, and mortifications which dis-
pose the soul to contemplation, and are
therefore to be recommended in preference
to certain others. Some among them form
the very substance of our treatise, and will
find mention or discussion below. It may
be said at once that this discipline in view
of contemplation, this precise adaptation of `
means to an end, is among the most original
and successful portions of Hilton’s ascetical
teaching. Other requirements of a more
general character are grouped together at
the outset of the work (Bk. I, chaps. xiii-
xxxvi). Among them are prayer and medi-
tation, to which our author devotes what is
quite a little treatise, on account of their
importance in view of contemplation (Bk. I,
chaps. xxiv-xxxvi).

Prayer, for Hilton, consists in raising the
desire of his heart to God. It is a highly
efficacious means of preparing for grace and
being “ cleansed by fire from desire.” It
makes us know our faults and helps us to
correct them (Bk. I, chaps. xxiv, xxv).
There are different degrees of prayer, and

xxii ;

INTRODUCTION

Hilton mentions them all up to the very
highest, at the same time concentrating
upon what is needful for the instruction
of a contemplative soul, without leading
her into curious and fruitless investigations.
All his own preference is evidently for
the prayer which is inspired by the
Church (Bk. I, chaps vii, xxvii; Bk. II,
chap. xlii).

As for meditation, he is well-acquainted
with its subject-matter ; the vices and virtues,
the truths of religion, the Humanity and
Passion of our Saviour Jesus Christ; but he
does not make it either, on the one hand,
an exercise for beginners, to be cast aside as
soon as possible, or, on the other hand, the
one unchangeable, fixed and final practice
of all devout persons. In his eyes medita-
tion runs parallel to prayer, but must on no
account be substituted for it. Like Richard
of Saint Victor, he wishes it to lead on to
contemplation, and he does not advise any-
one to dispense themselves from it at the
beginning of their spiritual course, and
without discretion (Bk. I, chap. xxviii). He
also points out that it cannot be bound down
under fixed rules, but must depend on the
grace given to each one (Bk. I, chap. xxxiv).
Again and again he insists upon the danger
that lies in becoming fettered in one’s spiritual
progress by too close an attachment to one

xxiii

INTRODUCTION

type of devotional exercise (Bk. I, chap. xci;
Bk. II, chap. xviii, etc.).

The account of these general dispositions
is followed by advice on the difficulties to
which beginners are exposed (Bk. I, chaps.
XXXVI1i-Xxxix), and this advice leads on by a
natural transition to the purification of the
soul and the acquirement of the virtues.
This is, after all, the most important of all
the works which prepare the soul for con-
templation, since without it solitude, pious
practices, and austerities would alike be
worthless, while it can, in a measure, atone
by itself for the want of them. This subject
occupies the whole of the latter part of the
first book (Bk. I, chaps. xl-xciii; cf. xiv, xcii).

The importance given by Hilton to this
portion of his theme is more than justified ;
according to the word of the Gospel only
the clean of heart shall see God (Bk. I,
chap. xv; Bk. II, chaps. xi, xxxix). In many
places also Hilton urges upon his disciples
to devote themselves to all those practices
both corporal and spiritual which may con-
tribute to the perfect purification of the soul.
It is not enough to have gained sufficient
self-control to repress the passions and avoid
exterior faults, nor even to practise the
virtues courageously. This constitutes, in-
deed, an indispensable groundwork, to the
absolute necessity of which Hilton is fully

XXiv

INTRODUCTION

alive, but he does not develop this part
of his subject further. It is to be found, he
says, in all books treating of the devout life
(Bk. II, chap. xx). Moreover, he is address-
ing himself to a soul already trained. He
insists rather on the necessity of mounting
higher in order to avoid the risk of falling
into a delusion which may prove fatal (Bk. I,
chaps. li, lv, xcii; Bk. II, chaps, xx, xxxvi).
The soul has entered upon her course with
fervour; she practises religious observance
and bodily mortification punctiliously; she
performs acts of virtue with a certain heroism
not wholly free from strain and over-empha-
sis; and then she believes herself to have
arrived at the goal, or, at any rate, not to be
far from it. But even if the revolts of the
flesh cease for a time, and prayer becomes
easy, or if, on the other hand, the soul believes
herself favoured with particular lights, there
is grave risk that she will be self-satisfied,
and fulfil in a greater or less degree the de-
scription of a hypocrite (Bk. I, chap. lix),
or an illuminata (Bk. II, chap. xxvi), so
powerfully delineated for us by Hilton.
Even supposing that these dangers have
been avoided, there must be no resting upon
one’s oars. These early achievements may
possibly suffice for performing the works
of the active life; one may nurse the sick,
preach or teach, and that profitably, albeit
XXV

INTRODUCTION

with passions incompletely mortified. But
to obtain the grace of contemplation, it is
necessary to mount higher, the ladder of
perfection: must be climbed; it is needful
that the virtues should not merely exist
“in the mind and in the will” (Bk. I,
chap. xiv), but that they should have entered
into the very temperament, so as to be really
loved and practised joyfully and easily.
And this is a long and difficult work, in view
of which the soul must arm herself with
courage (Bk. I, chap. xl; Bk. II, chap. xx).
It involves nothing less indeed than the
carrying out in ourselves of a radical reform,
or, to give the word reform the full sense.
which it bears in the book, to make a fresh
production or creation, to issue an entirely
new edition of our ego (Bk. II, chap. xxviii).

The teaching of Hilton on this subject is
only a development of that of Saint Paul
(Bk. I, chaps. xli, Ixxxv; Bk. II, chap. xxxi).
It is a fundamental doctrine with the Apostle
and the basis of all his moral teaching, that
we ought to transform ourselves and become
a new creature (2 Cor. iv), putting off the
old man and clothing ourselves with the new
(Eph. iv); dying with Christ in order to rise
again with him and live a new life (Rom. v
10-11). This absolute transformation is
for him the essential condition for the attain-
ment of the supernatural fulness which he

XXVi

INTRODUCTION

begs on his knees for his disciples from the
Father of our Lord Jesus Christ (Eph. iii-iv).
Two passages from Scripture seem to have
provided Hilton with the outline and heads
of his treatise. In the first Saint Paul
desires that, after having borne in ourselves
the image of the earthly man, the first Adam,
fallen and corrupt (1 Cor. xv), we should
substitute for it the image of the heavenly
man, our Saviour Jesus Christ. In the
second passage, which occurs in the third
chapter of the Epistle to the Colossians, he
enumerates everything which we must put
to death or cast away, and invites us to
acquire the virtues of the new man in order:
to‘come to the full knowledge of God.

This is to be henceforth the sole theme of
the Scala Perfectionis (cp. Bk. I, chap. xlii;
Bk. shigichap? xxx). o/fhexénd=oficther first
book deals with the work of mortification,
the destruction of everything connected with
fallen man, the “image of sin” and its
members (Bk. I, chap. Ixxxv); the second
book teaches us how to form anew in our-
selves the ‘“image of our Lord.” In actual
fact, the constructive work is inseparable
from the destructive, and although he dis-
tinguishes them: for the sake of clearness,
Hilton doesinot make any practical severance
between’ them. «:Indeed, it would: be truer
to say that he reinstates them in the position

xxvii

INTRODUCTION

which they commonly held under the older
mystical system. In order to cleanse and
detach the soul, he begins by gathering her
within herself; then, to foster her growth,
he raises her above herself, up to Almighty
God: Ab exterioribus ad interiora ; ab interior-
ibus ad superiora. In order to destroy ‘‘ the
image of sin ” in the soul, it is necessary to
enter into ourselves (Bk. I, chaps. xlii, lii)
and to learn to recognise the evil tendencies
which lie concealed at the bottom of our
hearts. Hilton will make this examination
of conscience with his reader (Bk. I, chaps.
lvi-Ixxii), but it is the way in which he carries
out his analysis, even more than his account
of the seven deadly sins and their deep roots
in ourselves, which demands .all our attention.
Contemplative souls—and with them many
active spirits also—are only too prone to
introspection. They must be taught to
know themselves and to be watchful, but
they must not be encouraged in endless self-
analysis. Hilton insists upon this point,
and conducts his study of the ego in such
a way as to teach the soul how to escape
from and go beyond herself. To achieve
this he turns her very early in her course
directly to God. Following the doctrine
so frequently met with in the teaching of
the Fathers, he reminds us that we have
been created in the image and likeness of the
xxviii

INTRODUCTION

sacred Trinity. The beauty and dignity
with which the soul was clothed, and from
which she has fallen by sin, are contrasted
with her present state, and, confronted with
the picture of her own deformity and
wretchedness, she desires to win back what
she has lost. Hilton shows her that she can
recover it only in Jesus and by Jesus. It is
towards Jesus that she must tend, and this
tendency will be an encouragement, at the
same time causing everything that hinders
union with Him whom she seeks to stand out
sharply in its true light, and thus aid her in
knowing and correcting herself. This tend-
ing or aspiration towards God is thus a
matter of great importance, and Hilton makes
it the first step to be taken in the work of
reform, devoting several chapters to it in
this section of his book (Bk. I, chaps. xliv-li).
It is not so much a question of finding
ourselves as of finding our Lord; we have to
go forth from our egoism and turn whole-
heartedly towards God. In spite of its
having come down to us from the Middle
Ages, and even from the Fathers of the
Desert, this teaching has lost none of its
force to-day.

Having set the soul in her true course,
Hilton now makes her pass all her faults in
review. ‘True to his plan he does not dwell
upon outwardly sinful acts, from which he

XXIX

INTRODUCTION

supposes his readers already freed; he insists
rather upon those interior motions and evil
tendencies which remain in the soul, often
without her own knowledge. All these
miseries are traceable to a common cause,
to wit the deep root of pride, the inordinate
love of self at which we strike a blow every
time we seek after God (Bk. I, chaps. xlii-
xliii). It is necessary to exterminate this
root if we would destroy the image of sin.
As long as we do not dry up this poisoned
spring, our very virtues themselves will be
imperfect and insufficient (Bk. I, chap. lv).
The end of the first book is devoted to
this inward conflict for the destruction of
the root of sin (Bk. I, chaps. Ixxii-xciii).
Since it is a wholly interior and spiritual
struggle, the means for ensuring victory
must be adapted to their end. To take
purely exterior measures would be useless,
at times even dangerous. Our author points
out plainly what we have to do, but at the
same time he does not fail to reckon with
the part played by grace and divine action.
He knows how to convince us of our own utter
inability to accomplish this work by our-
selves, and thus places us in a frame of mind
essential for the contemplative (Bk. I, chaps.
xxxiii, xliv, xlvii). Later on, in a series of
short, closely-packed chapters, he shows us
the means which we ourselves have to
XXX

INTRODUCTION

employ. Space forbids our entering into
a full account of them, as our present task
‘is simply to facilitate the reading of the
Scala and not to render it superfluous. One
may note, however, the caution given here,
not to proffer the whole of this teaching to
active souls. Some of them would certainly
be disconcerted by the exclusively spiritual
nature of the methods suggested. Accus-
tomed to external action, and the exigencies of
such action, they are prevented from realising
their own secret tendencies or attaching
weight to them, and thus they may be
inclined to find the weapons placed in their
hands by Hilton altogether too light for their
purpose. Who knows whether the aptitude
to make use of such weapons, as well as the
courage needed to bear without flinching
the special suffering which they inflict
(Bk. I, chap. lxxxix, etc.) be not a good
criterion of suitability for the contemplative
life? Almighty God will, nevertheless, pro-
vide active souls with other means of puri-
fication, incidental to the very course of their
activity, provided only that they do not try
to impose their methods upon contempla-
tives. These last are labouring to rectify a
wrong attitude, as it were, in the soul, and
they must be allowed to pursue the work of
correction even though they may do so
awkwardly at first. It is an undertaking
XXXi

INTRODUCTION

whose results will long remain invisible, since
everything takes place in that secret region
where the deepest springs of action have
their rise. Time must be allowed for the
truth thoroughly to penetrate the soul, and
change its very axis so to speak. There is,
in truth, only one means of doing this—
viz., to replace self-love by the love of God.
This is Hilton’s doctrine (Bk. I, chaps. li,
Ixiii, Ixviii, etc.), as well as Saint Augustine’s.
When once the image of sin has been recog-
nised in us, the real way to destroy it is by
creating the image of Jesus in our souls
through charity.

Whoever has put into practice the teachings
of the first book of the Scala Perfectionts
will most undoubtedly have hastened the
destruction of self-love through the growth
of charity. But the second book goes on
to point out a more excellent way, and leads
us to the very summits of the supernatural
life. This second book can be divided into
three portions. The early chapters (Bk. II,
chaps. i-xix) cover the Christian life, the
foundation and starting-point of all the
higher states which, after all, are only the
outcome of the gifts and graces implanted
in us by Baptism. Hilton, basing himself
upon Saint Anselm, reminds us of the
importance of faith and of the redemption
in the supernatural life. He makes use of

XXXil

INTRODUCTION

the opportunity afforded by introducing
these theological ideas in order to refute
several of Wycliffe’s errors which might
otherwise disturb or confuse his readers.
At the same time he gives them some very
sound practical advice, pointing out the
danger of halting by the way (Bk. II, chap.
xviii), the obstacles and delays to be expected
(Bk. II, chaps. xi, xviii), and, finally, the
necessity of believing in grace which is neither
seen nor felt (Bk. II, chaps. v, ix, xi).

But the author’s main object in pausing
to consider the state of beginners and those
on the way is, undoubtedly, that he may
compare and contrast it with the condition
of perfect souls, of whom he is to speak
henceforth. He lays stress upon the radical
difference between the two categories in
order to trace out a line of conduct for his
readers. The Christian striving to cast off
the old man and go to God is reformed in
faith alone. If he mounts higher, he will
arrive at what Hilton calls reform in faith and
feeling, or, to use the modern expression,
experimental union. This is, for him, the
highest state which a soul can reach here
below (Bk. II, chap. xvii), for it is only in
heaven that faith will vanish into sight and
give place to the fulness of experimental
knowledge (Bk. II, chaps. iv, xxxii). In
what does this higher reform consist?

xxxiii B

INTRODUCTION

Hilton characterises it, for the moment as it
were, by its outward effects (Bk. II, chaps. v,
xiii); but when, later on, he seeks actually
to define it (Bk. II, chap. xxxi), he will find
no better expression than the prayer for
interior regeneration which Saint Paul
addressed to the Christians of Colosse when
tracing out that plan of the spiritual life by
which Hilton was himself inspired... .
| “ Being filled with the knowledge of the will
of God in all understanding and spiritual
wisdom.” Like the Apostle he places before
us as our object the full development of the
highest spiritual gifts, and the perfect satisfy- .
ing knowledge of God and the mystery of
Christ, as it is engendered in the soul by the
gift of wisdom.

But before proceeding to describe the
state of perfect souls, it is necessary to
indicate the means whereby this state can
be reached, and this is the object of the
second part of the.book (Bk. II, chaps. xx-
Xxx). It is a question, as we have seen, of
replacing self-love with the love of God.
Self-love expresses itself mainly in two ways,
pride and selfishness; everything depends,
therefore, upon substituting for these two
evil tendencies the two opposite virtues,
» humility and charity. To these our atten-
tion has already been directed throughout
Hilton’s work, and their successive degrees

XXXIV

INTRODUCTION

form, as it were, the rungs of the ladder
which the soul has to climb.

Humility consisted, first of all, in the -
recognition of our faults and weaknesses
(Bk. I, chap. xvi; Bk. II, chap. xxxvii), and
sometimes in the early stages it was needful
that the will should intervene in order to
compel the understanding to acknowledge
our wretchedness (Bk. I, chap. xvii). But
little by little, thanks to reflection, to our
experiences (Bk. I, chap. xix), and especially
to that “ seeking after God ” which Hilton
has taught the soul to practise (Bk. I, chaps.
XXll-xxiii), we arrive at a truer estimate

of ourselves. It is this constant tending z

towards God which shows us, more clearly
than anything else, what keeps us far from
Him, and thus causes us to abjure all that
hinders us (Bk. I, chap. xxiv, etc.). But
this tendency mingles with desire, and desire
is already charity (Bk. I, chap. xxii), and
charity will exercise its transforming influence
upon humility. It is not with impunity
that we turn assiduously towards Infinite
Beauty and Sovereign Truth; the more we
do so the more we shall be convinced of our
own impotence and nothingness (Bk. II,
chap. xx). There will come a time when this
conviction will grow into something joyful
and ingrained in the character. Humility
will have become the universal virtue, placing
XXXV

INTRODUCTION

us before God in a fitting attitude (Bk. II,
chaps. xx, xxi, xxvii); “‘ when we shall be
annihilated before Him, then we shall be
humble: humility is truth.” This humility,
looking to God rather than to self, will, in
its turn, react upon our charity. The
attitude of self-annihilation, or as one might
say of adoration, which it causes us to assume,
will avail nothing unless the soul places
herself entirely in God’s hands and yields
herself to the divine action (Bk. II, chap.
xxxvi). Then is love truly master and reigns
in the soul: “ The more a soul annihilates
itself through grace by sight of this truth . . .
the nearer it comes to receiving the gift of
blessed love ” (Bk. II, chap. xxxv).

These two virtues, which are the very
livery of Christ, mutually complete and
increase each other. Hilton epitomises their
growth, and the movement towards God
with which they stamp all our faculties, in
the oft-repeated parable of the pilgrim
journeying to Jerusalem, the city of contem-
plation, unarmed save for charity and humi-
lity, but, thanks to them, victorious over
every obstacle (Bk. II, chaps. xxi-xxiii). But
this should be read in the text itself.

It must be recognised, however, that the
generous practice of these virtues involves
a self-abnegation exactly proportioned to the
loftiness of our aim. The ascent to God

XXXVI

p4

Ws

INTRODUCTION

presupposes the abandonment of all else
but Him. The pilgrim who goes up to
Jerusalem cannot attain the luminous peaks
of contemplation except by passing through
the dark night of self-immolation (Bk. II,
chaps. xxiv-xxvii). Going up to Jerusalem;
the Dark Night—these are very same expres-
sions consecrated by Saint John of the Cross
two centuries later. The likeness is not
confined to words; the teaching of the two
writers is identical, although their tempera-
ment and even their method is so utterly
dissimilar. Even after having accomplished
the work of purification described in the first
book of the Scale, much yet remains to be
done: having drained the source of evil, it
becomes necessary to eliminate everything
that is not God. We must learn to be
indifferent to all that proceeds from creatures ,
(Bk. II, chap. xxii), to die to all, to be ready -
to live by blind faith, to cast aside all desire ;
and all fear (Bk. II, chap. xxvii), to endure
spiritual tribulation even to abandonment
and the temptation to despair, save only
for “a little privy trust in God’s mercy ”
(Bk. II, chap. xxviii).

But although Hilton regards these renun-
ciations as the indispensable conditions of
success, he endeavours to concentrate atten-
tion on the goal and does not wish us to
dwell too much upon the inevitable suffer-

xxxvii

ca

ba

INTRODUCTION

ings of the way. It is towards Jerusalem
that we must constantly lift our eyes, even
in the night, and indeed especially then;
there is no better means of advancing than
this. Moreover, he explains clearly that it is
the special characteristic of these trials that
rest and comfort must not be sought outside,
and as it were beyond our renunciations.
We ought to look upon them as final and
know how to find interior joy in them;
we must be at our ease in the very midst of
them (Bk. II, chap. xxv). Itis in the dark
night itself that we must meet the light
(Bk. II, chaps. xxiv, xxvi, xxvii). It is a
question of winning our freedom, and this
not by passing through a series of fleeting
trials and temporary annoyances, after which
the ego can reassert itself and expand once
more. Our self-emptying must be complete,
and the old man must die. Then, and then
only, can we come into the haven of contem-
plation.

It is necessary to put our whole will into
this work, and yet it is especially at this
stage in the spiritual life that our own help-
lessness and the necessity for God’s action
comes out most strongly (Bk. II, chap. xx,
xxviii). “‘ We are an instrument, and it is
God who works in us ” (Bk. II, chap. xxiv).
His grace alone can extinguish every alien
affection in us “ and the soul does not even

xxxviii

INTRODUCTION

know how this destruction takes place”
(Bk. II, chaps. xxvii, xxviii). But the
needful grace will not be lacking to a generous
soul, and step by step the work will be
perfected. It only remains to describe the
results of this transformation, and this is the
object of the final section of the book (Bk. II,
chaps. xxxi-xlvi).

Basing himself once more upon the
authority of Saint Augustine, Hilton shows
how it is that love gradually. becomes the
soul’s chief motive power (Bk. II, chap. xxxv),
acting without any intermediary and bringing
about a purification far more perfect and
fundamental than that which was achieved
by the exercise of charity practised with joy
and sweetness (Bk. I, chap. xiv). Our Lord,
finding that the soul has become perfectly
pliable, moves it by means of the Gifts of
the Holy Ghost (Bk. II, chap. xxxv), and the
development of these gifts, or at least the

_ higher of them, constitutes what is almost

a new series of steps for the soul to climb, at
the same time cleansing her from the last
remnants of evil. Then the inward eye of the
soul opens, and “‘in that luminous darkness,
of which the learned know nothing,” she
receives together with wisdom and under-
standing the gift of contemplation (Bk. II,
chap. xl). Thus is accomplished that entire
XXX1X

INTRODUCTION

conversion which Hilton made the object
of his whole treatise at its very inception
(Bk. I, chap. i).

The logical connection linking up every
part of the work now appears very clearly.
At times, indeed, it might seem that con-
templation is set aside, while the subject
of self-reformation occupies the whole field,
but, in reality, Hilton has never ceased to set
the soul forward towards her goal. That
seeking and longing after God which he
extols in so many pages, and the humility
which of itself turns us to Him, are only
preliminaries, or more truly forms of con-
templation: and contemplation, like all other
virtues, is the fruit of love (Bk. II, chap.
xxxiv). The single eye, to give it its Gospel
name (Luke xi 34), has made the whole body
full of light, and the more the body is purified
the better will the eye be able to pierce the
clouds.

The final chapters have no need of any
commentary. Yet this makes it all the more
profitable to note the precision, and above
all the sobriety with which the different
degrees of union with God are described
(Bk. II, chaps. xl-xlvi). As Dom Ephrem
Guy remarks in the preface to his edition,
Hilton chose to dwell longest at the halting-
places of the spiritual life, or its transitional
phases as they may be called, because in

xl

INTRODUCTION

them the soul has greater need of counsel
and support. Here he gives us only what is
needful for instruction and encouragement,
without adding a single word which could
provoke idle curiosity or foster a delusion.
Even at the end, when he deals with the
very highest states, he characterises them in
a single stroke, then modestly retires and,
instead of revelling in description, simply
refers us to the “ Teaching of the doctors ”
(Bk. II, chap. xlvi). This measured style,
this discretion, in the full sense which the
Fathers attached to the word, together with
the simple, practical character of all he
wrote and the masterly sureness of touch
with which he describes contemplation and
its counterfeits, implies in our author a
perfect command of his subject. Hilton
pretends in several passages that he is not
speaking from personal experience (Bk. I,
chaps. xiii, xciii; Bk. II, chap. xxi), but his
book gives the lie to this humble assertion.
Moreover, his contemporaries were not taken
in by it. They have proclaimed his sanctity.

The success of what has been justly called
the ‘Manual for Contemplatives”’ was
great, and copies both of the original English
and a contemporary Latin version were
rapidly multiplied. The Scala seems to
have been highly prized by the Carthusians,

xli B2

INTRODUCTION

but the solitaries were not alone in appre-
ciating Hilton. Inthe middle of the fifteenth
century Edward IV’s mother caused his
works to be read aloud at meals together
with those of Saint Catherine of Siena,
Saint Bridget, and Saint Bonaventure. A
little later, in 1494, the Lady Margaret,
Henry VIPs mother, made Wynkyn de
Worde print “‘this heavenly book more
precious than gold,” and editions followed
one another so rapidly that the eighth
had already appeared by 1533. Even the
Reformation did not put an end to these
re-issues. In the seventeenth century Dom
Augustine Baker, the famous Benedictine
mystic, borrowed many points of his teach-
ing from Hilton’s treatise, and is said to have
prepared the edition—published after his
death by his disciple, Dom Serenus Cressy,
in 1659—on which are based the modern
editions of Dom Ephrem Guy (1869) and
Father Dalgairns (1870).

The wide circulation of Hilton’s book in
such unpromising circumstances is not so
surprising as would at first appear; neither
is it in any way incompatible with his teaching
on the exigencies of the contemplative life.
Besides those living in conditions of peace
and recollection, favourable to the exercise
and development of contemplative gifts,
there are always certain souls, perhaps more

xlii

INTRODUCTION

numerous than is generally believed, to whom
God has given the desire for this life, even
though they are unable to leave the world.
Such cases occurred in the fourteenth
century as they do to-day, and Hilton knew
them well. It was for such a soul that he
wrote his “‘ Letter to a Devout Man.” It is
addressed to one the duties of whose state
kept him in the world, although he was
possessed of a great longing for the contem-
plative life, and, at the same time as recom-
mending him to fulfil the duties belonging
to his condition, Hilton encourages him to
persevere in his desire, and provides him
with an epitome of advice corresponding to
that developed in the Scala, exhorting him
above all to tend towards God without allow-
ing himself to be turned aside from his
goal. The same advice may still be given
to-day to all who are in the same case. ‘They
should not grow weary, but endeavour to
establish themselves, as well as they can, in
the conditions requisite for contemplation,
while practising the virtues favourable to
it, especially confidence and self-abnegation.
Let them never cease to turn to God and
long after Him. He who has given them
these aptitudes and desires will only keep
them waiting for their own good. He will
justly show Himself severe towards those
who wilfully neglect Him while dwelling in
xliii

INTRODUCTION

the cloister (Bk. II, chap. xviii), but He
will never repulse the souls who desire Him
alone, whatever may be the circumstances in
which He has placed them. Nay, He will
Himself supply whatever is lacking to them,
They must abide patiently till the second
or third watch; God will not fail them. It
may be that it will all happen without their
knowledge, but the day will come when they
will be recompensed for their long waiting.
In the meantime let them seek help in the
Scala Perfectionis.

To them just as truly as to religious en-
closed in a monastery, to all those indeed who
simply seek God and God alone, is addressed
the wise and lofty teaching of him whom
contemporaries called Venerable Walter
Hilton. Upon such sound and consoling
doctrine the soul will be well nourished.

CHAP-FER I

THAT THE INNER STATE OF MAN’S SOUL SHOULD
BE LIKE THE OUTER

HOSTLY sister in Jesus Christ I
e pray thee that, in the calling in which

our Lord hath called thee to His
service, thou hold thee paid* and stand
steadfastly therein; travailing busily with
all the mights of thy soul by the grace of
Jesus Christ to fulfil in soothfastnesst of
good living the state which thou hast taken
in likeness and in seeming. And as thou
hast forsaken the world, as it were a dead
man. turned to our Lord bodily in sight of
men, right so let thine heart be as it were
dead to all earthly loves and dreads, turned
wholly to our Lord Jesus Christ. For wit
thou well, a bodily turning to God without
the heart following, is but a figure and a
likeness of virtues and no soothfastness.
Wherefore a wretched man or woman is he
or she that leaveth the inward keeping of

x Paid—z.e., content.

+ Soothfastness—z.e., truthfulness.

I

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

himself, and shapeth himself without only, in
a form and likeness of holiness, in habit or
clothing, in speech and in bodily works;
beholding other men’s deeds and deeming
their faults, weening himself to be aught
when he is right naught, and so beguiling
himself. Do thou not so; but turn thy heart
with thy body principally to God, and shape
thee within to His likeness by meekness and
charity and other ghostly virtues; for then
art thou truly turned to Him. I say not
that, on the first day, thou mayest so lightly
be turned in thy soul by fulness of virtues,
as thou mayest with thy body be enclosed
in a house; but that thou shouldst know that
the cause for thy bodily enclosing is that
thou mayest the better come to a ghostly
enclosing; and as thy body is enclosed from
bodily conversation of men, right so thine
heart may be enclosed from fleshly loves
and dreads of all earthly things. And how
thou mayest the better come thereto, I shall
tell thee in this writing as methinketh.

CHAPTER II
OF ACTIVE LIFE AND THE WORKS THEREOF

Tunov shalt understand that there are in Holy
Church two manner of lives, as Saint Gregory
saith, in the which Christian men shall be .

Ch. 2] OF PERFECTION

saved. The one is called active and the
other contemplative; without one of these
two no man may be saved. Active life lieth
in love and charity showed outwardly by
good bodily works; in fulfilling of God’s
commandments and of the seven works of
mercy, bodily and ghostly, to a man’s even-
Christian.* This life belongeth to all
worldly men which have riches and plenty
of goods to spend; and also to all others
which have state, office, or care of other
men, whether learned or lewd,f temporal
or spiritual; and generally all worldly men
are bound to fulfil it after their might and
cunning as reason and discretion asketh.
If he have much good, much good must he
do; if he little have, he may do less; and if
he have naught, then must he have a good
will. These are works of active life, either
bodily or ghostly. Also a great part of
active life lieth in great bodily deeds which
a man doth to himself, as great fasting, much
waking, and other sharp penance-doing, to
chastise the flesh with discretion for trespass
beforedone, and by such penance to refrain
the lusts and likings of it, and make him
buxom# and ready to the will of the spirit.
These works, though they be active, help

* Even-Christian—7.e., fellow-Christian.
+ Lewd—+.e., ignorant.
+ Buxom—+.e., docile, obedient.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

right much and ordain a man in the begin-
ning to come to contemplative life, if they be
used with discretion.

CHAPTER III

OF CONTEMPLATIVE LIFE AND THE WORKS
THEREOF

CONTEMPLATIVE life lieth in perfect love and
charity, felt inwardly by ghostly virtues and
by soothfast knowing of God and ghostly
things. This life belongeth specially to
them which for the love of God forsake all
worldly riches, worships,* and outward
business, and wholly give themselves body
and soul, after their might and cunning, to
the service of God by ghostly occupation.
Now then, since it is so that thy state asketh
thee to be contemplative—for that is the
intent of thy enclosing, that thou mayest
more freely and entirely give thyself to
ghostly occupation—therefore, it behoveth
thee to be right busy both night and day,
with travail of body and of spirit, to come to
that life as nigh as thou mayest, by such
means as thou hopest were best for thee.
Nevertheless, before I tell thee of the
means, I shall tell thee first a little more of
this life contemplative, that thou mayest
* Worships—7.e., honours.

Ch. 4] OF PERFECTION

somewhat see what it is, and so set it as a
mark in the sight of thy soul whereto thou
shalt draw in all thine occupation.

CHAPTER IV
OF THE FIRST PART OF CONTEMPLATION

CONTEMPLATIVE life hath three parts. The
first lieth in knowing of God and ghostly
things gotten by reason, by teaching of man,
and by study in holy writ, without ghostly
- affection and inward savour felt by the
special gift of the Holy Ghost. This part
of contemplation specially lettered men have
and great clerks, which by long study and
great travail in holy writ come to this
knowing, more or less, after the subtlety of
kindly* wit and continuance of study with
the general wit that God giveth to every man
that hath use of reason. This knowing is
good, and it may be called a part of contem-
plation, inasmuch as it is a sight of sooth-
fastness and a knowing of ghostly things.
Nevertheless, it is but a shadow of very
contemplation, for it hath not ghostly savour
in God nor inward sweetness, the which no
man may feel unless he be in great charity,
for it is the proper well of our Lord to
which cometh none alien. But this manner
x Kindly—z.e., natural.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

of knowing is common to good and bad, for
it may be had without charity. And, there-
fore, it is not very contemplation; as oft-
times hypocrites and fleshly living men have
more such knowing than many other Chris-
tian men, and yet have these men no charity.
Of this manner of knowing Saint Paul
speaketh thus: If I had full knowledge of all
things and I knew all privities and I had not
charity, I am right naught.* Nevertheless,
if they that have this knowing keep them-
selves in meekness and in charity, and flee
worldly and fleshly sins after their might, it
is to them a good way and a great disposing
to very contemplation, if they desire and
pray devoutly after the grace of the Holy
Ghost. Other men that have this cunning
and turn it to pride and glory of themselves,
or into covetousness, and desiring of worldly
states, worships and riches, not meekly
taking it to the praising of God, nor chari-
tably spending it in the profit of their even-
Christians, some of them. fall into heresies
and errors, or into other open sins by which
they scandalise themselves and all Holy
Church. Of this cunning Saint Paul saith,
Knowing alone lifteth up the heart unto pride ;
but mix it with charity and then turneth it
into edification.t| ‘This knowing alone is
but water, unsavoury and cold; and, there-
* Comin. T Ibid.

Ch. 5] OF PERFECTION

fore, if they that have it would but meekly
offer it up to our Lord and pray Him of His
grace, He would with His blessing turn the
water into wine, as He did at the prayer of
His mother at the feast of Architriclinus;
that is to say He would turn the unsavoury
knowing into wisdom, and the cold naked
reason into ghostly light and burning love,
by the gift of the Holy Ghost.

CHAPTER V
OF THE SECOND PART OF CONTEMPLATION

THE second part of contemplation lieth
principally in affection without light of
understanding of ghostly things; and this is
commonly of simple and unlettered men
which give themselves wholly to devotion.
And this is felt on this manner. When
man or woman in meditation of God, by the
grace of the Holy Ghost, feeleth fervour of
love and ghostly sweetness by the mind of
Christ’s passion or any of His works in His
manhood; or feeleth a great trust in the
goodness and in the mercy of God for the
forgiveness of his sins and for His great gift
of grace, or else a dread in his affection with
great reverence of the privy dooms of God
whichjhe'seethfnot, and of His rightwiseness ;*
or when in prayer he feeleth the thought of
* Rightwiseness—1.e., justice.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

his heart draw up from all earthly things,
strained together with all the mights* of it,
ascending to our Lord by fervent desire and
with ghostly delight; and nevertheless in
that time he hath no open sight in under-
standing of ghostly things nor of privities of
holy writ in special, but he thinketh only
for the time that nothing liketh him so much
as to pray and think as he doth, for savour,
delight and comfort that he findeth therein;
and yet he cannot tell what it is, but he feeleth
it well for it is the gift of God. For out of
it spring many sweet tears, burning desires
and still mournings, which scour and cleanse
the heart from all the filth of sin, and make it
melt into wonderful sweetness of Jesus Christ,
buxom, simple and ready to fulfil all God’s
will; insomuch that he thinketh that he
maketh no charget what becometh of himself
so that God’s will were fulfilled, with many
such stirrings more than I can or may say.
This feeling may not be had without great
grace; and whoso hath it, for the time he is
in charity; which charity may not be lost or
lessened though the fervour of it pass away—
save only by a deadly sin—and that is com-
fortable. This may be called the second part
of contemplation; nevertheless, this part
hath two degrees.
* Mights—i.e., powers.
t Charge—i.e., care, trouble,

Ch, 6] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER VI

OF THE LOWER DEGREE OF THE SECOND PART
OF CONTEMPLATION

THE lower degree of this feeling, men which
are active may have by grace when they be
visited by our Lord, as mightily and as
fervently as they that give themselves wholly
to contemplative life and have this gift.
But this feeling in its fervour cometh not
alway when a man would, nor lasteth it full
long. It cometh and goeth as He will that
giveth it, and, therefore, whoso hath it let
him meek himself, and thank God and keep
it privy—except it be to his confessor—and
hold it as long as he may with discretion.
And when it is withdrawn let him not dread
too much, but stand sadly* in faith and in a
meek hope, with patient abiding till it come
again. ‘This isa little tasting of the sweetness
of the love of God, of which David saith in
the psalter thus: Taste ye and see the sweetness
of our Lord.t

* Sadly—i.e., soberly, seriously.
T+ Ps. xxxiii 9.

THE SCALE [Bk. I

CHAPTER VII

OF THE HIGHER DEGREE OF THE SECOND PART
OF CONTEMPLATION

THE higher degree of this part of contempla-
tion may not be had and holden, save by
them which be in great rest of body and
soul, who, by the grace of Jesus and long
travail bodily and ghostly, feel a rest of
heart and cleanness of conscience, so that
they like nothing so much as to sit still in
rest of body and always to pray to God and
think on our Lord, and to think sometimes
on the blessed name of Jesus which is com-
fortable and delectable to them, so that by
the mind of it they feel themselves fed in
their affection. And not only the Name
but all other manner prayers, as the Pater
noster and the Ave, and hymns or psalms and
other devout sayings of Holy Church, are
turned as it were to a ghostly mirth and
sweet song, by which they are comforted and
strengthened against all sins, and much
relieved of bodily dis-ease.* Of this degree
speaketh Saint Paul thus: Be not drunken with
wine, but be ye fulfilled of the Holy Ghost,
singing to yourself in hymns and psalms and
ghostly songs, singing and psalming in your
hearts to our Lord.t Whoso hath this grace,
*x Dis-ease—i.e., distress. t Eph. v 18, 19.
Io

Ch. 8] OF PERFECTION

let him keep himself in lowness, and be ever
desiring to come to more knowing and
feeling of God, in the third part of contem-
plation.

CHAPTER VIII
OF THE THIRD PART OF CONTEMPLATION

THE third part of contemplation, which is
as perfect as it may be here, lieth both in
cognition and in affection; that is to say in
knowing and in perfect loving of God. And
that is when a man’s soul is first reformed
by fulness of virtues to the image of Jesus;
and after—when he is visited and taken in
from all earthly and fleshly affections, from
vain thoughts and imaginings of all bodily
creatures, and as it were much ravished out
of the bodily wits—then by the grace of the
Holy Ghost is he illumined to see by under-
standing Soothfastness—which is God—
and ghostly things, with a soft, sweet burning
love in him, so fitly that he is ravished of
his love: so that the soul is oned for the
time and conformed to the image of the
Trinity. The beginning of this contempla-
tion may be felt in this life, but the fulness
of it is kept unto the bliss of heaven. Of
this oneing and conforming to our Lord
speaketh Saint Paul thus: Whoso by ravish-
ing of love is fastened to God, then God and
II

his soul are not two but both one.* And

CHAPTER IX

OF THE DIFFERING OF THE THIRD PART OF
CONTEMPLATION FROM THE SECOND PART,
AND OF THE PRAISING THEREOF.

THE other part may be called burning love
in devotion, and this third part burning love
in contemplation ; that is lower, this is higher.
That is sweeter to the bodily feeling, this
better to the ghostly feeling. For it is more
inward, more worthy, more ghostly, more
wonderful. For this is verily a tasting—little
though it is—and an earnest of the sight of
heavenly joy, not clearly but half in darkness,
which shall be fulfilled and openly cleared
in the bliss of heaven, as Saint Paul saith:
We see now God by a mirror as it were in dark-
ness, but in heaven shall we see openly face to
faces} This is the illumination of under-
standing in delights of loving, as David saith
in the psalter: My might is my light in my
delight.t The other part is milk for
children, this is whole meat for perfect men
which have wits assayed to know good from

CHAPTER X

HOW THE SHOWING TO THE BODILY WITS AND
THE FEELING OF THEM MAY BE BOTH
GOOD AND EVIL

By this that I have said you may somewhat
understand that visions or revelations of
any manner of spirit, in bodily appearing or
in imagining, sleeping or waking, or else
any other feeling in the bodily wits* made as
it were ghostly; either in sounding of ear,
or savouring in the mouth, or smelling at the
nose, or else any sensible heat, as it were
fire glowing and warming the breast or any
other part of the body, or any other thing
that may be felt by bodily wit, though it
be never so comfortable and liking, these
are not very contemplation; nor are they
aught but simple and secondary, though
they be good in regard of ghostly virtues,
and of this ghostly knowing and loving of
God. For all such manner of feeling may
be good, wrought by a good angel, or they
* Wits—.e, the five senses.

Ch. 10] OF PERFECTION

may be deceivable, wrought by a wicked
angel when he transfigureth himself into an
angel of light. Wherefore, since they may
be both good and evil, it seemeth well that
they are not the best; for wit you well that
the devil may, when he hath leave, feign in
bodily feeling the likeness of the same things
which a good angel may work. For right
as a good angel cometh with light, so can the
devil. And so is it also of the other wits.
Whoso hath felt both, he would well tell
which were good and which were evil; but
he that never felt neither, or else but the
one, may lightly be deceived. They are
alike in the manner of outward feeling, but
they are full divers within; and, therefore,
they are not to be desired greatly, nor to be
received lightly, unless a soul might by the
spirit of discretion know the good from the
evil that he were not beguiled. Saint John
biddeth us that we should not trust to every
spirit, but we shall assay first whether he be
of God or no.* Wherefore by one assay, I
shall tell thee, as methinketh, how thou shalt
know the good from evil.

CHAPTER XI

HOW YOU SHALL KNOW WHETHER THE SHOWING
TO THE BODILY WITS AND THE FEELING
OF THEM BE GOOD OR EVIL

IF it be so that thou see any manner of light
or brightness with thy bodily eye, or in
imagining, other than every man may see;
or if thou hear any merry wonderful sounding
with thy bodily ear, or in thy mouth any
sweet sudden savour other than of kind, or
any heat in thy breast as it were fire, or any
manner of delight in any part of thy body,
or if a spirit bodily appeareth unto thee, as
it were an angel, to comfort thee and teach
thee, or any such feeling which thou knowest
well that it cometh not of thyself nor of any
bodily creature, beware in that time or soon
after, and wisely behold the stirring of thy
heart. If thou be stirred because of the
liking that thou feelest, to withdraw thine
heart from the mind and the beholding of
Jesus Christ and from ghostly occupation—
as from prayer and thinking of thyself and
thy defaults—from the inward desire of
virtues and of ghostly knowing and feeling
of God, to set the sight of thy heart and thy
affection, thy delight and thy rest principally
therein, weening that it should be a part
of heavenly joy and of angels’ bliss, and for

Ch. 11] OF PERFECTION

that thou thinkest that thou shouldest
neither pray nor think naught else, but all
wholly tend thereto, to keep it and delight
thee therein, this feeling is suspect and of
the enemy. And, therefore, though it be
never so liking and so wonderful, refuse it
and assent not thereto, for this is the sleight
of the enemy. When he seeth a soul that
would entirely give itself to ghostly occupa-
tion, he is wonderfully wroth; for he hateth
nothing more than to see a soul in body of
sin feel verily the savour of ghostly knowing
and loving of God, which he himself without
body of sin lost wilfully. And, therefore,
if he may not let* him by open sins, he would
hinder him by such vanity of bodily savours
or sweetness in the wits, to bring a soul into
ghostly pride and into a false secureness of
himself, weening that he had thereby a feeling
of heavenly joy, and that he were half in
paradise for delight that he feeleth about
him, when he is near at hell’s gates; and so
by pride and by presumption he may fall
into other bodily or ghostly mischiefs.

Nevertheless if it be so that this manner
of feeling let not thy heart from ghostly
occupation, but maketh thee more devout,
more fervent to pray and more wise to think
ghostly thoughts, and though it be so that it
astonish thee in the beginning, nevertheless

CHAPTER XII

WHAT KNITTETH JESUS TO MAN’S SOUL AND
WHAT LOOSETH HIM THEREFROM

THE knitting and the fastening of Jesus to a
man’s soul is by good will and great desire
to Him only, to have Him and see Him in
His bliss ghostly. The more that this desire
is, the faster is Jesus knit to the soul: the
less that this desire is, the looslier is He knit.
What spirit or what feeling soever it be,
which lesseneth this desire and would draw
it down from the steadfast mind of Jesus
Christ, and from the kindly stying to Jesus,
this spirit—for that it will unknit Jesus from
the soul—it is not of God, but is a working
of the enemy. Nevertheless if a spirit or a
feeling or a revelation make this desire more,
knit the knots of love and devotion to Jesus
faster, and openeth the eye of the soul into
ghostly knowing more clearly, and maketh
it more meek in itself; this spirit is of God.
Here mayest thou see somewhat that thou
shalt not suffer thy heart wilfully to rest, nor
to delight wholly, in any bodily feeling of
such manner of comforts or sweetnesses,
though they were good; but thou shalt hold
them in thy sight naught or little in regard*
* Regard—1z.e., comparison.

THE! SCALE [Bk. 1

of ghostly desire and of steadfast thinking
on Jesus, nor fasten the thought of thy heart
overmuch on them.

CHAPTER XIII

HOW AND IN WHAT THINGS SHOULD A CON-
TEMPLATIVE MAN BE OCCUPIED

BuT thou shalt ever seek by great business
in prayers that thou mayest come to the
ghostly feeling of God; and that thou mayest
know wisdom of God, the endless might of
Him, the great goodness of Him in Himself
and in His creatures; for this is contempla-
tion, and that other is none. Thus saith Saint
Paul: Be ye rooted and grounded in charity,
that ye may know, he saith, neither sound of
the ear nor sweet savour in the mouth, nor
any such bodily thing, but that ye may know
and feel with all hallows, which is the length
of the endless being of God, the breadth of the
wonderful charity and the goodness of God,
the height of the almighty majesty of Him and
the groundless deepness of the wisdom of God.*
In knowing and in ghostly feeling of these
should be the occupation of a contemplative
man, for in these may be understood the
full knowing of all ghostly things. This
occupation is that one thing which Saint
* Eph. iii 17-19.

Ch. 14] OF PERFECTION

Paul coveted, saying thus: One thing is best
for me to covet, and that is that I might forget
all things which are behindward or backward,
and I shall stretch out mine heart ever forward,*
to feel and to grip the sovereign meed of
the endless bliss. Hindward are all bodily
things, forward are ghostly things; and
therefore Saint Paul would forget all bodily
things, and his own body also, that he may see
ghostly things.

CHAPTER XIV

HOW IN REASON AND WILL VIRTUE BEGINNETH,
AND IN LOVE AND IN LIKING IT IS MADE
PERFECT

Now have I told thee a little of contempla-
tion, what it should be; for this intent, that
thou mayest know it and set it as a mark
before the sight of thy soul, and desire all
thy lifetime to come to any part of it by the
grace of our Lord Jesus Christ. This is the
conforming of a soul to God, which may not
be had unless it be first reformed by fulness
of virtues turned into affection; and that is
when a man loveth virtues, for that they
be good in themselves.

There is many a man that hath virtue of
lowness, patience, and charity to his even-
Christian and such other, only in his reason

* Phil. iii 13, 14.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

and will, and hath no ghostly delight nor
love in them. For ofttimes he feeleth
grouching, heaviness and bitterness to do
them, yet nevertheless he doth them, by
stirring of reason only for dread of God.
This man hath virtues in reason and in will,
but not love of them in his affection. But
when—by grace of Jesus and by ghostly
and bodily exercises—reason is turned into
light, and will into love, then hath he virtues
in affection: for he hath so well gnawed on
the bitter bark of the nut that he hath broken
it, and feedeth himself with the kernel.
That is to say, the virtues which at first were
heavy to do are now turned into a very delight
and savour, as when a man liketh in meek-
ness, in patience, in cleanness, in sobriety
and in charity, as in any delights. Soothly,
until these virtues be turned thus into
affection, he may well have the second part
of contemplation, but to the third soothfastly
shall he not come.

OF THE MEANS THAT BRING A SOUL TO
CONTEMPLATION

Now since virtues are disposing to contem-

plation, therefore it behoveth thee to use

certain means to come to virtues. Three

means there are, which men most commonly

devotion. By meditation shalt thou see,

CHAPTER XVI

WHAT A MAN SHOULD USE AND REFUSE BY
THE VIRTUE OF MEEKNESS

Now if wisely thou shouldst use ghostly
works and securely travail in them, it
behoveth thee to begin right low. Three
things thou needest to have first, upon
which, as on a secure ground, thou shalt set
all thy work. These three are meekness,
secure faith, and whole intent to God. First
it behoveth thee to have meekness in this
manner. Thou shalt hold thyself in thy
will and in thy feeling, if thou may, unable
to dwell among men, and unworthy to serve
God in conversation with his servants,
unprofitable to thy even-Christian, wanting
both cunning and might to fulfil good works
of active life in help of thy even-Christian,
as other men and women do; and, therefore,
as a wretch and outcast and refuse of all men

Thus it behoveth thee to feel for some time,
