CHAPTER XVII

WHO SHOULD BLAME MEN’S DEFAULTS, AND
DEEM THEM, AND WHO SHOULD NOT

BUT now sayest thou, how may this be?
since it is a deed of charity to blame men for
their defaults, and to deem them for their
amending is a deed of mercy. As to this
I answer as methinketh, that to thee, or to
any other which hath state and purpose of
life contemplative, it falleth not to leave the
keeping of themselves and to behold and to
blame other men for their faults, unless there
were full great need, so that a man would
perish unless thou blamed him. But the
men which are active and have sovereignty
and cure over others, as prelates and curates
and such other, they are bound by their
office and by way of charity to see and seek
and deem rightfully other men’s defaults:
not of desire and delight to chastise them,
but only for need, with dread of God and in
His name, for love of salvation of their souls.
Other men that are active and have no cure
of others, they are bound to blame other men
for their faults by way of charity, only when
the sin is deadly, and may not well be
corrected by any other man, and when he
troweth that he would the sooner be amended
by such correcting: else it is better that he

Ch. 18] OF PERFECTION

cease. That this is sooth it seemeth by
Saint John, which held the state of contempla-
tive life, and Saint Peter, which had the state
of active life. When our Lord in His last
supper with His disciples, at the privy stirring
of Saint Peter to Saint John, told Saint
John how Judas should betray Him, Saint
John told it not to Saint Peter as he asked,
but he turned him and laid his head upon
Christ’s breast, and was ravished by love
into contemplation of God’s privities, and
so meedfully* to him that he forgot both
Judas and Saint Peter, in tokening and
teaching to other men that they should dispose
them to do the same.

CHAPTER XVIII

WHY MEN SHOULD WORSHIP OTHERS AND
LOWER THEMSELVES IN THEIR OWN HEART
UNDER ALL OTHERS

THou seest here then somewhat how thou
shalt neither deem other men nor conceive
any evil suspicion against them, but thou
shalt love and worship in thy heart such as
lead active life in the world and suffer many
tribulations and temptations, which thou,
sitting in thy house, feelest naught of. And
they have full much travail and dis-ease
x Meedfully—z.e., profitably.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

for their own and other men’s sustenance,
and many of them would liefer serve God,
if they might, as thou dost in bodily rest: and
nevertheless they, in their worldly business,
flee many sins which thou, if thou wert in
their state, shouldst fall into, and they do
many good deeds which thou couldst not do.
There is no doubt that many do thus, though
which they be thou wottest not, and there-
fore thou shalt worship all and set them in
thy heart all above thee as thy sovereigns, and
cast thee down under their feet, that thou
be vilest and lowest in thine own sight. For
it is no dread nor any peril to thee how much
thou mayest low thyself beneath all others,
though it were so that in God’s sight thou
hadst more grace than any other. But it
is peril to thee to high thee and lift thyself
in thy thought wilfully above any other man,
though he were the most wretched or the
most sinful caitiff that is in earth. For our
Lord saith, Whoso higheth himself, he shall be
lowed, and whoso loweth himself, he shall be
highed.* This part of meekness thee beho-
veth to have in thy beginning, and by this
and by grace shalt thou come to the fulhead
of it and of all other virtues. For whoso hath
one virtue, he hath all other virtues. As
much as thou hast of meekness, so much hast
thou of charity, of patience, and of other
AMT ake Siva

Ch. 19] OF PERFECTION

virtues, though they be not showed out-
wardly. Be then busy to get meekness, and
hold it; for it is the first and the last of all
virtues. It is the first, for it is the ground
of all, as Saint Austin saith: ‘‘ If thou think
to build an high house of virtues, ordain thee
first a deep ground: of meekness.”’ Also it
is the last, for it is the keeping and saving
of all other virtues, as Saint Gregory saith:
“ He that gathereth virtues without meekness,
he is like to him which maketh and beareth
powder of spicery in the wind.” Do thou
never so many good deeds—fast thou, wake
thou, or any other good works do thou—if
thou have no meekness it is naught that thou
dost.

CHAPTER XIX

HOW MEN SHOULD DO THAT WANT THE FEELING
OF MEEKNESS IN AFFECTION, NOT DREAD-
ING TOO MUCH THEREFORE

NEVERTHELESS if thou mayest not feel this
meekness in thine heart with affection as
thou wouldest, do as thou mayest, meek
thyself in will by reason, trowing that it
should be so as I say though thou feel it not.
And in that hold thee a more wretch, in that
thou mayest not feel soothfastly thyself as
thou art. And if thou do so, though thy
flesh rise against thee and will not assent to

THE SCALE [Bk. I

thy good will, be not too much adread, but
bear and suffer the false feeling of thy flesh
as a pain, and then thou shalt dispose and
reprove that feeling and break down that
rising of thine heart, as if thou wouldest be
well paid to be trodden and spurned under
other men’s feet. And so, by the grace of
Jesus Christ, through steadfast thinking on
the meekness of His precious manhood, shalt
thou much abate the stirrings of pride;
and the virtue of meekness that was first in
thy naked will shall be turned into feeling
of affection; without which virtue, either in
true will or in feeling, whoso disposeth him-
self to serve God in contemplative life, as the
blind he shall stumble and never come
thereto. The higher he climbeth by bodily
penance and other virtues, and hath not
this meekness, the lower he falleth. For as
Saint Gregory saith: “He that cannot per-
fectly despise himself, he found never yet
the meek wisdom of our Lord Jesus Christ.”

CHAPTER XX

HOW HYPOCRITES AND HERETICS, FOR LACKING
OF MEEKNESS, HOLD THEMSELVES HIGH
IN THEIR HEARTS ABOVE ALL OTHERS

HYPOCRITES and heretics feel not this meek-
ness, neither in good will nor in affection,
but their hearts and their reins are full dry

32 i

Ch. 20] : OF PERFECTION

and full cold from the soft feeling of this
virtue, and so far are they from it that they
ween that they have it. They gnaw on the
dry bark without, but the sweet kernel of
it and the inly savour they may not come to.
They show outward meekness in habit and
holy speech, in a lowly bearing and, as it
seemeth, in many bodily and ghostly virtues.
But nevertheless in the will and in the
affection of their heart, where meekness
should be, it is feigned. For they deem
and despise and set at naught other men,
that will not do as they do and teach. They
hold them to be either fools by uncunning,
or blinded by fleshly living, and, therefore,
they lift themselves on high in their own
sight above all others, weening that they live
better than others and that they only have
the soothfastness of good living, both in
ghostly feeling and singular grace of God,
and knowledge surpassing other men. And
from this sight of themselves riseth a delight
in their hearts in which they worship and
praise themselves as if there were none but
they. They praise and thank God with their
lips, but in their heart they steal, as thieves,
the worship and the thanking from God, and
set it in themselves; and so they have neither
meekness in feeling nor in will. A wretched
caitiff, or a sinner which falleth all day and
is sorry that he doth so, though he have not

meekness in affection, hath it in a good will.
But a hypocrite or an heretic hath neither;
for they have the condition of the Pharisee
which came, as our Lord saith in the gospel,
with the publican into the temple to pray.
And when he came he prayed not nor asked
aught of God, for he thought he had no
need, but he began to thank God and said
thus: “ Lord, I thank thee that thou givest
me grace more than to other, that I am not
as other men be, robbers, lechers, or other
such sinners.” And he looked beside him
and saw the publican, which he knew for a
wretch, knocking on his breast only crying
after mercy. Then he thanked God he was
not such a one as he was. For “ Lord,” he
said, ‘“ I fast twice in the week, and I pay
truly my tithes.” And when he had done
so, our Lord saith that he gied home again
without grace as he came, and gat right
naught. But now sayest thou, wherein
trespassed this Pharisee, since he thanked
God, and what he said was sooth? As to
this I answer and say, that this Pharisee
trespassed inasmuch as he deemed and re-
proved in his heart the publican, which was
justified of God. And also he trespassed,
for he thanked God only with his mouth;
but he delighted privily and wilfully by a
privy pride in himself of the gifts of God,
stealing the worship and praising from God

Ch. 20] OF PERFECTION

and setting it in himself. This same con-
dition of the Pharisee soothly have heretics
and hypocrites. They will not gladly pray,
and if they pray they meek not themselves,
acknowledging truly their wretchedness, but
they make themselves by a feigning to thank
and love God and speak of Him with their
mouth. But their delight is vain and false
and notin God. And yet they ween not that
so they cannot love God, for as the wise man
saith: It 1s neither fair nor seemly, the praise
of God in the mouth of a sinner.* Wherefore
it is profitable to thee and to me and to other
such wretches, to leave the condition of this
Pharisee and feigned loving of God, and
follow the publican in lowness: asking mercy
and forgiveness of sins and grace of ghostly
virtues, that we may afterwards with a clean
heart soothfastly thank Him and love Him
and give Him wholly worship without feign-
ing. But our Lord asketh thus by His
prophet: Upon whom shall my spirit rest ? and
He answereth Himself and saith, Upon none
but upon the meek, poor and contrite in heart,
and that dread my words.t If, therefore,
thou wilt have the spirit of God ruling in thy
heart, have meekness and dread Him.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

CHAPTER XXI

WHAT THINGS MEN OUGHT TO BELIEVE BY A
SURE FAITH

THE second thing which it behoveth thee to
have, is sure troth in all the articles of the
faith, and in the sacraments of Holy Church,
trowing them steadfastly with all thy will
in thy heart. And though thou feel any
stirring in thy heart against any of them, be
thou steadfast and not too much adread of
feeling such stirrings, but forsake thine own
wit without disputing or ransacking of them,
and set thy faith generally in the faith of
Holy Church, and charge not the stirring of
thy heart which, as it seemeth to thee, is
contrary thereto. For that stirring is not
thy faith, but the faith of Holy Church is
thy faith, though thou never see it nor feel
it. Bear then such stirrings patiently as a
scourge of our Lord, by the which He will
cleanse thine heart and make thy faith
steadfast. And it behoveth thee to love and
worship in thine heart all the laws and or-
dinances made by prelates and rulers of
Holy Church, either in declaring of the faith,
or of the sacraments, or in general of all
Christian men. Meekly and truly assent to
them, though it may be that thou knowest
not the cause of their ordinance; and, though

Ch. 21] OF PERFECTION

thou think that some be unskilful, thou shalt
not deem them nor reprove them, but
worship them all though they belong but
little to thee. Neither receive any opinion
of fantasy or singular conceit under colour
of more holiness—as some do that are not
wise—neither by thine own imagining nor
by teaching of any other man, which con-
trarieth to the least ordinance or general
teaching of all Holy Church. Moreover,
thou shalt hope steadfastly that thou art
ordained of our Lord to be saved as one of
His chosen, by His mercy, and stir not from
this hope whatsoever thou hearest or seest,
or whatsoever temptation thou be in. And
though thou think thyself so great a wretch
that thou art worthy to sink in hell, for that
thou dost no good nor servest God as thou
shouldst do, yet hold thee in this troth and
in this hope and ask mercy, and all shall be
right well. Yea, and though all the devils
of hell appeared in bodily likeness, sleeping
or waking, saying to thee that thou shouldst
not be saved, or all men living in earth, or
all the angels in heaven, if it might be, said
to thee the same, thou shouldst not believe
them, nor be stirred much from the hope of
salvation. ‘This I say to thee, for some be
so weak and so simple that, when they have
given themselves all wholly to serve God
after their cunning, if they feel any stirring

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

within by the incasting of the enemy, or else
from without of any of the devil’s prophets,
which men call soothsayers, that they should
not be saved, or that their state or their
manner of living is not pleasing to God, they
be astonied and stirred up with such words,
and so for uncunning they fall sometimes
into great heaviness, and as it were into a
despair of salvation. Wherefore as me-
thinketh it is speedful to every creature which
by the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ is in
full will to forsake sin, and as clearly as his
conscience telleth him suffereth no deadly
sin to rest in him that he shriveth him not
soon thereof, and meeketh him to the sacra-
ments of Holy Church, to have a trust of
salvation; and much more they that give
themselves wholly to God and flee venial
sins after their might. And on the contrary
wise, as perilous it is to him which lieth
wittingly in deadly sin to have trust of
salvation, if, in hope of that trust, he will
not forsake his sin, nor low him truly to God
and to Holy Church.

Ch. 22] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XXII

HOW A STABLE INTENTION IS NEEDFUL TO
THOSE THAT WOULD PLEASE GOD, AND
DISCRETION IN BODILY WORKS

THE third thing which is needful to thee to
have in thy beginning is an whole and stable
intention, that is to say an whole will and a
desire only to please God; for that is charity,
without which all is naught that thou dost.
And thou shalt set thine intent always to
search and travail how thou mayest please
Him, and at no time to cease wilfully of good
occupation either bodily or ghostly. Nor
shalt thou set a time in thy heart, as thou
wouldest serve God thus long, and then
suffer thine heart to fall down into vain
thoughts and idle occupations, weening that
this were needful for saving of thy bodily
kind; leaving the keeping of thy heart and
good occupation, seeking a rest and comfort
for a time outwardly by thy bodily wits or
inwardly by vanities, as it were for a recrea-
tion of thy spirit, that it should be sharper
afterwards to ghostly travail; for I trow it
is not sooth. I say not that thou mayest
in deed perform thine intent; for ofttimes
thy bodily need, eating, drinking, sleeping,
and speaking, and the frailty of thy flesh
shall let thee, be thou never so busy, and

THE! SCALE [Bk. 1

hinder thee. But I would that thy will and
thine intent were alway whole to travail
ghostly and bodily, and no time to be idle,
but alway lifting up thine heart by desire to
God and to the bliss of heaven, whether
thou eatest or drinkest, or any other bodily
travail that thou usest; as much as thou
mayest wilfully leaving it not. For if thou
have this intent it shall make thee ever quick
and sharp in thy travail; and if thou fall
by frailty or negligence into any idle occupa-
tion or vain speech, it shall smite upon thy
heart sharply as a prick, and make thee to
irk all vanities and to turn again hastily to
inward thinking of Jesus Christ or to some
good occupation. For anent thy bodily
kind it is good to use discretion in eating,
drinking, and sleeping, and in all manner
of bodily penance, and in long prayers by
speech or in bodily feeling by great fervour
of devotion, either in weeping or such other,
and also in imagining of the spirit when a
man feeleth no grace. In all these works it
is good to keep discretion, for the mean is
the best. Butin destroying of sin by keeping
of thine heart, and in continual desire of
virtues and the bliss of heaven, and to have
ghostly knowing and loving of Jesus Christ,
hold thou no mean, for the more that is of
this, the better it is. For thou shalt hate
sin and all fleshly loves and dreads in thine

Ch. 23] OF PERFECTION

heart without ceasing; and thou shalt love
virtues and cleanness, and desire them with-
out stinting if thou mayest. I say not that
it is needful to salvation, but I hope it is
speedful, and if thou keep it thou shalt profit
more in a year in virtues, than thou shalt
without this intent in seven years.

CHAPTER XXIII

OF A LITTLE REHEARSING OF THINGS. SAID
BEFORE, AND OF MAKING OF OFFERING
THAT SHOULD BE OFFERED TO GOD

Now I have told thee first of the end which
thou shalt behold in thy desire, draw thee
towards it as much as thou mayest. Also
I have told thee of the beginning, what it
needeth to have, as meekness, sure troth,
and an whole intent to God; upon which
ground thou shalt set thy ghostly house by
prayer and meditation, and by other ghostly
virtues. Then I say to thee thus; pray thou,
or think thou, or any other deed that thou
dost, good by grace or bad by thine own
frailty, or whatsoever thou feelest, seest or
hearest, smellest or savourest, without in
thy bodily wits or within in imagination,
knowing or feeling in thy reason: bring it all
within the troth and the rules of Holy Church,
and cast all into the mortar of meekness, and
4I D

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

break it small with the pestle of the dread
of God, and throw the powder of all this
into the fire of desire, and offer it so to God;
and I tell thee forsooth, well shall that offer-
ing be liken in the sight of thy Lord Jesus,
and sweet shall the smoke of that fire smell
to the face of thy Lord Jesus. This is to
say, draw all this that thou feelest within
the troth of Holy Church and break thyself
in meekness, and offer the desire of thine
heart to thy Lord Jesus, to have Him only
and naught else but Him. And if thou do
thus I hope by the grace of Christ thou shalt
never be overcome by thine enemy. This
taught us Saint Paul, when he said thus:
Whether ye eat or drink, or what manner of
deed that ye do, do allin the name of our Lord
Fesus Christ,* forsaking yourself, and offer
it up to Him. Means which thou shalt
most use, as I have before said, are prayer
and meditation. First I shall show thee a
little of prayer, and after of meditation.

CHAPTER XXIV

OF PRAYER THAT IS SPEEDFUL TO GET CLEAN-
NESS OF HEART AND VIRTUES

PRAYER is profitable and speedful to use to

get cleanness of heart by destroying of sin

and receiving of virtues. Not that thou
xen Gorsxak

Ch. 25] OF PERFECTION

shouldest by thy prayer make our Lord know
what thou desirest, for He knoweth well
enough what thou needest, but to make thee
able and ready by thy prayer that thou
mayest receive as a clean vessel the grace that
our Lord will freely give to thee; which grace
may not be felt till thou be assayed and
purified by the fire of desire in devout prayer.
For though it be so that prayer is not the
cause for which our Lord giveth grace,
nevertheless it is a way by which grace freely
cometh to a soul.

CHAPTER XXV

HOW MEN SHOULD PRAY, AND WHEREON THE
POINT OF THEIR THOUGHT SHOULD BE
SET IN PRAYER

BUT now desirest thou peradventure to know
how thou shouldest pray, and upon what
thing thou shouldest set the point of thy
thought in thy prayer; and also what prayer
were best for thee to use. As to the first,
I answer and say thus: that when thou art
wakened of thy sleep and ready to pray,
thou shalt feel thyself fleshly and heavy;
ever downward drawn to vain thought,
either of dreams or fantasies or of unskilful
business of the world and of thy flesh.
Then it behoveth thee to quicken thine
heart by prayer, and stir it as much as thou

THE SCALE [Bk. I

mayest to some devotion. And in thy prayer
thou shalt not set thine heart in any bodily
\thing, but all thy travail shall be to draw in
thy thought from beholding of all earthly
things, that thy desire may be as it were
naked and bare from all earthly things, ever
upward stying unto Jesus Christ, whom thou
mayest never see bodily as He is in His god-
head, nor by bodily likeness in imagination;
though thou mayest, through devout and
continual beholding of the meekness of His
precious manhood, feel the goodness and
grace of His godhead. When thy desire is
eased and holpen and, as it were, made free
from all fleshly thoughts and affections, and
is much lifted up by ghostly might into
savour and delight in Him of His ghostly
presence, and held therein much of the time
of thy prayer, so that thou hast no great
mind of earthly thing, or else the mind
grieveth thee but little: if thou canst pray
thus, then canst thou pray well. For prayer
is naught else but a stying desire of the heart
into God, by withdrawing of the heart from
all earthly thoughts. And so is prayer
likened to a fire which of its own kind leaveth
the lowness of the earth and always styeth
up into the air. Right so desire in prayer,
when it is touched and lightened of the
ghostly fire which is God, it is ever upstying
to Him that it came from.

Ch. 27] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XXVI
OF THE FIRE OF LOVE

ALL men that speak of the fire of love know
not well what it is; for what it is I cannot tell
thee, save this (I may tell thee) that it is
neither bodily nor bodily felt. A soul may
feel it in prayer or in devotion, which soul
is in the body, yet feeleth it not by any bodily
wit. For though it be so that, if it work
in a soul, the body may be turned into an
heat and, as it were, chafed* for loving
travail of the spirit; nevertheless, the fire
of love is not bodily, for it is only the ghostly
desire of the soul. ‘This is no doubt to any
man or woman that feeleth devotion; but
some are simple, and ween—because it is
called fire—that it should be as hot as bodily
fire is, and for this I say what I have said.

CHAPTER XXVII

THAT THE CERTAIN PRAYER IN SPEECH OR-
DAINED OF GOD AND OF HOLY CHURCH IS
BEST FOR THEM THAT ARE NEWLY TURNED
TO GOD AND TO DEVOTION

Now as to the other question, to know what
prayer were best for thee to use, I shall say
* Chafed—z.e., warmed.

THE SCALE [Bk. I

as methinketh. Thou shalt understand that
there be three manner of prayers: the first
is prayer of speech made specially of God as
is the Pater noster, and made also more
generally by the ordinance of Holy Church,
as matins, evensong and hours; and also made
by devout men of other special sayings, as
to our Lord and to our Lady, and to His
saints. As to this manner of prayer, which
is called vocal, methinketh unto thee—that
art religious, and by certain rule art bound
to say matins and hours—I hold it most
speedful to say them as devoutly as thou
mayest. For when thou sayest thy matins,
thou sayest also thy Pater noster principally;
and overmore to stir thee to more devotion
was it ordained to say also psalms and
hymns and such other which He made by the
Holy Ghost, as the Pater noster is. And
therefore thou shalt not say them hastily
and recklessly, as if thou wert evil apaid*
that thou art bound to them, but thou shalt
gather thy affection and thy thought to say
them more sadly and more devoutly than
any other special prayer of devotion, trowing
forsooth that, since it is the prayer of Holy
Church, there is no vocal prayer so profitable
to thee to use commonly as that is. And
so shalt thou put away all heaviness, and by
grace shalt thou turn thy need into a good
* Evil apaid—.e., ill-content.

Ch. 27] OF PERFECTION

will, and thy bond into a great freedom, that
shall be to thee no letting of ghostly occupa-
tion. And after this thou mayest, if thou
wilt, use others, as the Pater noster or any such
other, and of these that in which thou feelest
most savour and most ghostly comfort, that
I hold best for thee. This manner of
prayer is speedful commonly to every man
in the beginning of his conversion to use
most of any other ghostly occupation. Fora
man in the beginning is rude and boisterous
and fleshly, unless he have the grace, and
cannot think any ghostly thoughts in medita-
tion, for his soul is not yet cleansed from old
sin. And, therefore, I think it is most
speedful to use this manner of prayer, as to
say his Pater noster and his Ave, and to read
upon his psalter and such other. For he
that cannot run lightly by ghostly prayer, for
his feet of knowing and loving be sick for
sin, he needeth to have a sure staff to hold
him by. This staff is special prayer of
speech ordained by God and Holy Church
in help of men’s souls; by the which prayer
the soul of a fleshly man—that is alway
falling downward into worldly thoughts, and
fleshly affections—shall be lifted up from
them, and be held by it as by a staff, fed with
the sweet words of the prayer as a child with
milk, and ruled by it that he fall not into
errors nor fantasies by his vain meditations.

THE SCALE [Bk. I

For in this manner of prayer is no deceit;
whoso will, may steadfastly and meekly
travail therein.

CHAPTER XXVIII

WHAT PERIL IT IS TO MEN THAT, IN THE
BEGINNING OF THEIR TURNING TO GOD,
LEAVE TOO SOON THE COMMON PRAYER
OF HOLY CHURCH AND GIVE THEMSELVES
TO MEDITATION

THOU mayest see by this, therefore, that
these men, if any be such, that in the begin-
ning of their conversion or soon after—when
they have felt a little ghostly comfort either
in devotion or in knowing and are not yet
stabled therein—leave such vocal prayer
and other bodily exercise too soon and give
them wholly to meditation, they are not wise.
For often in the rest of their meditation they
imagine and think on ghostly things after
their own wit, and follow their bodily feeling,
and have not yet received grace thereto; and
therefore by indiscretion they often over-
travail their wit and break their bodily might,
and so fallinto fantasies and singular conceits,
or into open errors, and let the grace that God
giveth them by such vanities. The cause of
all this is a privy pride and presumption in
themselves; as when they have felt a little

Ch. 29] OF PERFECTION

grace they ween it is so much surpassing
others, that they fall into vainglory and so
they lose it. If they wist how little it were
that they feel in regard of that which God
giveth or may give, they would be ashamed
to speak out thereof, unless it were in great
need. Of this manner of prayer by speech
David the prophet, to stir other men to pray
both with heart and with mouth, said: With
my voice I cried to God, and with my speech
I besought our Lord.*

CHAPTER XXIX

OF THE SECOND MANNER OF PRAYER, THAT IS
IN SPEECH NOT CERTAIN, BUT FOLLOWETH
THE STIRRING OF DEVOTION

THE second manner of prayer is by speech,
but it is not of any certain special saying.
And this is when a man or woman feeleth
grace of devotion by the gift of God, and in
his devotion speaketh to Him as if he were
bodily in His presence, with such words as
accordeth most to his stirring for the time,
and as come to his mind after divers regards
which he feeleth in his heart : either rehearsing
his sins and his wretchedness, or the malice
and sleights of the enemy, or else the mercy
and the goodness of God. And with that

he crieth with desire of heart and with speech
of mouth to: our Lord for succour and for
help, as a man that were in peril among his
enemies or in sickness, showing his sores to
God as to a leech; saying as David said:
Ah, Lord, deliver me from mine enemtes,*
or else thus: Ah, Lord, heal my soul for I
have sinned against Thee,t or other such as
come to mind. And also he thinketh so
much goodness and grace and mercy in God,
that him liketh with great affection of the
heart to love Him and thank Him by such
words and psalms as accord to the loving and
praising of God: as David said: Love ye and
praise our Lord for He is good and merciful,
and by such other as he is stirred to say.

CHAPTER XXX

THAT THIS SECOND MANNER OF PRAYER
PLEASETH GOD MUCH, AND MAKETH A
MAN TO BEHAVE HIM IN BODY AS HE
WERE DRUNKEN, AND IN SOUL TO BE
WOUNDED WITH THE SWORD OF LOVE

THIS manner of prayer much pleaseth God,
for it is only of the affection in the heart;
and therefore it goeth never unsped without
some grace. This prayer belongeth to the

second part of contemplation as I have said
before, for whoso hath this gift of God fer-
vently, it behoveth him for the time to flee
presence and company of all men, to be
alone that he be not letted. Whoso have it,
let him hold it while he may, for it may not

plenteously it is wonderfully travailous to
the spirit, though it be liking; and it is much
wasting to the bodily kind whoso much uses

man that were mad or drunken and could
have no rest. This is a point of the passion
of love the which by great violence and
mastery breaketh down lusts and likings of
any earthly thing, and it so woundeth the
soul with the blissful sword of love, that the
body falleth down and may not bear it.
This touching is of so great might that the
most vicious or fleshly man living in earth,
if he were well touched once mightily with
this sharp sword, he should be right sad and
sober a great while after, and loathe all the
lusts and likings of his flesh and all earthly
things which he had most delight in.

CHAPTER XXXI

HOW THE FIRE OF LOVE WASTETH ALL FLESHLY
LUSTS, AS OTHER FIRE WASTETH BODILY
THINGS

Or this manner of feeling speaketh the
prophet Jeremy thus: The love and the
feeling of God was made in my heart not
fire but as fire glowing.* For as bodily fire
burneth and wasteth all bodily things where
it cometh, right so ghostly fire, as is the love
of God, burneth and wasteth all fleshly loves
and likings in a man’s soul. And this fire
is stokent in my bones, as the prophet saith
of himself. That is to say, This love ful-
filleth the mights of my soul as mind, reason
and will, with grace and ghostly sweetness,
as marrow filleth the bone: and that within,
not without in the wits. Nevertheless, it is
so mighty within that it smiteth out into the
body, and maketh all the body to quake and
tremble. It is so far from the bodily kind
and so uncouth¢ that he cannot reason of it
and may not bear it, but faileth and falleth
down as the prophet saith. And, therefore,
our Lord tempereth it and withdraweth the
fervour and suffereth the heart to fall into a
sobriety of more softness. Whoso can pray

+ Uncouth—7.e., unknown, unusual.

€hi32] OF PERFECTION

thus often, he speedeth soon in his travail.
He shall get more of virtues in a little time
than some man without this (or another as
good) shall get in long time, for all the bodily
penance that he might do; and whoso hath
this needeth not to charge the bodily kind
with more penance than it beareth if he have
it often.

CHAPTER XXXII

OF THE THIRD MANNER OF PRAYER, THAT IS
ONLY IN THE HEART WITHOUT SPEECH
OUTWARD

THE third manner of prayer is only in the ,
heart without speech, by great rest and soft-
ness of the body and of the soul. A clean
heart him behoveth to have that shall pray
well thus, for it is of such men and women
that, by long travail bodily and ghostly or
else by such sharp smitings of love as I have
before said, come into a rest of spirit so that
their affection is turned into a ghostly savour,
that they may nigh continually pray in their
heart and love and praise God without great
letting of temptations or of vanities, as I have
before said in the second part of contempla-
tion. Of this manner of prayer saith Saint
Paul thus: Jf I pray with my tongue only
by the will of the spirit and by travail, the
prayer is meedful, but my soul is not fed, for

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

it feeleth not the fruit of ghostly sweetness
by understanding. What shall I then do?
saith Saint Paul. And he answereth and
saith: I shall pray by travail and desire of the
spirit* and I shall pray also more inwardly
in my spirit without travail in ghostly savour
and sweetness of the love and the sight of
God; by the which sight and feeling of love
my soul shall be fed. Thus, as I under-
stand, Saint Paulcould pray. Of this manner
of prayer speaketh our Lord in holy writ
by figure thus: The fire of love shall ever be lit
in the soul of a devout and clean man or
woman, the which is the altar of our Lord,
and the priest shall every day at morn lay to
sticks and nourish the fire. That is to say
this man shall by holy psalms, clean thoughts,
and fervent desire nourish the fire of love in
his heart, that it go not out at any time.t
This rest our Lord giveth to some of His
servants, as it were a reward of their travail
and a shadow of the love which they shall.
have in the bliss of heaven.

CHAPTER XXXIII

HOW MEN SHOULD DO THAT ARE TRAVAILED
WITH VAIN THOUGHTS IN THEIR PRAYERS

BuT now sayest thou that I speak over high
to thee in this manner of prayer, for it is
no mastery to me to say it, but to do it there
is the mastery. Thou sayest that thou canst
not pray thus devoutly nor so wholly in heart
as I speak of. For when thou wouldst heave
the mind of thine heart upward to God in
thy prayer, thou feelest so many vain thoughts
of thine own deeds before done, or what thou
shalt do, and of other men’s deeds and such
many other, letting and tarrying* thee so,
that thou mayest neither feel savour nor rest
nor devotion in thy saying; and ofttimes, the
more thou travailest to keep thine heart,
the further it is from thee and the harder—
sometimes from the beginning to the last
end—so that thou thinkest all that thou dost
is but lost. As unto this that thou sayest,
that I speak too high to thee of prayer, I
grant well that I speak otherwise than I
can or may do. Nevertheless, I say it for
this intent, that thou shouldest know how we
ought to pray if we did well. And since we
may not do so, that we know then meekly
our feebleness and cry God mercy. Our

Lord bade thus Himself when He said: Thou
shalt love God of all thine heart and all thy
soul and all thy mght.* It is impossible to
any man living in earth to fulfil this bidding
so fully as it is said, and yet nevertheless our

Saint Bernard saith, that we should know
thereby our feebleness and then meekly cry
after mercy, and we shall have it. Neverthe-
less, I shall tell thee as methinketh in this
asking.

When thou shalt pray, make thine intent
and thy will in the beginning as whole and
as clean to God as thou mayest shortly in
thy mind, and then begin and do as thou
mayest. And though thou be never so much
letted against thy first will be not too much
adread, nor too angry with thyself, nor
impatient against God, that He giveth thee
not that savour and ghostly sweetness with
devotion as thou thinkest that He giveth to
other creatures. And see thereby thine own
feebleness and bear it easily, holding in thine
own sight thine own prayer, feeble as it is,
with meekness of heart, trusting also surely
in the mercy of our Lord that He shall make it
good and profitable to thee more than thou
knowest or feelest. For wit thou well that
thou art excused of thy debt, and thou shalt
have meed for it as for any other good deed

“of Luke x27

Ch. 33] OF PERFECTION

that thou dost in charity, though thine heart
were not thereupon in the doing. Do,
therefore, that which belongeth to thee, and
suffer our Lord to give what He will, and

strive no more therewith, nor hang longer
thereupon, as thou wouldest by mastery not
feel such wretchedness. Leave off and go
to some other good deed, bodily or ghostly;
and think to do better another time. But
though another time thou fall in the same—
yea an hundred times, yea a thousand times—
yet do as I have said, and all shall be well.
Furthermore, a soul that never may find rest
of heart in prayer, but all her lifetime is
striving with her thoughts and tarried and
troubled with them, if she keep her in meek-
ness and charity in other sides, she shall have
full much meed in heaven for her good
travail.

CHAPTER XXXIV

OF MEDITATION OF SINFUL MEN AFTER THAT
THEY ARE WHOLLY TURNED TO GOD

Now of meditation shall I tell thee a little,
as methinketh. Thou shalt understand that
in meditations may no certain rule well be
set for every man to keep; for they are in free
gift of our Lord after the divers disposings
of chosen souls, and after the state that they
are in. And also after that they profit in
virtues and in their estate, so He increaseth
their meditations both in ghostly knowing
and loving of Him; for whoso is ever alike
wise* in knowing of God and ghostly things,
it seemeth that he waxeth but a little in the
love of God: And that may be showed
openly in the apostles; when they in the day
of Pentecost were fulfilled with burning love
of the Holy Ghost, they were made neither
fools nor foltes,} but they were made won-
drous wise both in knowing and speaking of
God and of ghostly things, as much as a
man might have in flesh living. Thus
speaketh Holy Writ of them: They were
fulfilled of the Holy Ghost, and they began to
speak the great marvels of God. And all
* Query ? always equally wise.
+ Foltes—i.e., fools, dolts. + Acts ii 4, 11.

Ch. 34] OF PERFECTION

that knowing they had by ravishing in the
love of the Holy Ghost.

Divers meditations there are which our
Lord putteth in a man’s heart. Some shall
I tell thee as methinketh: for this intent, if
thou feel any of them, that thou shouldest
the better travailin them. In the beginning
of conversion of such a man that hath been
much soiled with worldly or fleshly sins,
commonly his thought is most upon his sins
with great compunction and sorrow of heart,
and with great weepings and many tears of
the eye, meekly and busily asking mercy
and forgiveness of God for them. And if
he be touched sharply, for that our Lord will
make him soon clean, he shall well think that
his sins are ever in his sight, so foul and so
horrible that scarcely shall he more bear
himself. And though he shrive him never
so cleanly, yet shall he find fighting and
fretting and biting of his conscience, such
that he shall think that he is not shriven right.
And scarcely shall he have any rest more,
insomuch that he could not endure such
travail, were it not that our Lord of His
mercy comforteth him sometimes as He will,
by great devotion of His passion or of some
other, as He will give it. Upon this manner
worketh our Lord in some men’s hearts,
more or less, as He will. And all this is the
great mercy of our Lord, that not only will

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

He forgive the sin or the trespass, but He will
forgive the trespass and the pain for it in
purgatory, for such a little pain here of biting
of conscience. And also if He will dispose
a man to receive any special gift of the love
of God, it behoveth him first to be scoured
and cleansed by such a fire of compunction
for all the great sins before done. Of this
manner of travail speaketh David in many
places of the psalter, and specially in the
psalm Miserere mei Deus.*

THAT THE MEDITATION OF THE MANHOOD OF

right profitable and gracious, and that shalt

thou art stirred to a meditation in God, and

CHAPTER XXXVI

THAT THE MEDITATION OF THE PASSION OF
CHRIST IS WITHDRAWN FROM THEM THAT
IT IS GIVEN TO, OFTTIMES FOR DIVERS
CAUSES

THIS manner of meditation a man hath not
always when he would, but when our Lord
will give it. Unto some men and women
He giveth it all their lifetime, by times, when
He visiteth them; for some men are so tender
in their affection that, when they hear men
speak or else when they think of His precious
passion, their hearts melt in devotion and are
fed and comforted against all manner temp-
tations of the enemy: and that is a great gift
of God. To some men He giveth it first
plenteously, and afterwards He withdraweth
it for divers causes, either if a man were
proud of it in his own sight or for some other
sin, by which he maketh himself unable to
receive the grace; or else our Lord with-
draweth it and all other devotions sometimes
from a man or a woman, for He will suffer
him to be assailed by temptations of his
enemy. And so will He dispose a man to
know and feel himself more ghostly. For
He said so Himself to His disciples: Jz zs
speedful* to you that I go from you bodily,
* Speedful—z.e., profitable.

THE. SCALE [Bk. I

for if I go not the Holy Ghost may not come
to you.x For as long as He was with them
they loved Him much, but it was fleshly in
His manhood; and therefore it was speedful
to them that He should withdraw the bodily
form from their sight, that the Holy Ghost
might come to them and teach them to love
Him and know Him more ghostly, as He
did on the day of Pentecost. Right so is it
speedful to some that our Lord withdraw
a little the bodily and the fleshly likeness from
the eye of the soul, that the heart might be
set and fixed more busily in ghostly desire
and seeking of His godhead.

CHAPTER XXXVII
OF DIVERS TEMPTATIONS OF THE FIEND

NEVERTHELESS, it behoveth a man to suffer
many temptations first. And these tempta-
tions fall ofttimes to some men and women
after comfort is withdrawn, upon divers
manners, by malice of the enemy. As thus,
when the devil perceiveth devotion much
withdrawn, so that the soul is left as it were
naked for a time, then sendeth he to some
men temptations of lechery and gluttony,
so hot and so burning that they shall think
that they felt never none so grievous in all
* John xvi 7.

Ch. 37] OF PERFECTION

their lifetime before, when they gave them-
selves most to sin. Insomuch that they
shall think it impossible to stand long and
suffer, and that they shall needs fall unless
they have help. And, therefore, have they
then much sorrow, both for the lacking of
comfort and devotion which they were wont
to have, and they have much dread of falling
from God by such open sins. And all this
worketh the devil at the sufferance of God,
to make them forego their good purposes
and turn again to sin as they were wont to
do. But whoso will abide awhile and suffer
a little pain, and not turn again to sin for
anything, the hand of our Lord is full near
and helpeth rightsoon. For Hekeepeth him
full safely, and the man wots not how; as
the prophet David saith in the person of our
Lord: I am with him in his tribulation, and in
temptation I shall deliver him, and I shall
make him glorious in my bliss.* Some men
be tempted by ghostly sins maliciously, as
by mis-trowing of the faith, or of the sacra-
ment of our Lord’s blessed body: also of
despair or of blasphemy in our Lord or any
of his saints: or loathing of their life, or
bitterness or unskilful heaviness, or of too
much dread of themselves or of their body,
if they put themselves wholly to God’s
service. Some men be tempted also—and

namely solitary men and women—by dreads

CHAPTER XXXVIII

OF DIVERS REMEDIES AGAINST TEMPTATIONS
OF THE FIEND

REMEDY to such men or women that are
travailed thus or any other wise may be
this: first that they will put all their trust in
our Lord Jesus Christ and bring to mind
often His passion and the pains that He
suffered for us, and that they then trow
steadfastly that all sorrows and travail that
they suffer in such temptations—which to
an uncunning man seemeth a forsaking of
God—they are no reproving nor any for-
saking, but assaying for their betterment;
either for cleansing of their sins before done,
or for great increasing of their meed and
disposing to much grace, if they will suffer
awhile and stand fast, so that they turn not
again wilfully to sin. Another remedy is
that they dread not nor charge not as for a
sin, nor set not at heart such malicious

Ch. 38] OF PERFECTION

stirrings of despair or of blasphemy, or of
the sacrament, or any such other that were
ugly to hear; for the feeling of these tempta-
tions defileth the soul no more than if they
heard an hound bark or felt a flea bite. They
tarry the soul, but they appair* it not, if a
man will despise them and set them at
naught. For it is not good to strive with
them, to put them out by mastery; for the
more that men strive with such thoughts the
more they cleave to them. And, therefore,
they shall, as much as they may, draw out
their thought from them, and set it to some
other occupation. And yet if they will ever
hang upon them, then it is good to them that
they be not angry nor heavy for feeling them,
but with a good trust in God do bear them,
as it were a bodily pain or a scourge of our
Lord for cleansing of their sins, as long as
He will, for His love; as He was scourged
and bare the cross for their love. And over
this it is good to them to show their hearts
to some wise man in the beginning before
they be rooted in the heart, and that they
leave their own wit and follow the counsel of
him; and that they show them not lightly to
any uncunning or worldly man, which never
felt such temptations, for such might lightly
bring a simple soul into despair by uncunning
of himself. Of this manner of temptations,

CHAPTER XXXIX

HOW GOD SUFFERETH THEM THAT HE CHOOSETH
TO BE TARRIED AND TEMPTED, AND AFTER-
WARDS COMFORTETH THEM AND STABLISH-
ETH THEM IN GRACE

ALSO in comfort of such men that they should
not despair in temptation, the Wise Man
saith of our Lord: In temptation our Lord
forsaketh not a man, but goeth with him from
the beginning unto the last end; for he saith,
first He chooseth Him, and that is when He
draweth a man to Him by comfort of devo-
tion: and afterward sorrow and dread and
assaying He bringeth upon him, and that is
when He withdraweth devotion and suffereth
him to be tempted. And he saith that He
tormenteth him in tribulation until He have well
assayed him in his thoughts, and until a man
will put all his trustin Him fully. And then
after this our Lord bringeth him out into the
right way, and fasteneth him to Him and glad-
deth him, and then showeth him His privities,
and giveth him His treasure of knowing and
understanding of righteousness.*

By these words of Holy Writ mayest thou
see that these or any other temptations, be
they never so ugly, are speedful and profitable
to a man that by grace is in full will to forsake

* Ecclus. iv 18-21.

THE SCALE [Bk. I

sin, if he will suffer as he may and abide
God’s will, and not turn again to sin which
he hath forsaken, for no sorrow nor pain nor
dread of such temptations, but ever stand
still in travail and in prayer with good hope.
Our Lord of His endless goodness, having
pity and mercy of all His creatures, when He
seeth time layeth to His hand, and smiteth
down the devil and all his power; and easeth
them of their travail, and putteth away all
dreads and sorrows and darkness out of their
hearts. And He bringeth into their souls

speedful to them, giving them also a new
ghostly might to withstand all the fondings*
of the fiend, and all deadly sins without great
travail, and leadeth them into a sadness of
good virtuous living. In which, if they be
meek, He keepeth them unto the last end,
and then taketh them all wholly to Him.
This thing I say to thee, if thou be tarried or
travailed with any such manner temptations,
be not too much adread, but do as I have said
and better if thou may; and I hope by the
grace of Jesus Christ thou shalt never be
overcome by thine enemy.

CHAPTER XL

THAT A MAN SHOULD NOT GIVE HIMSELF TO
IDLENESS, NOR LIGHTLY LEAVE THE GRACE
THAT IS GIVEN HIM OF GOD

AFTER this, when thou art escaped such
temptations, or else our Lord hath kept thee
(as He doth many by His mercy) that thou
shalt not be tarried much with any such,
then it is good for thee that thou turn not
thy rest into idleness; for there is many a
man that taketh rest upon him too soon.
But thou shalt, if thou wilt, begin a new game
and a new travail; and that is to enter within
into thine own soul by meditation, to know
what it is, and by the knowing thereof to
come to the ghostly knowing of God. For
Saint Austin saith: ‘‘By the knowing of
myself I shall get the knowing of God.”’ I
say not that it is needful for thee and due
to travail so, nor for any other man, unless
he feel him stirred by grace as he were called
thereto. For our Lord giveth divers gifts
whereso He will, not to one man all, nor to all
men one; save charity which is common to
allmen. And, therefore, if a man or woman
have received a gift of God—as devotion in
prayer or in the passion of Christ, or any
other be it never so little—let him not leave
it too soon for any other, unless he feel

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

soothfastly a better; but hold that he hath
and travail therein stably, ever desiring a
better when God will give it. Nevertheless
if that be withdrawn somewhat, and he
seeth a better and feeleth his heart stirred
thereto, then seemeth it a calling of our Lord
to the better, and then it is time that he
follow after to get it, and fulfil it as swiftly
as he may,

CHAPTER XLI

THAT A MAN SHOULD KNOW THE MEASURE OF
HIS GIFT, THAT HE MAY DESIRE AND TAKE
A BETTER WHEN GOD WILL GIVE IT

OUR holy fathers herebefore taught us that
we should know the measure of our gift,
and work upon that; not taking upon us by
feigning more than we have in feeling. We
may ever desire the best, but we may not
ever work the best, for we have not yet
received that grace. An hound that runneth
after the hare only because he seeth other
hounds to run, when he is weary resteth him
or turneth him home again. But if he run
because he seeth the hare, he will not spare
for weariness till he have gotten it. Right `
so it is ghostly. Whoso hath grace, be it
never so little, and leaveth wilfully the work-
ing thereof, and maketh himself to travail
in another which he hath not yet, but only

72 ;

Ch. 41] OF PERFECTION

because he seeth or heareth that other men
did so, soothly he may run awhile till he be
weary, and then shall he turn home again;
and unless he beware, he may hurt his feet
by some fantasies ere he come home. But
he that worketh in such grace as he hath,
and desireth by prayer meekly and lastingly
after more, and after feeleth his heart stirred
to follow the grace which he desired, he may
safely run if he keep meekness. And there-
fore desire of God as much as thou mayest,
without measure or discretion, of all that
belongeth to His love or to heavenly bliss;
for who can most desire of God, most shall
feel of Him. But work as thou mayest, and
cry God mercy for that thou mayest not.
Thus it seemeth Saint Paul said that every
man has his gift of God, one thus and another
thus ; for to every man that shall be saved is
given a grace after the measure of Christ’s
gift.* And, therefore, it is speedful that we
know the gifts which are given us of God,
that we may work in them, for by these we
shall be saved; as some by bodily works and
by deeds of mercy, some by great bodily
penance, some by sorrow and weeping for
their sins all their lifetime, some by preaching
and teaching, some by divers graces and gifts
of devotion shall be saved and come to bliss.

CHAPTER XLII

THAT A MAN SHOULD TRAVAIL TO KNOW HIS
OWN SOUL AND THE MIGHTS THEREOF,
AND BREAK DOWN THE GROUND OF SIN ~
THEREIN

NEVERTHELESS, there is one work which is
needful and speedful to travail in, and I
hope an high plain way, as much as may be,
in man’s work to contemplation. And that
is for a man to enter into himself, to know
his own soul and the mights thereof, the
fairness and the foulness of it. In this —
inward beholding thou shalt see the worship

and dignity which it should have by kind
of the first making, and thou shalt see the
wretchedness and the mischief which thou
art fallen into for sin. And of this sight
shall come a desire with great longing in
thine heart to recover again that dignity and
worship which thou hast lost. Also thou
shalt feel a loathing and a dread of thyself,
with a great will to destroy and bear down
thyself and all things that let thee from that
dignity and joy. This is a ghostly travail
hard and sharp in the beginning, whoso will
quickly travail therein. For it is a travail
in the soul against the ground of all sins little
and much, which ground is naught else but
a false misruled love of man to himself.

Ch. 42] OF PERFECTION

Out of this love, as Saint Austin saith,
springeth all manner of sin deadly and venial;
and soothly until this ground be well ran-
sacked and deep delved, and as it were near
dried up by outcasting of all fleshly and
worldly dreads and loves, a soul may never
feel ghostly the burning love of Jesus Christ,
nor have the homeliness of His gracious
presence nor clear sight of ghostly things by
light of understanding. This is the travail
whereby it behoveth a man to draw his heart
and his mind from fleshly love and the liking
of all earthly creatures, from vain thoughts
and from fleshly imaginations, and out from
the love and vicious feeling of himself, that
the soul may find no rest in any fleshly
thoughts nor earthly affection. Then, inas-
much as the soul may not find his ghostly
rest in the love and in the sight of Jesus
Christ, needs must that it suffer pain. This
travail is some deal strait and narrow, never-
theless I hope it is the way which Christ
taught to them that would be His perfect
lovers in the gospel, saying thus: Strive ye
to enter by a strait gate, for the way that
leadeth to heaven 1s narrow, and few men find
it.* And how strait this way is, our Lord
telleth in another place thus: Whoso will
come after Me, let him forsake himself and hate
his own soul ; that is to say, forsake all fleshly
* Luke xiii 24.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

love and hate his own fleshly life and vain
liking of all his bodily wits for love of Me;
and take the cross, that is to say, suffer the
pain of this world awhile, and then follow
Me,* that is to say, in contemplation of My |
manhood and of My godhead. This is a
strait way and a narrow, that no bodily thing
may pass through it, for it is a slaying of all

CHAPTER XLIII

HOW A MAN SHALL KNOW THE WORTHINESS
AND THE WORSHIP OF HIS SOUL THAT
HE HAD FIRST OF GOD; AND WHAT
WRETCHEDNESS AND MISCHIEF IT IS
FALLEN INTO FOR SIN

THE soul of a man is a life made of three
mights—mind, reason, and will—to the
image and likeness of the Blessed Trinity.
Inasmuch as the mind was made mighty
and steadfast, by the virtue of the Father
Almighty, to behold Him without forgetting,
distracting or letting of any creature, so it
hath likeness of the Father. The reason
was made bright and clear without error or
darkness, as perfectly as a soul in a body
unglorified might have; and so it hath the
likeness of the Son, which is endless wisdom.
And the love and the will was made clean,
burning into God without beastly love of the
flesh or of any creature, by the sovereign
goodness of God the Holy Ghost, and so it
hath the likeness of the Holy Ghost which
is blessed love. So that a man’s soul—which
may be called a made trinity—was fulfilled
in mind, sight, and love, of the unmade
blessed Trinity which is our Lord. This
is the dignity, the state, and the worship
of a man’s soul by kind of the first making.

i!

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

This state thou haddest in Adam before the
first sin of man. But when Adam sinned,
choosing love and delight in himself and in
creatures, he lost all his worship and his
dignity (and thou also in him), and fell from
that blessed trinity into a foul, dark, wretched ©
trinity; that is into forgetting of God and
unknowing of Him, and into a beastly liking
of himself. And that unskilfully, for as
David saith in the psalter: A man when he was
in worship knew it not,* and therefore he
lost it and was made like a beast. See now
then the wretchedness of thy soul; for as thy
mind was some time stable in God, right so
it hath now forgotten Him and seeketh its
rest in creatures—now from one, now from
another—and never may find full rest, for
it hath lost Him in whom is full rest. And
right so it is of reason, and the love also which
was clean in ghostly savour and sweetness,
now it is turned into a foul beastly
lust and liking in itself and in creatures,
and in fleshly savours both in the wits,
as in gluttony and lechery, and in imagining,
as in pride, vainglory, and covetousness.
Insomuch that thou mayest scarcely do any
deed, without being defiled with vainglory;
nor mayest thou well use any of thy five
wits cleanly in any creature delectable,
without thy heart being taken and inflamed

with a vain lust and liking of it, which
putteth out the love of God from the heart

delivered thee and restored thee again.

HOW EVERY MAN MAY BE SAVED BY THE
PASSION OF CHRIST, BE HE NEVER SO
WICKED

AND, therefore, if thou thinkest that I have
herebefore spoken too high to thee, for thou
mayest not take it nor fulfil it as I have said
or shall say, I will now fall down to thee as
low as thou wilt, for my profit as well as for
thine. Therefore say I thus: though thou
be never so much a wretch, hast thou done

THE | SCALE [Bk. I

never so much sin, forsake thyself and all

passion meekly and trustfully, and without

original sin and from all other thou shalt -
be saved; yea, and thou shalt be saved as an
anker* enclosed. And not only thou, but
all Christian souls which trust upon this
passion and meek themselves, acknowledging
their wretchedness, asking mercy and for-

only, lowing themselves to the sacraments

of our Lord Jesus Christ be saved and come
to the bliss of heaven. All this thou knowest
well, but yet it liketh me to say it. See here
the endless mercy of our Lord, how low He
falleth to thee and to me and to all sinful
caitiffs. Ask mercy, therefore, and have it.
Thus said the prophet in the person of our
Lord: Every man, what that he be, that calleth
the name of God, that is to say, who asketh
salvation by Jesus and His passion, he shail
be saved.t This courtesy of our Lord some

But then sayest thou “‘ If this be sooth,

sickness; as Saint John saith of himself and

CHAPTER XLV

THAT A MAN SHOULD BE BUSY TO REGAIN HIS
WORTHINESS, AND REFORM AGAIN IN
HIMSELF THE IMAGE OF THE TRINITY

NEVERTHELESS, though this be sooth of the
endless mercy of God unto thee and to me
and to all mankind, we shall not therefore in
trust of this be more reckless wilfully in our
living, but the more busy to please Him;
namely now since we are restored again in
hope by the passion of our Lord to the
dignity and bliss which we had lost by Adam’s
sin. And though we may never get it fully
here, yet we should desire that we may re-
cover here living a figure and a likeness of
the dignity, that our soul might be reformed
as it were in a shadow by grace to the image
of the Trinity, which we had by kind and
after shall have fully in bliss. For that is
the life which is very contemplative, to begin
here in that feeling of love and ghostly
knowing of God by opening of the ghostly
eye, which shall never be lost nor taken away,
but the same shall be fulfilled otherwise
in the bliss of heaven. ‘Thus behight our
Lord to Mary Magdalene which was con-
templative, and said thus of her: Mary hath
chosen the better part, that is the love of God
in contemplation, for zt shall never be taken

THE SCALE [Bk. I

away from her.* I say not that thou mayest
here living recover so whole nor so perfect
cleanness and innocence, knowing and loving
of God, as thou haddest first, nor as thou
shalt have; nor that thou mayest escape all
the wretchedness and pains of sin; nor that
thou, living in deadly flesh, mayest destroy
and quench all wholly the false vain love in
thyself, nor flee all venial sins so that they
will not—unless they be stopped by great
fervour of charity—spring out of thine heart
as water doth from a stinking well. But I
would that, if thou mayest not fully quench
it, thou mayest somewhat slake it and come
to the cleanness of soul as near as thou
mayest. For our Lord behight the children
of Israel when he led them into the land of
behest, and in figure of them all Christian
men thus: As much land as thou mayest tread
upon with thy foot of very desire, so much here
shalt thou have in the land of behest,t that
is in the bliss of heaven when thou comest
thither.
CHAPTER XLVI

HOW JESUS SHALL BE SOUGHT, DESIRED, AND
FOUND

SEEK then what thou hast lost, that thou

mayest find it. Well I wot, whoso might

once have an inward sight of a little of that
A TUKE 42, ee + Deut. xi 24.

Ch. 46] OF PERFECTION

dignity and ghostly fairness which a soul
had by kind and shall have by grace, he
should loathe and despise in his heart all the
bliss, the liking, and the fairness of this world “
as the stench of carrion; and he shall never
have will to do other deed night and day—
saving the frailty and the bare need of the
bodily kind—but desire, mourn and pray,
and seek how he may come again thereto.
Nevertheless, inasmuch as thou hast not yet
seen what it is fully, because thy ghostly eye
is not yet opened, I shall tell thee one word
for all in the which thou shalt seek, desire,
and find it; for in that one word is all
that thou hast lost. This word is Jesus. I
mean not this word Jesus painted upon the
wall, or written with letters on the book, or
formed with lips in sound of the mouth, or
feigned in thy heart by travail of thy mind;
for in this wise may a man out of charity find
Him. But I mean Jesus Christ that blessed
Person, God and Man, son of the Virgin
Mary, whom this name betokeneth; that is
all goodness, endless wisdom, love and sweet-
ness, thy joy, thy worship and thy everlasting
bliss, thy God, thy Lord and thy salvation.
Then if it be so, that thou feelest a great
desire in thine heart to Jesus, either by mind
of this name Jesus, or by mind and saying
of any other word, or in prayer or in any deed
that thou dost, which desire is so much that

THE! SCALE [Bk. 1

it putteth out, as it were, by strength all
other thoughts and desires of the world and
of the flesh, that they may not rest in thine
heart, then seekest thou well thy Lord Jesus.
And when thou feelest this desire to God,
to Jesus—all is one—holpen and comforted
by a ghostly might, insomuch that it is turned
into love and affection, ghostly savour and
sweetness, light and knowing of soothfast-
ness, so much that for the time thy thought

appear that time, but only is enclosed, rested,

found somewhat of Jesus; not Him as
He is, but a shadow of Him. For the
better that thou findest Him, the more shalt
thou desire Him. Then by what manner of
prayer or meditation or occupation that thou
mayest have greatest and cleanest desire to
Him and most feeling of Him, by that
occupation thou seekest Him best and best
findest Him. Therefore, if it come to thy
mind, as it were, to ask what thou hast lost
and what thou seekest, lift up thy mind and
the desire of thy heart to Jesus Christ, though
thou be blind and mayest see naught of His
godhead; and say that Him thou hast lost and
Him thou wouldest have, and nothing but
Him, to be with Him where He is; none other

joy, none other bliss in heaven nor in earth
but Him. And though it be so that thou
feel Him in devotion or in knowing, or in
any other gift whatever it be, rest not there
as though thou haddest fully found Jesus,
but forget that thou hast found and alway be
desiring after Jesus more and more to find
Him better, as though thou haddest right
naught found of Him. For wit thou well
that what thou feelest of Him, be it never
so much—yea, though thou wert ravished
into the third heaven with Paul—yet hast
thou not found Jesus as He is in His joy.
Know thou or feel thou never so much of
Him, He is yet above it. And, therefore,
if thou wilt fully find Him as He is in the
bliss of loving, cease never while thou livest
of ghostly desiring.

CHAPTER XLVII

WHAT PROFIT IT IS TO HAVE THE DESIRE OF
JESUS

SOOTHLY I had liefer feel and have a sooth-
fast desire and a clean love in mine heart to
my Lord Jesus Christ, though I see right
little of Him with my ghostly eye, than to
have without this desire all bodily presence
of all men living, all visions or revelations
of angels appearing, songs and soundings,

‘THE! SCALE [Bk. 1

savours and smells, burnings and any likings
or bodily feelings, and—shortly to say—all

David the prophet felt as I say, as I under-
stand, when he said thus: Lord what thing is
to me in heaven, or what would I without Thee
above earth?* As if he had said thus:
Lord. Jesus, what heavenly joy is liking to me

Thus coveted David when he said thus:
My soul coveted the desire of Thy righteousness
in every time.t Seek then, as David did,
desire by desire; and if thou mayest feel by
desire, in prayers and in meditations, the
homely presence of Jesus Christ in thy soul,
bind thine heart fast thereto, that it fall not
therefrom, and if thou stumble that thou
mayest find Him again.

CHAPTER XLIX

WHERE JESUS IS LOST AND FOUND AGAIN
THROUGH HIS MERCY

SEE now, therefore, the courtesy and the

mercy of Jesus. Thou hadst lost Him, but

where? Soothly in thy house, that is in

thy soul. That is to say, if thou hadst

lost all the reason of thy soul by the first
* Swink—.e., toil.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

sin, thy soul should never have found Him
again; but He left to thee thy reason, and so
He is in thy soul, and never shall be lost out
of it. Nevertheless, thou art never the nearer
to Him till thou hast found Him. He is
in thee, though He be lost from thee; but
thou art not in Him till thou hast found
Him. This then was His mercy, that He
would not suffer Him to be lost only where
He may be found. It needeth not to run
to Rome nor to Jerusalem to seek Him there,
` but turn thy thought into thine own soul
where He is hid, as the prophet saith: Soothly
Lord, thou art an hid God,* and seek Him
there. Thus He saith Himself in the gospel:
The kingdom of heaven ts likened to a treasure
hid in a field, which when a man findeth, for
joy of it he goeth and selleth all that he hath,
and buyeth the field.} Jesus is treasure hid
in thy soul; then if thou mightest find Him
in thy soul, and thy soul in Him, I am sure
for joy of it thou wouldest give the liking of
all earthly thing to have it. Jesus sleepeth
in thine heart ghostly, as He did sometime
bodily, when He was in the ship with His
disciples, but they for- dread of perishing
wakened Him, and soon after He saved
them from the tempest. Do thou so stir Him
by prayer and wake Him with great crying of
desire, and He shall soon rise and help thee.

CHAPTER L

WHAT LETTETH A MAN FROM HEARING AND
SEEING JESUS WITHIN HIMSELF

But nevertheless I hope better that thou
sleepest to Him more often than He doth to
thee; for He calleth thee full oft with His
sweet privy voice, and stirreth thine heart
full stilly, that thou shouldest leave all other
jangling of all other vanities in thy soul and
only take care of Him for to hear Him speak.
Thus saith David of our Lord: My daughter
hear and see and bow down thine ear to me,
and forget the folk of thy worldly thoughts
and the house of thy fleshly and kindly
affections.* Lo! here mayest thou see how
our Lord calleth thee and all other which
will hearken to Him. What letteth thee
then, that thou mayest neither see Him nor
hear Him? Soothly there is so much din
and crying in thine heart of vain thoughts
and fleshly desires that thou mayest neither
hear Him nor see Him. Therefore put
away unrestful din and break the love of sin
and of vanity, and bring in thine heart love
of virtues and full charity, and then shalt
thou hear thy Lord speak to thee.

CHAPTER LII

HOW A MAN SHALL SEE THE GROUND OF SIN
WITHIN HIMSELF

Now hast thou heard a little what thy soul
is, and what worship it had, and how thou
lost it; and also I have told thee that this
worship might by grace and busy travail
somewhat be recovered again in part of
feeling. Now shall I tell thee feebly as I
can how thou shalt be able to enter into
thyself to see the ground of sin, and to
destroy it as much as thou mayest, and so
shalt thou be able to recover a part of thy
dignity. Thou shalt cease for a time from
all bodily works, from all outward business
as thou mayest well. Then shalt thou draw
into thyself thy thought from thy bodily
wits, that thou take no heed what thou
hearest or seest or feelest; so that the point
of thy heart be not fixed on them. And after
this draw in nearer thy thought from all
* John xiii 34, 35.

THE! SCALE [Bk. 1

manner of imaginings, if thou mayest, of all
bodily things, and from all thoughts of thy
bodily deeds before done, or of other men’s
deeds. This is little mastery to do when
thou hast devotion; but thou shalt do it when
thou hast no devotion, for then it is right
much the harder. And set thine intent and
thy full purpose upon it, as if thou wouldest
not seek, nor feel, nor find aught but only
the grace and the ghostly presence of Jesus.
This is travaillous, for vain thoughts will
press thick into thine heart to draw thy
thought down to them; and if thou do
thus thou shalt find somewhat, not Jesus
whom thou seekest, but only a naked mind of
His name. Whatthen? Soothly thus thou
shalt find but a dark image and a painful of
thine own soul, which hath neither light
of knowing nor feeling of love nor liking.
This image, if thou behold it wittily,* is all
umbelappedf with black stinking clothes of

death. ‘This image and this black shadow
thou bearest about with thee wheresoever
thou goest. Out of this spring many great

CHAPTER LIII

UNTO WHAT THING THE IMAGE OF SIN IS LIKE,
AND WHAT IT IS..IN ITSELF

Now peradventure thou beginnest to think,
unto what thing this image should be like.
And therefore that thou shouldst not long
study thereabout, I tell thee that it is like
to no bodily thing. What is it then ? sayest
thou. Soothly it is naught, and that mayest
thou find if thou wilt assay as I have said to
thee. Draw into thyself thy thought from
all bodily things, and then shalt thou find
right naught wherein thy soul may rest.
This naught is naught else but darkness of
conscience, a lacking of love and of light;
as sin is naught but a wanting of God. If
it so were that the ground of sin were much
abated and dried up in thee, and thy soul

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

were reformed aright to the image of Jesus,
then, if thou drew into thyself thy heart,
thou shouldest not find naught, but thou
shouldest find Jesus; not only the naked
mind of His name, but Jesus Christ in con-

not reformed, therefore when thy soul cometh
in from all bodily things, and findeth naught

it an hundred winters till thou be out again
by some bodily delight or vain thought, and
that isno wonder. For whoso cometh home
unto his house, and findeth nothing therein
but stink and smoke and a chiding wife, he
would soon run out of it. Right so thy
soul, when it findeth no comfort in itself
but black smoke of ghostly blindness and
great chiding of fleshly thoughts crying upon
thee that thou mayest not be in peace,
soothly it is soon weary till it be out again.
This is the darkness of conscience.
