CHAPTER LIV

WHOSO WILL FIND JESUS, IF BEHOVETH HIM
ABIDINGLY TO TRAVAIL IN GHOSTLY DARK-
NESS AGAINST THE IMAGE OF SIN

NEVERTHELESS in this dark conscience it
behoveth thee to swink and sweat—that is
to say, to draw thy thought from all bodily
things as much as thou mayest. And then,
when thou findest right naught but sorrow
and pain and blindness in this darkness, if
thou wilt find Jesus it behoveth thee to suffer
the pain of this dark conscience and abide
awhile therein. And here it behoveth thee
to beware that thou take Jesus Christ into
thy thought against this darkness in thy
mind, and by busy prayer and fervent desire
to God, not setting the point of thy thought
in that naught beforesaid but in Jesus Christ
which thou desirest, think stiffly on the
passion and on His meekness, and through
might of Him thou shalt arise. Do as though
thou wouldest bear down and go through it.
Thou shalt dread and loathe this darkness and
this naught right as the devil, and thou shalt
despise it and burst through it, for within
this naught is Jesus hid in His joy, whom
thou mayest not find by thy seeking unless
thou pass the darkness of this conscience.
This is the ghostly travail that I speak of,
IOI

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

and this travail is the cause of all this writing,
to stir thee thereto if thou feel grace. This
darkness of conscience and this naught
that I speak of is the image of the first Adam.
Saint Paul knew it well, for he said thus of
it: As we have herebefore borne the image of
the earthly man, that is the first Adam, right
so that we might now bear the image of the
heavenly man,* which is Jesus, the second
Adam. He bare this image oft full heavy,
for it was so cumberous to him that he cried
out on it saying thus: O who shall deliver me
from this body and this image of death? And
then he comforteth himself and other also,
thus: The grace of God by Jesus Christ.

CHAPTER LV

WHAT IS PROPERLY THE IMAGE OF SIN, AND
WHAT COMETH OUT THEREOF

Now I have told thee a little of this image,
how it is naught. Nevertheless, if it be far
from thy knowing how it might be an image—
for naught is but naught, and so thou
mightest not lightly understand it—I shall
tell thee more openly of this image as me-
thinketh. This image is a false misruled
love unto thyself, and from this come all
manner of sins by seven rivers which are
* 1 Cor. xv 49. + Rom. vii 24, 25,

Ch. 55] OF PERFECTION

these: pride, envy, ire, accidie, covetousness,
gluttony, and lechery. Lo! this issomewhat
that thou mayest feel. By one of these rivers
runneth out all manner of sin and putteth
thee out of charity if it be deadly sin, or
letteth the fervour of charity if it be venial.
Now mayest thou grope* that this image is
not naught, but it is much of bad, for it is
a great spring of love unto thyself with such
seven rivers as I have said. But now sayest
thou: How may this be sooth? I have
forsaken the world and I am stokent in a
house, I meddle with no man, I chide not, I
strive not, I neither buy nor sell, I have no
worldly business, but by the mercy of God
I keep me chaste and withhold me from
delights. And besides this I pray, I wake,
I travail bodily and ghostly as I may. How
should then this image be so much in me as
thou speakest of? As to this I answer and
grant to thee that I hope thou dost all these
works and more thereto, and yet may it be
sooth as I say. Thou art busy upon thy
might to stop the rivers without, but the
spring within haply thou leavest whole.
Thou art like to a man which had in his
yard a stinking well with many runnings
from it. He went and stopped the runnings
and left the spring whole, and weened all
* Grope—i.e., feel. f Stoken—.e., enclosed.
+ Yard—z.e., garden,

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

had been safe. But the water sprang up at
the ground of the well and stood still, so
much that it corrupted all the fairness of his
garden, and yet ranthere no waterout. Right
so it may be with thee. If it beso that thou
hast by grace stopped the rivers of this image
without, so much is well: but beware of the
spring within. Soothly unless thou stop
and cleanse that as much as thou mayest,
it will corrupt all the flowers of the garden of
thy soul, show they never so fair outwardly
in sight of men. But now sayest thou:
Whereby shall I know that the ground is
stopped if I travail about it? As unto this
I shall tell by assay, how thou shalt know if
this image be in thee, and how much it is in
thee, and thereby shalt thou know how much
it is stopped and how little also in thee.
And inasmuch as pride is the principal
river, I shall tell thee thereof first.

CHAPTER LVI
WHAT PRIDE IS, AND WHEN IT IS SIN

PRIDE is naught else, as clerks say, but love

of thine own excellence, that is of thine own

worship. The more thou lovest and likest in

thine own worship, therefore, the more is the

pride, and so the more is this image in thee.

If thou feel in thine heart a stirring of pride,

Ch. 56] OF PERFECTION

that thou art holier, wiser, better, and more
virtuous than another is, that God hath given
thee grace to serve Him better than others
do, and thou thinkest all others beneath thee
and thee above them; or any other thought
of thyself which showeth to the sight of thy
soul an excellence and an overpassing of
other men or women, and of this stirring thou
feelest love and delight and vain pleasing in
thyself that thou art so: this is a token that
thou bearest this black image which, if it be
privy from men’s eyes, nevertheless it show-
eth openly in God’s sight. But now sayest
thou that thou mayest not flee such stirrings
of pride, for often thou feelest them against
thy will, and, therefore, thou holdest them
no sin; or if they be sin, they are but venial.
As to this I say thus, that the feeling of these
stirrings of pride or of any other which spring
out, either of the corruption of this foul
image or by incasting of the enemy, it is no
sin inasmuch as thou feelest them. And
that is a grace and privilege by virtue of the
passion of Jesus Christ granted to all Christian
men baptised in water and in the Holy Ghost;
for soothly to Jews and Saracens which trow
not in Jesus Christ all such stirrings are
deadly sins. For Saint Paul saith: AU that
is done without troth in Christ is deadly sin.*
But we Christian men have this privilege of
* Rom. xiv 23.
105 F

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

His mercy that such feelings are no sin, but
they are pain of original sin. Nevertheless,
when, by negligence and by blindness of
thyself, this feeling is received unawarely
in thy thought, and turned into love and
liking, then is there sin more or less after
the measure of the love, sometime venial
and sometime deadly. When it is venial
and when deadly, fully can I not tell thee,
nevertheless a little shall I say as methinketh.

CHAPTER LVII

WHEN PRIDE IS DEADLY SIN, AND HOW IT IS
IN FLESHLY LIVING MEN DEADLY SIN

WHEN the stirring of pride is received and
turned into liking, so much that the heart
chooseth it for a full rest and a full delight,
and seeketh none other end but only liking
therein, then is this pride deadly sin; for he
maketh and chooseth this delight as his god
without withstanding of reason and of will,
and, therefore, it is deadly sin. But now
sayest thou: What foolis he that would choose
pride as his god? No man that liveth would
do so. As unto this I say that I cannot tell
thee in special who sinneth in pride deadly,
but in general I shall tell thee. There are
two manners of pride, the one is bodily pride,
the other is ghostly pride. Bodily pride is of

Ch. 57] OF PERFECTION

fleshly living men, ghostly pride is of hypo-
crites and heretics. ‘These three sin deadly
in pride. I mean of such a fleshly living
man as Saint Paul speaketh of thus: If ye
live after your flesh, ye shall die.* Then say
I thus: that a worldly man which loveth
and seeketh principally the worship of
himself, and chooseth the liking of it as the
rest of his heart and the end of bliss, he
sinneth deadly. But now sayest thou: Who
would choose love of his worship instead of
God ? As to this I say thus; that he that
loveth his worship so as to seem better and
greater of estate than any other, and travail-
eth about it as much as he may, if he love it
so much that, for the getting of it, the keeping
and the saving of it, he breaketh the com-
mandment of God, or breaketh love and
charity to his even-Christian, or is ready and
in full will to break it rather than that he
should forbear his worship or lose it, either
of his name or of his state, or of fulfilling
of his will, soothly he sinneth deadly; for he
loveth his worship and chooseth it more
than the love of God and of his even-Chris-
tian. And yet, nevertheless, the man that
sinneth thus deadly, he would say with his
mouth that he would not choose pride for
his god; but he beguileth himself, for he
chooseth it by his deed. Nevertheless,
* Rom. viii 13.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

another worldly man that loveth worship of
himself and pursueth thereafter, if he love it
not so much that he would for the getting
or the saving of it do a deadly sin or break
charity to his even-Christian, he sinneth not
deadly but venially, more or less, after the
measure of his love and his liking, with other
circumstances.

CHAPTER LVIII
HOW PRIDE IS IN HERETICS DEADLY SIN

AN heretic sinneth deadly in pride, for he
chooseth his rest and his delight in his own
opinion and his own saying, for he weeneth
that it is sooth—which opinion is against
God and Holy Church—and therefore sin-
neth he in pride deadly, for he loveth himself
and his own will and wit so much that,
though it be openly against the ordinance
of Holy Church, he will not leave it, but
resteth him therein as in soothfastness, and
so maketh it his god; but he beguileth him-
self. For God and Holy Church are so oned
and accorded together that whoso doth
against one he doth against both. And,
therefore, he that saith he loveth God and
keepeth His biddings, and despiseth Holy
Church and setteth at naught the laws and
the ordinances of it made by the head and

Ch. 58] OF PERFECTION

the sovereign in governance of all Christian
men, he lieth. He chooseth not God, but
he chooseth the love of himself contrary to
the love of God, and so he sinneth deadly.
And in that he weeneth most to please God,
he most displeaseth Him; for he is blind
and will not see. Of this blindness and this
false resting of an heretic in his own feeling,
speaketh the Wise Man thus: There is a way
which seemeth to a man rightful, and the last
end of it bringeth him to endless death.* ‘This
way specially is called heresy, for other
fleshly sinners, that sin deadly and lie therein,
commonly suppose amiss of themselves, and
feel biting in conscience that they go not in
the right way. But.an heretic supposeth
that he doth well and teacheth well, and no
other man so well; and so weeneth he that
his way were the right way, and therefore
feeleth he no biting of conscience nor meek-
ness in heart. And soothly unless God
send him meekness of His mercy, at the last
end he goeth to hell; and nevertheless yet
weeneth he to have done well and get him
the bliss of heaven for his teaching.

CHAPTER LIX
HOW PRIDE IS IN HYPOCRITES DEADLY SIN

THE hypocrite also sinneth deadly in pride.
He is an hypocrite that chooseth vain joy of
himself as the rest and the full delight of
his heart, upon this wise. When a man
doth many manner of good deeds bodily
and ghostly, and there is put into his mind
by suggestion of the enemy a beholding of
himself and his good deeds, how good, how
holy he is, how worthy in men’s doom, and
how high in God’s sight above other men, he
perceiveth this stirring and receiveth it
wilfully, for he weeneth it good and of God,
inasmuch as it is sooth, for he doeth all these
good deeds better than other men. And
when it is received thus by assent of his will
as good, there riseth from it in his heart a
love and a delight of himself that he hath so
much grace, so that it ravisheth his mind
out of all other thoughts both ghostly and
fleshly for the time, and setteth it in vain joy
of himself as in a rest of his heart. This
ravishing in ghostly pride is delectable, and
therefore he keepeth it, holdeth it, and
nourisheth it, as much as he may; for this
love and vain delight he prayeth and waketh,
he fasteth and weareth the hair, and doth
other afflictions; and all these things grieve
IIO

Chiss9] '’ OF PERFECTION

him but little. He praiseth, he thanketh
God sometimes with his mouth, and some-
times wringeth a tear out of his eye, and then
he thinketh all safe enough. But soothly
all this is for love of himself, which he
chooseth and receiveth as it were love and
joy in God; and in that is all the sin. He
chooseth not sin wilfully as for sin, but he
chooseth this delight and joy that he feeleth
for his God, as the rest of his soul (the which
is sin) without displeasing or withstanding
of will. For he weeneth it were a joy in God
‘and it is not so, and therefore sinneth he
deadly. Job saith thus of an hypocrite:
The joy of an hypocrite is no more than a point,
for if he sty into heaven with raising of his
heart, and his head touch the skies, at the last
end he shall be cast out as a dung-heap.* ‘The
joy of an hypocrite is but a point; for if
he worship himself never so much, and joy
in himself never so much all his lifetime, and
depict himself with all his good deeds in
sight and in praising of the world, at the last
it is right naught but sorrow and pain. But
now sayest thou, there are few such or else
none that are so blind that they would hold
and choose vain joy in themselves as joy in
God. As unto this I cannot say, nor will
not if I could, but one thing I tell thee, that
there be many hypocrites, and nevertheless

CHAPTER LX

HOW STIRRINGS OF PRIDE AND VAINGLORY IN
GOOD MEN BE BUT VENIAL SINS

NEVERTHELESS a man or woman which dis-
poseth him to live contemplative, if it be so
that he forsaketh himself in will, and offer
him wholly to God with a full general will
that he would not sin in pride wittingly, nor
have joy in himself wittingly, but only in
God if he could and might, and after this
full will offered to God he feeleth many
stirrings of vainglory and delighteth in them
for the time, for he perceiveth them not; this
liking is but venial sin, and especially if it
be so that, when he cometh to himself, he
reproveth himself and withstandeth this
stirring with displeasing of will, and asketh
mercy and help of God. Then the liking

Ch. 60] OF PERFECTION

which before was sin our Lord of His mercy
soon forgiveth; and yet he shall have meed
for his good travail in the withstanding of
it. And that is a courtesy of our Lord
granted to all those which are specially His
servants and more homely of His court, as

what man or woman soever, in what degree

his soul forsake all the worship and likings

his might, by devout prayers and holy

And for this good will and good purpose, that

that will which they have generally set in
their heart before to please God and to for-
sake all manner of sin, if they knew it, keepeth
them here in such stirrings, and in all other
that come of frailty, that they sin not deadly,
and shall keep them as long as the ground of
the will is kept whole.

Ch. 61] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER LXI

HOW DIVERS STATES IN HOLY CHURCH SHALL
HAVE DIVERS MEEDS IN HEAVEN; AND OF
TWO SPECIAL MEEDS IN HEAVEN

AND over this I say more, in comfort of thee
and all other having the state of anker en-
closed, and also by the grace of God in com-
fort of them all that enter any religion
approved by Holy Church, that all those who
by the mercy of our Lord shall be saved, shall
have a special meed and a singular worship
in the bliss of heaven for their state of living,
before other souls that had not that state in
Holy Church, though they be never so holy.
Which worship is better than all the worship
of this world without comparison, for if
thou mightest see what it were, thou wouldest
not for all the worship of this world (though
thou mightest have it without sin) change
thy state either of anker or of religious, nor
lose that singular meed in the bliss of heaven;
which meed is called accidental meed.
Nevertheless that other men mistake not
this that I say, therefore I shall say it more
openly. Thou shalt understand that there
are two meeds in the bliss of heaven, which
our Lord giveth to chosen souls. The one
is sovereign and principal, as is loving and
knowing of Him after the measure of charity
IIS

THE SCALE [Bk.. 1

given of God to a soul living in deadly flesh.
This meed is best and sovereign, for it is
God Himself, and it is common to all the
souls that shall be saved, in whatever state
or degree they be living in Holy Church, more
or less, after the quantity of their charity in
this life. For he that most loveth God in
charity, he shall have most meed in the bliss
of heaven, for he shall most love God and
know Him; and that is the sovereign meed.
And as for this meed it shall fall that some
manner of man or woman, as a lord or a
lady, knight or squire, merchant or plough-
man, or what degree he be in, man or woman,
shall have more meed than some priest or
friar, monk or canon or anker enclosed. And |
why? Soothly because he loved God more
in charity. And other meed there is, that
is secondary; which our Lord giveth for
special good deeds that a man doth wilfully
over that he is bounden to. Of three works
principal do the doctors of Holy Church
make mind—of martyrdom, preaching and
maidenhood. These three works as for an
excellence, inasmuch as they pass all others,
shall have a special meed which they call
aureole. And that is naught else but a
singular worship and special token ordained
of God in regard of that special deed, before
other men that did not so, over the sovereign
meed of love of God which is common to

Ch. 61] OF PERFECTION

them and to all others. Right so it is of all
other special good deeds, the which, if they
be done soothfastly, are specially acceptable
to the sight of God, and in the doom of
Holy Church they are excellent, as are en-
closing of ankers done by the authority of
Holy Church, also entering into any religion
approved; and the stricter that the religion is,
the more excellent is the deed in the doom of
Holy Church. And also, after and beneath
these, the taking of the order of priest, either
for cure of men’s souls, or to minister the
sacraments of Holy Church, or else for
singular devotion to please God and profit
their even-Christians by the sacrifice of the
precious body of our Lord Jesus Christ.
Soothly they are special deeds and excellent,
openly showed in the doom of Holy Church
and in the sight of our Lord. When they
are done soothfastly for God they are excel-
lent, and they shall have special meed, each
man in his degree, in the bliss of heaven.
The state of bishop and prelate is above all
these deeds, as for this accidental meed.
That this is sooth it seemeth by Holy Writ,
where it saith thus, in the prophet Daniel.
The angel, when he had showed to Daniel
the privities of God, said to him thus: Go
- thou to the rest of thy bodily death, and thou
shalt stand in thy sort*® as a prophet at the last
* Sort—i.e., lot, destiny.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

day.* And soothly, as Daniel shall stand
as a prophet at the last day of doom and have
the worship and the excellence of a prophet
over the sovereign blessed meed of love
and sight of God, right so shalt thou stand
in thy sort as an anker in that sort, and a
religious in the sort of religion, and so of
‘other excellent deeds, and have a singular
worship passing other men at the day of

CHAPTER LXII

A SHORT STIRRING TO MEEKNESS AND TO
CHARITY

Now by these words thou wilt, if thou wilt
trow them, conceive comfort for thy degree
of living, and also matter of meekness. For
though it be so that thou shalt have so much
meed specially for thy state of living, if thou
be saved; nevertheless, it may be that there
is many a wife and many a worldly woman
shall be nearer to God than thou, and more
shall love God and better know Him than
thou shalt for all thy state. And that ought
to be a shame for thee, unless thou be busy
to get love and charity as fully and as per-
fectly as a worldly man or woman. For if
thou mayest have as much charity of the
gift of God as he or she hath that dwelleth
* Dan. xii 13.

Ch. 63] OF PERFECTION

still in worldly business, thou shalt have as
much of the sovereign meed as he shall.
And thou shalt over that, for that state which
thou hast taken, have a singular meed and a
worship which he shall not have. If thou
wilt do well, therefore, meek thyself and forget
thy state as it were right naught, for it is
sooth, by itself it is right naught; and let
thy desire and thy business be to destroy
sins and to get charity and meekness and
other ghostly virtues, for therein lieth all.

CHAPTER LXIII

HOW A MAN SHALL KNOW HOW MUCH PRIDE
IS IN HIM

I HAVE nigh forgotten this image; but now I
turn again thereto. If thou wilt wit how
much pride is therein, thou mayest yet assay
thyself thus. Look now wisely and flatter
not thyself, if loving, praising, worshipping
or fleshly favour of worldly men or of other
be liking to thine heart and turn it into vain
gladness and well paying* of thyself, thinking
stilly in thine heart that men should praise
thy life, or regard thy speech more than that
of others; and also on contrary wise if it be
so that men reprove thee and set thee at
naught, holding thee but a fool or an hypo-
* Well paying—.e., satisfaction.

IIQ

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

crite, or if they slander thee or speak evil of
thee falsely, or in any other way dis-ease thee
unskilfully, and for this thou feelest in thine
heart a grievous heaviness against them and a
great rising in thine heart with withstanding
to suffer any shame or villainy in the sight of
the world; if it be so with thee, it is a token
that there is much pride in this dark image,
seem thou never so holy in the sight of men.
For though these stirrings be naught but
little and venial, nevertheless they show well
that there is much pride hid in the ground
of thine heart, as the fox lurketh in his den.
These stirrings with many more spring out
of this image, so much that thou mayest
scarcely do any good deed but it shall be
mingled with some pride or vain delight in
thyself, and so with thy pride thou defoulest
all thy good deeds and makest them loath-
some in the sight of thy Lord. I say not
that they are lost because they are mingled
with this pride, but I say that they are not
so pleasant to thy Lord as they should be if
they were simple and truly rooted in the
virtue of meekness. And therefore, if thou
wilt have cleanness of heart to come to the
love of God, it behoveth thee not only to
flee the rest of thine heart in vainglory by
wilful assenting to pride, and also the reckless
liking therein of thy frailty if it be against
thy will, but also thou shalt flee the feeling

Ch. 64] OF PERFECTION

of pride and shalt eschew it as much as thou
mayest. But that thou mayest not do,
unless thou be full quick and ready about
T keeping of thine heart, as I shall tell thee
after.

CHAPTER LXIV

OF ENVY AND OF IRE AND OF THEIR BRANCHES;
AND HOW, INSTEAD OF SIN, MAN’S PERSON
IS OFTEN HATED

TURN this image upside down and look well
therein, and thou shalt find two members
of envy and ire fastened thereto, with many
divers branches springing out of them, the
which do let the love and charity which thou
shouldest have to thy even-Christians. The
branches of ire and envy are these: hatred,
evil suspicion, false and unskilful deeming,
melancholy rising of heart against them,
despising and unkindness, backbiting and
mis-saying, unskilful blaming, misliking
and heaviness against them that despise thee
_ or speak any evil of thee or against thee, a

gladness of their dis-ease, a felness* against
sinful men and others that will not do as
thou thinkest they should do, with great
desire of thine heart (under colour of charity
and righteousness) that they were well
punished and chastised for their sin. ‘This

* Felness—i.e., fury.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

stirring seemeth good, nevertheless, if thou
ransack it well, thou shalt find it sometimes
more fleshly against the person than ghostly
against the sin. Thou shalt love the man,
be he never so sinful, and thou shalt hate

are beguiled in this, for they set the bitter
instead of the sweet, and take darkness

Woe be to them that say good 1s evil and evil

when they should hate the sin of their even-
Christian and love the person, hate the
person instead of the sin, and ween that
they hate the sin. Wherefore it is a craft
by itself, whoso could do it well.

CHAPTER LXV

THAT IT IS MASTERY TO LOVE MEN’S PERSONS
AND WISELY HATE THEIR SINS

IT is no mastery to wake and fast till thine
head ache, nor to run to Rome and to Jeru-
salem upon thy bare feet, nor to start about
and preach as if thou wouldst turn all men
by thy preaching; nor is it any mastery to
make churches or chapels, to feed poor men
and make hospitals. But it is a great mastery
* Isa. v 20.

Ch. 65] OF PERFECTION

for a man to love his even-Christian in
charity, and wisely to hate the sin of him
and love the man. For though it be so that
all these deeds beforesaid are good in them-
selves, nevertheless they are common to good
men and to bad, for each man might do them
if he would and had whereof: and for thee to
do what each man may do, I hold it no
mastery. But to love his even-Christian
in charity and hate his sin, no man may do
but good men only, which have it of the
gift of God and not of their travail, as Saint
Paul saith: Love and charity is shed and spread
in your hearts by the Holy Ghost which is
given to you,* and, therefore, it is the more
precious and the more dainty to come by.
All other good deeds without this make not
a man good nor worthy the bliss of heaven,
but this alone and only this maketh a man
good and all his good deeds meedful. All
other gifts of God and works of man are
common to good and to bad, to chosen and
to reproved,+ but this gift of charity is only
of good and of chosen souls.

* Rom. v §. + Dainty—.e., difficult.
+ Reproved—.e., reprobate.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

CHAPTER LXVI

THAT FOR THE SAME DEEDS DIVERS MEN SHALL
HAVE DIVERS MEEDS

A GooD man for the love of God fasteth,
waketh, goeth on pilgrimage, and forsaketh
all the likings of the world soothfastly in his
heart without feigning. He shall have his
meed in the bliss of heaven. And an hypo-
crite for vainglory of himself doth the same
deeds and receiveth his meed here. Also a
very preacher of God’s word, fulfilled of
charity and of meekness, sent of God and of
Holy Church received, if he preach and teach
God’s word, he shall have a special meed of
God, that is the aureole, for his preaching.
An hypocrite or an heretic that have no
meekness nor charity, nor are sent of God
nor yet of Holy Church, if they preach they
have their meed here. Also a good man in
worldly state, who for love of God maketh
many churches, chapels, abbeys, hospitals,
and doth many other good deeds of mercy,
he shall have his meed in the bliss of heaven;
not for the deed in itself, but for the goodwill
and the charity that he hath of the gift of
God to do these good deeds. Another man,
for vanity of himself and worship and pleasing
of the world and for his own name, doth the
same good deeds and hath his meed here.

Ch. 67] OF PERFECTION

The cause is, in all these, that the one hath
charity and the other none. Which is the
one and which is the other, our Lord know-
eth and none but He.

CHAPTER LXVII

THAT ALL MEN’S GOOD DEEDS SHALL BE
APPROVED THAT HAVE THE LIKENESS OF
GOOD, SAVE THE OPEN HERETIC AND THE
CURSED MAN

AND therefore we should love and worship
all men in our hearts, and approve, examine,
and receive all their deeds that have the
likeness of goodness, though the doers in
God’s sight are bad, save of the open heretic
and of the open cursed man. Of these two
specially shall we flee and eschew the pre-
sence and the communing with them, and
then we shall reprove and refuse their deeds,
seem they never so good, as long as they are
rebels to God and Holy Church. As if a
worldly cursed man make a church or feed
poor men, thou mayest surely hold it naught
and deem it as itis. Also if an open heretic
which is rebel to Holy Church preach and
teach, though he convert a hundred thousand
souls, hold the deed as to himself right
naught. For these men are openly out of
charity, without which all is naught that a
man doth.

CHAPTER LXVIII

THAT NO GOOD DEED MAY MAKE MEN SAFE
WITHOUT CHARITY, AND THAT THEY
ONLY THAT ARE MEEK FEEL CHARITY

AND, therefore, it is a great mastery for a
man to cun* to love his even-Christian in
charity. All this saying may be openly
proved by Saint Paul’s words thus, in praising
of charity: If I speak the language of all men
and of angels also, if I have no charity I am
right naught. And if I have so great faith
that I may turn hills and bear them away, and
I have no charity, I am right naught. And
also though I had all manner of knowing of
all privities without charity, I am right naught.
And if I give all that I have to poor men and
my body to the fire to be burnt, and I have no
charity, it profiteth me right naught.t Here
it seemeth by Saint Paul’s words that a man
may do all good deeds bodily without charity,
and that charity is naught else but to love
God and his even-Christian as himself. How
should then any wretched caitiff living in
earth, what that he be, have delight or trust
or security in himself for aught that he can
or may do with all his bodily mights or his
kindly reason, since all this is naught worth
x Cun—i.e., know, ken, can.
T 1 Cor. xiii 1-3.

Ch. 68] OF PERFECTION

without love and charity to his even-Chris-
tian? And this charity may not be gotten
with working of himself, for it is a free gift
of God sent into a meek soul, as Saint Paul
saith. Who then dare hardily say, ‘‘ I have
charity,” or “I am in charity”? Soothly
no man may say it surely but he that is
perfectly and soothfastly meek. Other men
may trow of themselves and hope that they
may be in charity by tokens; but he that is
perfectly meek feeleth it, and therefore
might he soothly say it. ‘Thus meek was
Saint Paul, and for this said he thus of
himself: Who shall depart me from the charity
of God? Tribulation or anguish? And he
answereth himself and saith: There shall no
creature put me from the charity of God which
I have in Christ Fesus.* Many men do
deeds of charity and have no charity as I
have said. For to reprove a sinner for his
sin to his amending and in convenable time,
it is a deed of charity, but to hate the sinner
instead of the sin, it is against charity. He
that is verily meek can depart the one from
the other, and no man but he. For though
a man had all moral virtues of all philosophers
he could not do this. He should be able to
hate the sin in all other men, for he hateth
it in himself, but he could not love the man
in charity for all his philosophy. Also if a
* Rom. viii 35.
P27

THE SCALE [Bk. I

man had knowing of all clergy and of divinity

within our travail; and on the contrary wise
I say that there is no gift of God that may so
lightly be had as charity, for our Lord giveth
no gift so freely nor so gladly nor so commonly
as He doth charity. How shalt thou then
have it, sayest thou? Be meek and low in
spirit and thou shalt have it; and what is
lighter for thee to do than to be meek?
Soothly nothing. Then seemeth it that there
is nothing that so lightly may be had as
charity, and, therefore, thou needest not be
much adread. Be meek and have it. Thus
saith Saint James the apostle: Our Lord
withstandeth proud men, but to meek men He
giveth grace,* which grace is properly His
charity, for after the measure of thy meekness
so shalt thou have charity. If thou have
* Jast ivit6.

Ch. 69] OF PERFECTION

meekness imperfectly, in will only not in

But if thou have meekness perfectly, then

best. The other it behoveth that we needs
must have if we will be saved, and this
should we desire. Then if thou ask me who
is perfectly meek, thou shalt no more have
of me at this time but this. He is meek that
soothfastly knoweth himself as he is.

CHAPTER CXIX

HOW A MAN SHALL WIT HOW MUCH WRATH
AND ENVY IS HID IN THE GROUND OF
HIS HEART

Now turn yet again to this image if thou
wilt assay how much ire and envy is hid in
thine heart that thou feelest not. Look
well and behold thyself wisely, when such
stirrings of ire and envy against thine even-
Christian spring out of thine heart. The
more stirred that thou art by melancholy,
bitter, or wicked will against him, the more
is this image in thee; for the more thou
murmurest by impatience either against God
* Ps, cxxxviii 16.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

for any tribulation or sickness or other bodily
dis-ease sent of God, or against thine even-
Christian for aught that he doth against
thee, the less is the image of Jesus reformed
in thee. I say not that such murmurings of
fleshly angriness are deadly sins; but I say
that they let cleanness of heart and peace of
conscience, that thou mayest not have full
charity, by which thou shouldest come to
life contemplative. For that end is the
purpose of all my saying, that thou shouldst
not only cleanse thine heart from deadly
sins, but also from venial, as much as thou
mightest, and that the ground of sin might
by grace of Jesus Christ somewhat be
slackened in thee.

CHAPTER LXX

BY WHAT TOKENS THOU SHALT KNOW IF THOU

SAMPLES THOU SHALT TAKE OF CHRIST
TO LOVE HIM

For though it be so that thou feelest no evil
against thine even-Christian for a time, yet
art thou not sure that the ground of ire is
quenched in thee, nor yet art thou lord of the
virtue of charity. For suffer him to touch
thee a little by an angry or a shrewd word,
and thou shalt feel anon if thine heart be yet

Ch. 70] OF PERFECTION

made whole by fulness of charity. The
more thou art stirred and evil willed against
the person, the further art thou from charity.
And if thou be naught stirred against the
person, neither by angry cheer* outward
nor by privy hate in thine heart, to despise
and deem him or set him at naught, but the
more shame and villainy he doth to thee in
word or in deed, the more pity and com-

him—for love is so good in itself—but pray
for him and help him and desire his amending,
not only with the mouth as hypocrites can
do, but with affection of love in thine heart,
then hast thou perfect charity to thine even-
Christian. ‘This charity had Saint Stephen
perfectly when he prayed for them that
stoned him to death. This charity coun-
selled Christ to those that would be His
perfect followers when He said thus: Love
your enemies and do good to them that hate
you, pray for those that pursue you.t And
therefore if thou wilt follow Christ be like
to Him in this craft. Learn to love thine
enemies and sinful men, for all these are
thine even-Christians.. Look and bethink
thee how Christ loved Judas, which was

for God. In God thou lovest thyself when

lovest not thyself except only for that

thee. Then lovest thou thyself in God,

rightful, thou shalt love them by charity in

for then lovest thou God in them, as good-

evidence that they be not in grace. Yet
shalt thou love them; not as they are, nor as
good men and rightful, for they are bad and
unrightful, but thou shalt love them for
God, that they might be good and rightful.
And so shalt thou nothing hate in them, but
that thing that is contrary to righteousness,
and that is sin. This is, as I understand,
the teaching of Saint Austin, to separate the
love of the man from the hate of his sin, for
the love of thine even-Christians. He that
is meek, or would soothfastly be meek, can
love his even-Christian, and none but he.

CHAPTER LXXI

HOW A MAN SHALL KNOW HOW MUCH COVET-
OUSNESS IS HID IN HIS HEART

LIFT up well this image and look well about,
and thou shalt be able to see covetousness
and love of earthly things occupy a great
part of this image, though it seem little.
Thou hast forsaken riches and much savour
of this world, and art shut up as ina dungeon,
but hast thou forsaken cleanly the love of
all this? Itrownot yet. It is less mastery
to forsake worldly good than to forsake the
love of it. Peradventure thou hast not
forsaken thy covetousness, but thou hast
changed it from great things into small, as
from a pound into a penny, and from a silver
piece into the disc of a halfpenny. ‘This is
a small change; thou art no good merchant.
These examples are childish, nevertheless
they betoken more. If thou believe me not,
assay thyself. If thou have love and delight
in the having and holding of anything that
thou hast, such as it is, with which love thou
feedest thine heart for a time; or if thou have
desire and yearning to have something that
thou hast not, with which desire thine heart
is travailed and troubled with unskilful
business, so that the clean desire of virtue
and of God may not rest therein, this is a

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

token that there yet is covetousness in this
image. And if thou wilt assay better, look
if anything that thou hast be taken away from
thee, by mastery, or by borrowing, or by any
other wise, so that thou mayest not get it
again, and for this thou art dis-eased, angered
and troubled in thine heart, both because
thou wantest that thing that thou wouldest
have and mayest not have it, and also thou
art stirred against him that hath it, to strive
and chide with him because he might restore
it again and will not, that is a token that thou
lovest worldly goods. For thus do worldly
men. When their goods and their riches are
taken from them, they are heavy, sorrowful,
angry and chiding, and stirring against
them that have it, openly by word and by
deed; but thou dost all this in thine heart
privily where God seeth, and yet thou art
in more default than a worldly man. For
you have forsaken in likeness the love of
worldly things, but a worldly man hath not,
and therefore he is excused though he strive
and pursue for his goods by lawful ways,
to have them again. But now sayest thou
that it behoveth thee to have thy necessaries
of such things as belong to thee, as well as a
worldly man. I grant thee well thereto,
but thou shouldest not love it for itself, nor
have liking in the holding nor in the keeping,
nor feel sorrow or heaviness in the losing or

Ch. 71] OF PERFECTION

in the withdrawing of it, for, as Saint Gregory
saith, as much sorrow as thou hast in the
losing of a thing, so much love hadst thou in
the keeping. And therefore, if thine heart
were made whole and thou hadst sooth-
fastly felt desire of ghostly things, and had
there withall a sight of the least ghostly thing
that is, thou shouldest set at naught all the
love and the liking of any earthly thing, and
it should not cleave upon thee. For to love
and to have more than thou needest skil-
fully, but only for lust and liking, it is a great
default. Also to set thy love upon anything
that thou needest, for itself, is a default, but
not so great. But to have and use that thing
that thou needest, without love of it more
than kind or need asketh—without which
the thing may not be used—it is no default.
Soothly in this point, as I trow, many that
have the state and the likeness of. poverty
are much letted and hindered from the love
of God. I accuse no man, and reprove not
any state, for in each estate some are good
and some are other. But one thing I say
to every man or woman that hath taken
the state of wilful poverty, whether he be
religious or secular, or what degree he be in:
as long as his affection is bound and fastened,
and, as it were, glued with love of any earthly
thing that he hath or would have, he may
not have nor feel soothfastly the clean love

137 G

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

and the clear sight of ghostly things. For
Saint Austin saith to our Lord thus: Lord,
he loveth Thee but little, that loveth anything
with Thee that he loveth not for Thee. For
the more love and covetousness of any earthly _
thing there is with thee, the less is the love
of God in thy heart. For though it be so
that this love of earthly things putteth not
men out of charity—unless it be so much that
it strangle the love of God and of their even-
Christian—soothly it hindereth them and
letteth them from the fervour of charity,
and also from that special meed which they
should have in the bliss of heaven for perfect
poverty; and that is a great loss if they might |
see it. For whoso might know ghostly meed,
how good, how precious, and how worthy
it is—for it is everlasting—he would not
for the love of all earthly joy or for savour
of any earthly thing, though he might have
it without sin, let or lessen the least meed of
the bliss of heaven which he might have if
he would. I speak further than I do, but I
pray thee do thus as I say by the grace of
God, if thou mayest, or any other man whoso
will; for that were a comfort to mine heart,
that, if I may not have it in myself as I say,
I might have it in thee, or in any other
creature which hath received more plenty of
His grace than I. But see now then, since
covetousness in the naked ground letteth a

Chie] OF PERFECTION

man or woman so much from the ghostly
feeling of the love of God, how much more
then it letteth and cumbereth worldly men
and women which, by all their wits and
bodily business night and day, study and
travail how they may get riches and plenty
of worldly good. They can have no delight
but in worldly things, nor will they, for they
see it not. I say no more of them at this
time, for in this writing I speak not to them;
but this I say, if they might see and would
see what they do, they would not do so.

CHAPTER LXXII

HOW A MAN SHALL KNOW WHEN HE SINNETH
NOT IN EATING AND DRINKING, AND
WHEN HE SINNETH VENIALLY, AND WHEN
DEADLY

YET mayest thou see more in this image
though it be dark; and that is fleshly love to
thyself, in gluttony, accidie, and lechery.
These fleshly likings make a man full beastly
and far from the inward savour of the love
of God, and from the clear sight of ghostly
things. But now sayest thou, since it be-
hoveth thee of necessity to eat and drink and
sleep, and that mayest thou not do without
liking, therefore thou thinkest that this
liking is no sin. Unto this I say that if in

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

eating and drinking and other needful things
thou keep to the bodily measure of thy need,
and receivest no more liking than nature
asketh, and dost all this for ghostly delight
which thou feelest in thy soul, I grant thee
forsooth that thou then sinnest right naught -
therein, for then canst thou eat and sleep
well. Soothly and without doubt I am full
far from that knowing and further from the
working; for to eat I have by nature, but to
ken how I should eat I may not but by the
grace of God. Saint Paul had by grace this
cunning as he saith himself thus: I am
informed and kenned in all things, for I can
hunger and I can eat, I can with plenty and I
can with poverty. I may do all in Him that
strengtheneth me.* Saint Austin said to our
Lord thus: Lord, thou hast teached me that
I should take meat as a medicine. Hunger is
a sickness of nature and meat is a medicine
thereto, therefore the liking that cometh
withal, inasmuch as it is natural and needful,
it is no sin, but when it passeth into lust
and into wilful liking, then it is sin. And
therefore there lieth all the mastery to have
cunning to dispart wisely thy need from lust
and wilful liking. They are so knit together,
and the one cometh with the other, so that
it is hard to receive that one as thou needest
and reprove that other as wilful lust, the
* Phil. 1v 12, 13:

Ch. 72] OF PERFECTION

which often cometh under colour of need.
Nevertheless since it is so that need is the
ground of this, and that need is no sin—for
be a man never so holy it behoveth him to eat
and drink and sleep—therefore the lust and
the liking that cometh under colour of this
need and surpasseth this need is the less sin.
For commonly a man sinneth not deadly in
gluttony unless he be encumbered with other
deadly sins before done; then may he the
lightlier sin deadly in this. For it is sooth
he that chooseth lust and liking of his flesh,
and delighteth in welfare of meat or drink
as full rest of his heart—so that he would
never have other joy nor other bliss but live
ever in such lust of his flesh if he might—it
is no doubt that he sinneth deadly, for he
loveth his flesh more than God. But he that
liveth in deadly sin of pride, of envy, or such
other, he is so blinded by the devil that for
the time he hath no power of his free will;
and therefore he may not well withstand
fleshly likings when they come, but falleth
down wilfully to them, as a beast doth to
carrion. And inasmuch as he hath no
general will before to God (principally be-
cause he is in deadly sin), therefore the lust
of gluttony, the which he falleth into lightly,
is to him deadly sin, for he maketh no with-
standing general nor special. But another
man or woman which is in grace or charity

THE SCALE [Bk. I

and hath always a good general will to God
in his soul, whether he sleep or wake, eat or
drink, or what good deed soever he doth (so
that it be not evil in itself), by that will and
desire he chooseth God above all things, and

hath liefer forbear all things of the world than `
offend his God, because of his love of Him.
This will, though it be but general, is of so
great virtue by the grace of our Lord Jesus,
that if such a one fall by frailty in lust, or in
liking of meat and of drink, or of such other
sickness, either by excess of eating too much,
or too often, or too greedily, too lustily or
too delicately, too soon or in time unseason-
able, it saveth him and keepeth him from
deadly sin. And this is sooth, as long as he
is in charity by other deeds, and keepeth
his general will to all that he doth, and namely
if he know how to admonish his own wretched-
ness and cry after mercy, and be in purpose
specially to withstand such fleshly lusts. For
our Lord is good and merciful, and these
venial sins of gluttony He forgiveth right soon
unto a meek soul; for the stirring and the
liking of gluttony—inasmuch as they are
hardest to flee because of the need of our
bodily nature—among all other sins are most
excusable and least perilous. And there-
fore thou shalt not rise against the ground of
this sin as thou shalt against all other sins,
for the ground of this sin is only need, which

Ch.' 73] OF PERFECTION

may not be escaped, unless thou wilt do
worse and slay the need as many fools do
which should slay the thief and spare the true
man. That is to say they should slay the
unskilful lust and the wilful liking, and spare
and keep the bodily nature, but they do not
so. But against all other sins thou shalt
arise to destroy; not only deadly sins and the
great venials, but also the ground of them as
much as thou mayest. See by this skill,
thou mayest not live without meat and drink,
but thou mayest live without lechery if thou
wilt, and be ever the better therefor. And
therefore thou shalt not only flee the deed
of it, which is a deadly sin, but also wilful
liking of it in thine heart without deed, which
is venial sin (and sometimes it is deadly),
but also thou shalt travail against the ground
of it to destroy the feeling and the rising of
fleshly stirring.

CHAPTER LXXIII

HOW THE GROUND OF LECHERY SHOULD BE
DESTROYED WITH GHOSTLY TRAVAIL AND
NOT WITH BODILY

But this travail against the ground of lechery
shall be ghostly, as by prayers and ghostly
virtues, and not by bodily penance. For
wit thou well that if thou fast and wake and
scourge thyself, and do all that thou canst,

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

thou shalt never have cleanness and chastity
without the gift of God and the grace of
meekness. Thou shouldest sooner slay thy-
self than thou shouldest slay fleshly stirrings
and feelings of lust and lechery, either in
thine heart or in thy flesh, by any bodily
penance. But by the grace of Jesus in a
meek soul the ground may be much stopped
and destroyed, and the spring may be much
dried, and that is very chastity in body and
in soul. On the same manner may be said
of pride and of covetousness and of such
other, for thou mightest live if thou wert
not proud nor covetous; and therefore
shalt thou destroy all the feelings of them as
much as thou mayest. But in gluttony thou
shalt rise and smite away the unskilful
stirrings and save the ground whole.

CHAPTER LXXIV

THAT A MAN SHOULD BE BUSY TO PUT AWAY
ALL STIRRINGS OF SIN; BUT MORE BUSY
OF GHOSTLY SINS THAN OF BODILY

AND therefore he that riseth against the
feeling of fleshly liking in meat and drink,
more fully and more sharply than of pride or
covetousness, which because they seem fair
are not lightly reproved, or of envy or of
lechery, I say that he is half blind, for he

Ch. 74] OF PERFECTION

seeth not yet ghostly uncleanness, as of pride
and envy, how foul it is in God’s sight. I
hope if a man might see with his eye ghostly
how foul pride and covetousness are in the
sight of God, and how contrary to Him, he
should more loathe a stirring of pride and the
vain liking of it, and also he should the more
dread and arise against an evil will of envy
or ire to his even-Christians than many a
stirring or liking either of gluttony or of
lechery. Nevertheless, all men ween not
so. For commonly men are more afraid to
feel a stirring of fleshly sin, and have for it
more sorrow and heaviness, than for the
great liking in vainglory or in other ghostly
sins. But they are not wise, for if they will
understand Holy Writ and doctors’ saws
thereof, they should find as I say, which I
neither may nor will rehearse now.

I will not excuse them that fall in likings
of gluttony and lechery that they sin not,
for I wot well that all the species of them are
sin more or less, after the measure of the lust
of the sin and other likings with wilful cir-
cumstances. But I would that thou knewest
and chargest all, ilk a sin as it is, more the
more as are ghostly sins, less the less as are
fleshly sins. And yet shalt thou neverthe-
less hate and flee all, both bodily and ghostly,
upon thy might; for wit thou well that fleshly
desires and unskilful likings in meat and

145 G2

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

drink, or any likings that belong to the body,
passing reasonable need, though they be not
always great sins to him that is in charity,
nevertheless to a soul that desireth cleanness
and ghostly feeling of God, they are full
heavy, painful and bitter, and much to
eschew. For the spirit may not feel his
kindly savour within till the flesh have lost
his beastly savour without.

CHAPTER LXXV

THAT HUNGER AND OTHER BODILY PAINS
MUCH LET GHOSTLY WORKING

AND therefore if thou wilt come to clean-
ness of heart, it behoveth thee to withstand
unskilful stirring of fleshly delights. But
against the ground thou shalt not rise, as I
have said bı fore: for the ground of it is need,
as natural hunger which thou shalt needs
feel and tend thereto in time, and help thyself
against it by medicine of meat, as thou
wouldest keep thyself reasonably against a
bodily sickness, that thou mightest more
freely serve God both bodily and ghostly.
For wit thou well what man or woman that
shall be occupied ghostly in thoughts, un-
skilful pain or hunger wilfully taken, or
bodily sickness in the stomach or in the head,
or in other part of the body for the defaul

146 ;

Ch. 76] OF PERFECTION

of good ruling of himself, by too much fast-
ing or by any other wise, such a one shall
much let the spirit, and much hinder him
from the knowing and the beholding of
ghostly things, unless he have the more
grace. For though it be so that the bodily
pain, either of penance or of sickness, or else
bodily occupation, sometimes letteth not the
fervour of love to God in devotion, but often
increaseth it; nevertheless, I hold that it
letteth the fervour of love in contemplation,
the which may not be had nor felt but in
rest of body and soul.

CHAPTER LXXVI

WHAT REMEDY A MAN SHALL USE AGAINST
DEFAULT MADE IN EATING OR DRINKING

For this do thou skilfully that belongeth to
thee, and keep thy bodily kind upon reason,
and suffer God then to send what He will,
health or sickness; take it gladly and murmur
not against God wilfully. Do thou as I say,
take thy meat as it cometh, and ordain for it
if need be upon reason, and take it gladly
as for need. But be well ware of lust that
cometh with need; eschew too much as well
as too little. And so when thou hast done,
and then the biting of conscience cometh
to thy mind that thou hast eaten too much,

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

and so then beginnest to tarry thee and draw
to overmuch bitterness, lift up the desire of
thy heart to thy good Lord Jesus, and know
thyself for a wretch and a beast, and ask of
Him forgiveness, and saythat thou wiltamend
it, and trust of forgiveness. Leave off then
and tarry no longer withal, nor strive not
much as thou wouldest destroy it utterly,
for it is not worth to do so. Thou shalt
never so bring it about, but readily ordain
thee to some other occupation bodily or
ghostly, after thou feelest thee disposed, that
thou mightest more profit in other virtues,
as meekness and charity. For wit thou well
he that in his desire and in his travail hath
regard to none other thing but to meekness
and charity, aye crying after them how he
might have them, he shall in that desire with
working following after, profit and wax in all
other virtues, as in chastity, abstinence, and
such other, though he have but little regard
to them, more in a year, than he should with-
out this desire profit in seven, if he strive
with gluttony, lechery, and such other con-
tinually, and beat himself with scourges each
day from morn to evensong time.

CHAPTER LXXVII

THAT THROUGH BUSY DESIRE AND TRAVAIL
FOR MEEKNESS AND CHARITY, A MAN
COMETH SOONER TO OTHER VIRTUES
THAN BY TRAVAIL IN HIMSELF

GET to thee then meekness and charity, and
if thou wilt travail and swink busily to have

ofthem. And if you may get them they shall
rule thee and measure thee full privily how
thou shalt eat and how thou shalt drink and
succour all thy bodily need, that there shall
no man wit it unless thou wilt: and thou shall
not be in perplexity nor in ire nor in angriness
and heaviness, nor in lusts nor in likings,
but in peace of glad conscience with a sad
restfulness. I speak further than I thought
to have spoken in this matter, but neverthe-

God shall make all well. By this that I
have said may you somewhat see in this
image of sin how much it letteth thee. The
gospel saith how Abraham spake to the rich

CHAPTER LXXVIII

OF THE DARKNESS OF THE IMAGE OF SIN, AND
WHAT COMETH IN BY THE WINDOWS
THEREOF

LirT up thy lantern and see in this image
five windows by the which sin cometh into
thy soul, as the prophet saith: Death cometh
in by our windows.x* These windows are
the five wits by which thy soul goeth out
from himself, and seeketh his delight and his
feeding in earthly things against his own kind;
as by the eye to see curious and fair things,
and so of other wits. By the unskilful
using of these wits unto vanity wilfully, thy
soul is much letted from the ghostly wits
within; and therefore it behoveth thee to
stop these windows or bar them, and only
when need asketh to open them.

CHAPTER LXXIX

THAT THE SOUL FOR DEFAULT OF KNOWING OF

AND that were little mastery to thee if thou
mightest once see thy soul by clear under-

with a black mantle of this foul image; but,
because thou knowest it not, therefore thou
leavest the inly sight of thyself and seekest

and feed thy goats.* And it is thus much for

likeness of God, frail as a woman in thy
body for the first sin, because thou knowest
not thyself, that angels’ food should be thy
delights within, therefore thou goest out by
the bodily wits and seekest thy meat and thy
liking as a beast of the flock—that is as one
reproved—and therefore thou feedest thy
thoughts and thine affections, which are
unclean as goats. It is a shame to thee to
do so.

CHAPTER LXXXI

THAT THE HOLE OF THE IMAGINATION NEEDETH
TO BE STOPPED, AS WELL AS THE WINDOWS
OF THE WITS

BuT now sayest thou that thou dost so.
Thou seest no worldly things nor hearest
them, nor hast thou use of thy bodily wits
more than need asketh, and for this thou art
enclosed. As to this I say, if thou do thus,
as I hope thou dost, thou hast stopped a
great window of this image, but yet art thou
not secure, for thou hast not stopped the
privy holes of imagining in thine heart. For
albeit thou see me not with thy bodily eye,
thou mayest see me with thy soul by imagina-
tion, and so mayest thou do of all bodily
things. If thy soul be fed wilfully by
imaginations of vanities of the world, and
desiring of worldly things for a wilful com-
fort and ease, soothly although thy soul be
within as for the bodily wits, it is nevertheless

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

full far without by such vain imagination.
But now askest thou whether it be any great
sin for a soul to occupy him in such vanities
either in wits or in imagining. As unto this
I say I would that thou shouldst ask no man
this question. For he that will soothfastly
love God, he asketh not commonly whether
this or that is the greater sin. For he shall
think that anything which letteth him from
the love of God is great sin, and he shalt
think nothing sin but that thing which is not
good and letteth him from the love of God.
What is sin but a wanting or a forbearing of
good? I say not that it shall be painful to
him as a deadly sin, or a venial should be,
nor say I not but that he knoweth deadly from
venial and more fleeth it.

CHAPTER LXXXII

WHEN THE USE OF THE WITS IS DEADLY SIN,
AND WHEN VENIAL

NEVERTHELESS, some deal shall I say to thy
question, for thy desire draweth more out of
mine heart than I thought to have said in
the beginning. Our Lord saithin the Gospel
thus: A man made a great supper and called
many thereto, and sent his servant at supper
time after them that were prayed. The first
excused him and said on this wise, that he

Ch. 82] OF PERFECTION

might not come, for he had bought a town;
the other excused him that he might not come,
for he had bought five yokes of oxen and went
to assay them ; the third for that he had
wedded a wife.* I leave to speak of the first
and the last, and I tell thee of the middest,
of him that bought the oxen, for he is to this
purpose. Five yokes of oxen betoken the
five wits which are beastly as an ox. Now
this man that was called to the supper was
not reproved for that he bought these oxen
but because he went to assay them, and so
would not come. Right so I say to thee.
For to have thy wits and use them in need,
it is no sin; but if thou go wilfully to assay
them by vain delight in creatures, then it is
sin. For if thou choose that delight as a
final rest of thy soul, and as a full liking, that
thou keepest none other bliss to have but
such manner of worldly vanity, then it is
deadly, for thou choosest it as thy God, and
so shalt thou be put out from the supper.
For Saint Paul forbade us that we should
assay our wits so, when he said thus: Thou
shalt not go after thy lust,t not wilfully assay
thy likings. A man or a woman that is
encumbered with deadly sins shall not well
escape deadly sin in this, though he see it
not. Nevertheless if thou, by frailty, delight
thee in thy wits and such vanity, but in that
* Luke xiv 16-20. + Rom. xiii 14.

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

thou keepest in charity in other sides, and
thou choosest not this delight for a full rest
of thy soul, but thou settest aye God before
all things in thy desire, this sin is venial,
after the circumstances, more or less; nor
shalt thou for these venial sins be put from
the supper in the bliss of heaven. But thou
shalt want the tasting and the assaying of
that delicate supper while yet living on earth,
unless thou be busy with all thy might to
withstand such venial sins. For though it
be so that venial sins break not charity,
soothly they let the fervour and the ghostly
feeling of charity.

CHAPTER LXXXIII

HOW A GHOSTLY MAN OR WOMAN SHALL
BEHAVE TO THEM THAT COME TO THEM

BUT now sayest thou that thou mayest not
keep thee from hearing of vanities, for divers
men, worldly and other, come often to speak
with thee, and tell thee some tales of vanity.
Unto this I say thus, that communing with
thine even-Christian is not much against
thee, but helpeth thee sometimes if thou
work wisely. For thou mayest assay thereby
the measure of thy charity to thine even-
Christian, whether it be much or little.
Thou art bound, as each man or woman is,

Ch. 83] OF PERFECTION

to love thine even-Christian, principally in
thine heart, and also in deed, to show them
tokens of charity as reason asketh, after thy
might and after thy knowing. Now since it
is so that thou oughtest not to go out of thine
house to seek occasion how thou mayest
profit thine even-Christian by deeds of
mercy, because thou art enclosed, neverthe-
less thou art bound to love them all in thine
heart, and to show them that come to thee
tokens of love soothfastly. And, therefore,
whoso will speak with thee, whosoever he be,
or in what degree that he be, and thou
knowest not what he is nor why he cometh,
be soon ready with a good will to wit what
his will is. Be not dangerous* nor suffer
him not long to bide with thee, but look
how ready and how glad thou wouldest be if
an angel of heaven would come so and speak
with thee: so ready and so buxom be thou in
will to speak with thine even-Christian when
he cometh to thee, for thou wottest not
what he is, nor why he cometh, nor what
need he hath of thee or thou of him, till
thou hast assayed. And though thou be in
prayer or in devotion, so that thou art loath
to break off, because thou thinkest thou
shouldest not leave God for no man’s speech,
methinketh not so in this case. For if thou
be wise thou shalt not leave God, but thou

shalt find Him and have Him and see Him
in thine even-Christians as well as in prayer,
but in another manner shalt thou have
Him than in prayer. If thou canst well
love thine even-Christian, it should not

Discretion shalt thou have on this manner as

speech comfort thee, ask of him and make
thee not to teach him. It falleth not to thee
to teach a priest, but in need. If his speech
comfort thee not, answer little, and he will
soon take his leave. If it be another man
that cometh to give thee his alms, or else to

goodly and meekly to them all. Reprove

CHAPTER LXXXIV

OF THE DARK IMAGE OF SIN, AND OF THE
CLOTHING THEREOF

By this that I have said mayest thou see a
little the darkness of this image of sin; not
because I have described it fully to thee
as it is, for I cannot. Nevertheless by this
little thou mayest see more if thou look well.
But now sayest thou: Whereby wottest thou
that I bear such an image about with me
as thou speakest of? As on this I can
answer: I may take upon me a word said
by the prophet, and it is this: I have found
a false image, that men call a mawment,* in
myself,t well foul, disfigured and forshapen
with wretchedness of all those sins which
I have spoken of, by the which I am cast

down in many fleshly likings and worldly

CHAPTER LXXXV
WHICH BE THE LIMBS OF THE IMAGE OF SIN

THEN is this an ugly image to look upon.
The head is pride, for pride is the first and
the principal sin, as the Wise Man saith: The
beginning of all manner of sin is pride.t The
back and the hind part of it is covetousness,
as Saint Paul saith: J shall forget all worldly
things which are backward, and I shall stretch
me forward} to endless things. The breast,

in the which is the heart, is envy, for it is no

CHAPTER LXXXVI

WHEREOF THE IMAGE OF JESUS IS MADE, AND
HOW WE ARE PASSING FORTH IN. THE
IMAGE OF SIN

Tuis is not the image of Jesus, but is liker an
image of the devil. For the image of Jesus
is made of virtues, with meekness, and
perfect love, and charity. But this is of
false fleshly love to thyself with all these
members fastened thereto. This image
bearest thou and every man, whatsoever
he be, until by grace of Jesus it be some
deal destroyed and broken down. Thus it
seemeth that David saith in the psalter book,
that though it be so that a man were made in
the image of God, stable and steadfast, never-
theless because of sin he forth passeth living in
this world in the image of sin, by the which
he is unstable and troubled in vain.* Also
Saint Paul speaketh of this image thus: If
we will come to the love of God, as we have
borne herebefore the image of the earthly man,
the first Adam that is the image of sin, right
so now let us bear the image of the heavenlyt
man Jesus, Which is the image of virtues.

CHAPTER LXXXVII

HOW WE SHOULD CRUCIFY THE IMAGE OF SIN,
AND QUICKEN THE IMAGE OF JESUS

WHAT then shalt thou do with this image ?
I answer thee by the words that the Jews said
to Pilate of Christ: Tolle, tolle, cructfige eum !
Take thou this body of sin and do Him upon
the cross ;* that is to say, Break down this
image, and slay the false love of sin in thyself.
As Christ’s body was slain for our trespass,
right so it behoveth thee—if thou wilt be
like to Christ—to slay thy bodily liking and
fleshly lust in thyself. Thus said Saint
Paul: These, that are Christ’s followers, have
crucified and slain their flesh, that is the image
of sin, with all the lusts and unskilful likings
of 1t.T

Slay then and break down pride, and set
up meekness. Also break down ire and envy,
and raise up love and charity to thine even-
Christian. Also, instead of covetousness
have poverty of spirit, instead of accidiet
have fervour of devotion, with a glad readi-
ness to all good deeds, and in the stead of
gluttony and lechery have sobriety and
chastity in body and in soul. Thus coun-
selled Saint Paul when he said thus: Ye shall

 Accidie—i.e., spiritual sloth.

put down the old man, that is the image of sin
of the old Adam, with all his members, for he
is rotten in desires of error, and ye shall shape

CHAPTER LXXXVIII

WHAT PROFIT COMETH OF KEEPING OF THE
HEART, AND HOW MUCH THE SOUL IS, AND
WHAT IT LOVETH

GATHER then thy heart together and do after
the counsel of the Wise Man, when he saith
thus: With all thy business keep thine heart,
for out of it cometh lfe.; And this is sooth
when it is well kept, for then wise thoughts,
clean affections, and burning desires of
virtues and of charity and of the bliss of
heaven come out of it, and make the soul to
live a blessed life. Also on the contrary
wise, if it be not kept, then as our Lord saith
in the gospel: Evil thoughts and unclean
affections come out of the heart, the which
A Eph wiv225.24% 7 Prov. iv 23.

Ch. 89] OF PERFECTION

defoul man.* They either injure the life of
the sou! by deadly sin, or else they feeble the
soul and make it sick if they be venial. For
what is a man but his thoughts and his loves ?
These only make a man good or bad. As
much as thou lovest God and thine even-
Christian and knowest Him, so much is thy
soul; and if thou little love Him, little is thy
soul; and if thou naught love Him, naught
is thy soul. It is naught as for good, but it
is much as for sin. And if thou wilt wit
what thou lovest, look whereupon thou
thinkest; for where the love is there is the eye,
and where the liking is there is most the heart
thinking. If thou love God much, it liketh
thee to think much upon Him; and if thou
love little, then little thou thinkest upon Him.
Rule well thy thoughts and thine affections,
and then art thou virtuous.

CHAPTER LXXXIX

HOW THE IMAGE OF SIN SHALL BE BROKEN
DOWN

BEGIN then on, and break this image when

thou hast inwardly bethought thee of thyself

and of thy wretchedness, as I have said; how

proud, how vain, how envious, how melan-

cholious, how covetous, how fleshly, and how
x Matt. xv 19, 20.

THE SCALE [Bk. I

full of corruption; also how little knowing,
feeling or savour thou hast of God and of
ghostly things, how wise, how quick and how
much savour thou hast in earthly things,
and shortly that thou think thee also full of
sin as the hide is full of flesh. Be thou not
too much adread, though thou think so of
thyself. And when thou hast done thus,
lift up then the desire of thy heart to thy
Lord Jesus, and pray of Him help. Cry to
Him by great desires and sighings that He
will help thee to bear this great burden of
this image, or else that He will break it.
Think also what a shame it is for thee to be
fed with swines’ meat of fleshly savours that
shouldest feel a ghostly savour of heavenly
joy. If thou do thus, then beginnest thou
to’ arise against the whole ground of sin in
thee as I have said. And it may so be that
thou shalt feel pain and sorrow, for thou
shalt understand that there may no soul live
without great pain, unless he have rest and
delight either in his creator or in a creature.
Then when thou risest against thyself by a
fervent desire to feel of thy Lord Jesus, and
to draw out of thy love from all bodily things
and from rest in thy bodily feeling, inasmuch
as thou art encumbered of thyself, and thou
thinkest that all creatures rise against thee,
and all things which thou hadst delight in
before turn for thee to pain. And when

Ch. 90] OF PERFECTION

thou forsakest thus thyself and may not
lightly find comfort in God, needlings thy
soul shall suffer pain. Nevertheless I hope
whoso would suffer this pain awhile, stead-
fastly cleaving upon this desire and naked
mind of Jesus Christ, and upon desire that
he have not but his Lord and fall not lightly
therefrom, nor seek no comfort outward for
a time—for it iasteth not long—our Lord is
nigh and soon shall ease thy heart, for He will
help thee to bear thy body full of corruption,
and He will with His merciful might of
gracious presence break down this false
image of love in thyself, not all at once, but
by little and by little, till thou be some deal
reformed 'to His likeness.

CHAPTER XC

HOW A MAN SHALL BEHAVE HIM TO THE
STIRRING OF PRIDE AND OF ALL OTHER
VICES

AFTER such a whole rising against thyself,
when it is passed, thou shalt more soberly
and more easily rule thyself and set thee
more sadly to keep thy thoughts and thine
affections, to know them whether they be
good or bad. And thenif thou feel a stirring
in pride in any manner of species of it, be
soon wary if thou mayest, and suffer it not

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

to scape lightly away, but take it in mind and
rend it, break it and despise it, and do all
the shame that thou mayest thereto. Look
thou spare it not nor trow it not, speak he
never so fair, for it is false though it seemeth
sooth, as the prophet saith: Thou man of my
people, they that say thou art blessed and holy,
they beguile and bring thee into error.* And
if thou do thus often busily, thou shalt, by
the grace of Jesus, within short time stop
much of the spring of pride, and much abate
the vain delight thereof, so that thou shalt
scarcely feel it. And when thou feelest it,
it shall be so weak and as if it were half dead,
that it shall not much grieve thee. And
then thou shalt be able to have a ghostly
sight of meekness, how good and how fair it
is, and thou shalt desire it and love it for the
goodness of itself, that thou shalt like to be
holden as thou art, and if need be to suffer
gladly despite and reproof for love of right-
eousness. Upon the same manner, when
thou feelest stirrings of ire and of melan-
choly rising of heart, or any evil will against
thine even- Christian for any manner of
cause, though it seem reasonable and for
charity, beware of it and be ready with thy
thought to refrain it that it turn not into
fleshly appetite. Withstand it and follow it
not, neither in word nor in deed, as much as
* JTsa, iii 12.

Ch, 91] OF PERFECTION

thou mayest, but as it riseth smite it down
again, and so shalt thou slay it with the
sword of dread of God that it shall not
grieve thee. For wit thou well that in all
these stirrings of pride, vainglory, envy, or
any other, as soon as thou perceivest it and
with displeasure of thy will and of thy
reason withstandest it, thou slayest it, though
it be so that it cleave still upon thy heart
against thy will, and will not lightly pass
away. Dread it not; for it letteth thy soul
from peace, but it defileth not thy soul.
Right so upon the same wise shalt thou do
against all evil stirrings of covetousness,
accidie, gluttony and lechery, that thou be
always ready with thy reason and thy will'to
reprove them and despise them.

CHAPTER XCI

WHAT THING HELPETH MOST A MAN’S KNOW-
ING, AND GETTETH HIM THAT WHICH HE
LACKETH, AND MOST DESTROYETH SIN IN
HIM

AND that mayest thou do the better and the

more readily if thou be busy to set thine

heart most upon one thing. And that thing

is naught else but a ghostly desire to God, to

please Him, to love Him, to know Him, to

see Him, and to have Him by grace here in a
169 H

THE SCALE [Bk. 1

little feeling and in the bliss of heaven in a
full being. This desire, if thou keep it,
shall well tell thee which is sin and which is
not, and which is good and which is better
good. And if thou wilt fasten thy thought
thereto, it shall teach thee all that thou .
needest, and it shall get thee all that thou
wantest. And therefore, when thou shalt
rise against the ground of sin in general or
else in any special sin, hang fast upon this
desire, and set the point of thy thought more
upon God whom thou desirest than upon the
sin which thou reprovest. For if thou do so,
then God fighteth for thee, and He shall
destroy sin in thee. Thou shalt much
sooner come to thy purpose if thou do so,
than if thou leave thy meek desire to God
principally, and will set thine heart only
against the stirring of sin, as though thou
wouldest destroy it by mastery of thyself.
Thou shalt never so bring it about.

CHAPTER XCII

HOW A MAN SHALL BE SHAPEN TO THE IMAGE
OF JESUS, AND JESUS SHAPEN IN HIM

But do as I have said and better if thou

mayest, and I hope by the grace of Jesus thou

shalt make the devil ashamed, and all such

wicked stirrings thou shalt break away, that

Ch, 92] OF PERFECTION

they shall not much grieve thee. And upon
this manner wise may the image of sin be
broken down in thee and destroyed, by the
which thou art forshapen from the kindly
shape of Christ’s image. And thou shalt
be shapen again to the image of Jesus man by
meekness and charity; and thou shalt be full
shapen to the self image of God, here living
by shadow in contemplation, and in the
bliss of heaven in full soothfastness. Of
this shaping to the likeness of Christ
speaketh Saint Paul thus: My dear children,
which I bear, as a woman beareth her child,
until Christ be again shapen in you.* Thou
hast conceived Christ by troth, and He
hath life in thee inasmuch as thou hast a
good willand a desire to serve Him and please
Him, but He is not yet full shapen in thee nor
thou in Him by fulness of charity. And
therefore Saint Paul bare thee and me and
other also with travail, as a woman beareth a
child, unto the time that Christ hath His full
shape in us and wein Him. Whoso weeneth
to come to the working and to the full use of
contemplation and not by this way, that is
to say not by fulhead of virtues, he cometh
not by the door, and therefore as a thief he
shall be cast out. I say not but that a man
may by the gift of God have by times a
tasting and a glimmering of life contem-
* Gal, iv 19.

I7I

THE SCALE ` [Bk. 1

plative, as do some men in the beginning;
but the sad feeling of it he shall not have.
For Christ is the door and He is porter, and
without His life and His livery may there no `
man come in, as He saith Himself: No man
cometh to the Father but by Me.* ‘That is to
say, no man may come to the contemplation
of the godhead, unless he be first reformed
by fulness of meekness and charity to the
likeness of Jesus in His manhood.

CHAPTER XCIII

THE CAUSE WHY THIS BOOK WAS MADE, AND
HOW SHE SHOULD BEHAVE HER IN READ-
ING THEREOF THAT IT WAS MADE FOR.

Lo I have told thee a little as methinketh,
first of contemplative life what it is, and then
of the ways which by grace lead thereto.
Not that I have it in feeling and in working
as I have it in saying. Nevertheless I would
by these words, such as they are, first stir
mine own negligence to do better then I
have done, and also my purpose is to stir thee
or any other man or woman that hath taken
the state of life contemplative to travail more
busily and more meekly in that manner of
life by such simple words as God hath given
me grace to say. And therefore if any aia
* John xiv 6.

Ch. 93] OF PERFECTION

be therein that stirreth thee or comforteth
thee more to the love of God, thank God, for
it is His gift and not of the word. And if it
comfort thee not, or else thou take it not
readily, study nor too long thereabout, but
lay it beside thee till another time, and give
thee to prayer or to other occupation. Take
it as it will come, and not all at once. Also
these words that I write, take them not too
strictly, but where thou thinkest by good
advisement* that I speak too shortly, either
for lacking of English or wanting of reason, I
pray thee amend it only where need is.
Also these words that I write to thee, they
belong not at all to a man that hath active
life, but to thee or to any other which hath
the state of life contemplative.

The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ be with
thee.
