BOOK II

CHAPTER:

THAT A MAN IS THE IMAGE OF GOD AFTER THE
SOUL AND NOT AFTER THE BODY

FoRASMUCH as thou covetest greatly and
askest it for charity to hear more of an image
the which I have beforetimes in part de-
scribed to thee, therefore I will gladly with
dread fall to thy desire, and, helping the
grace of our Lord Jesus in whom I fully
trust, I shall open thee a little more of this
image. At the beginning, if thou wilt wit
plainly what I mean by this image, I tell thee
forsooth that I understand naught else but
thine own soul. For thy soul and my soul
and every reasonable soul is an image, and
that a worthy image, for it is the image of
God as the apostle saith: A man is the image
of God,* and made to the image and the
likeness of Him; not in the bodily shape
without but in the mights of it within, as
Holy Writ saith: Our Lord God shaped man in
his soul to His own image and likeness.t| ‘This
Ke TCO RU + Gen. i 27.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

is the image that I have spoken of. This
image, made to the image of God, in the first
shaping was wonderfully fair and bright, full
of burning love and ghostly light, but through
sin of the first man Adam it was disfigured
and forshapen into another likeness, as I |
have said before. For it fell from that
ghostly light and heavenly food into painful
darkness and lust of this wretched life;
exiled and cast out from the heritage of
heaven that it should have had if it had stood
still, into the wretchedness of this earth, and
afterwards into the prison of hell there to
have been without end. From the which
prison to the heavenly heritage it might
never have come again unless it had been
reformed to the first shape and the first like-
ness. But that reforming might not be made
by none earthly man, for every man was in
the same mischief, and none might suffice
to help himself and so much less another
man. Therefore it needed to be done by
Him that is more than man, that is only God;
and that was skilful that He should reform
and restore man to bliss if he should be
saved, which of His endless goodness first
shaped him thereto. How then it might be
reformed, and how it is reformed to the first
likeness by Him that first formed it, by the
grace of God shall I tell thee; for that is the
intent of this writing.

Ch. 2] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER II

HOW MANKIND NEEDETH THAT, ONLY THROUGH
THE PASSION OF CHRIST, WHAT WAS FOR-
SHAPEN BY THE FIRST SIN SHOULD BE
RESTORED AND REFORMED

THE righteousness of God asketh that a
trespass done be not forgiven, unless amends
be made for it, if it may be done. Now it is
sooth mankind, that was whole in Adam the
first man, trespassed against God so wonder
grievously, when it forfeited the special
bidding of Him and assented to the false
counsel of the fiend, that it deserved right-
fully to have been departed from Him and
damned to hell without end; so far forth that,
standing the righteousness of God, it might
not be forgiven, unless amends and full satis-
faction were first made therefor. But this
amends might no man make that was man only
and came out of Adam by kindly generation,
for this reason, because the trespass and
unworship was endless great, wherefore it
passed man’s might to make amends for it.
And also for this reason: he that hath tres-
passed and shall make amends, it behoveth
him to give to him that he hath trespassed
against, all that he oweth though he had not
trespassed; and also above that it behoveth
177 H2

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

him to give somewhat that he oweth not but
only because he trespassed. But mankind had
not wherewith that he might pay God for his
trespass, over that he owed Him. For what
good deed a man might do in body or in soul,
it was but his debt. For every man oweth,
as the Gospel saith, to love God with all his
heart and all his soul and all his mights;
and better might he not do than this.
And nevertheless this deed sufficed not
the reforming of mankind, nor might he do
this unless he were first reformed. Then
needeth it that, if man’s soul should be re-
formed and the trespass made good, our
Lord Himself should reform this image
and make amends for this trespass since
no man might do this. But that He might
not do in His godhead; for He might not
nor owed He to make amends by suffer-
ing of pain in His own kind. Therefore it
needed that He should take the same man-
kind that had trespassed, and become man;
and that He might not do by the common law
of generation, for it was impossible for God’s
son to be born of a touched woman. There-
fore He must become man through a gracious
generation, by the working of the Holy
Ghost, of a clean gracious maiden, our Lady
Saint Mary. And so it was done. For our
Lord Jesus, God’s Son, became man, and
through His precious death that He suffered

Ch. 2] OF PERFECTION

made amends to the Father of Heaven for
man’s guilt. And that might He well do,
for He was God, and He owed not for
Himself, but inasmuch as He was man born
of the same kind that Adam was that
first trespassed. And so though He owed it
not for His own person, for Himself might
not sin, nevertheless He owed it of His
freewill for the trespass of mankind, the
which mankind He took for the salvation of
man of His endless mercy. For sooth it is
there was never man that might yield to God
anything of his own that he owed not, but
only this blessed Jesus. For He might pay
God one thing that He owed not as for Him-
self, and that was naught but one thing; that
was to give His precious life by wilful taking
of death for love of soothfastness. This
owed He not. As much good as He might do
to the worship of God in this life, all was but
debt. But to take death for love of righteous-
ness He was not bound thereto. For death
is only a pain ordained to man for his own
sin; but our Lord Jesus sinned never nor
might He sin, and therefore He owed not to
die. And since He owed not to die and yet
wilfully He died, then paid He to God more
than He owed. And since that was the best
man’s deed and most worthy that ever was
done, therefore was it just that the sin of
mankind should be forgiven; inasmuch as

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

mankind had found a man of the same kind
without venom of sin, that is Jesus, that
might make amends for the trespass done,
and pay our Lord God all that he owed and -
over more that He owed not. Then since
our Lord Jesus, God and man, died thus for
salvation of man’s soul, it was rightful that
sin should be forgiven and man’s soul, that
was His image, should be reformed and
restored to the first likeness and to the bliss
of heaven.

This passion of our Lord and this precious
death is the ground of all the reforming of
man’s soul, without which might never man’s
soul have been reformed to the likeness of
Him nor come to the bliss of heaven. But
blessed may He be in all His working. Now
is it so that, through the virtue of His precious
passion, the burning sword of cherubin that
drove Adam out of paradise is put away, and
the endless gates of heaven are open to every
man that will enter in thereto. For the
person of Jesus is both God and King of
heaven in the bliss of the Father; and as man
He is porter at the gate ready to receive every
soul that will be reformed here in this life to
His likeness. For now may every soul, if he
will, be reformed to the likeness of God; since
the trespass is forgiven and amends through
Jesus is made for the first guilt. Neverthe-
less, though this be sooth, all souls have not

Ch. 3] OF PERFECTION

the profit nor the fruit of this precious
passion, nor are all reformed to the likeness
of Him.

CHAPTER III

THAT JEWS AND PAYNIMS AND ALSO FALSE
CHRISTIAN MEN ARE NOT REFORMED EFFEC-
TUALLY THROUGH THE VIRTUE OF THE
PASSION, THROUGH THEIR OWN FAULT

Two manner of men are not reformed by the
virtue of this passion. One is of them that
trow it not, and another is of them that love
it not. Jews and paynims have not the
benefice of this passion, for they trow it not.
Jews trow not that Jesus, the son of the Virgin
Mary, is- God’s son of heaven. Also the
paynims trow not that the sovereign wisdom
of God would become the son of man, and in
manhood would suffer the pain of death.
And therefore the Jews held the preaching
of the cross and of the passion of Christ
naught but slander and blasphemy, and
the paynims held it naught but phantom
and folly. But true Christian men held
it the sovereign wisdom of God, and His
great might. Thus saith Saint Paul: We
preach to you that we trow, that Jesus
Christ crucified, the son of Mary, 1s God’s son,
sovereign virtue and wisdom of God. The
IŜI

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

which Jesus to Jews and paynims that trow not
in Him is but slander and folly.* And there-
fore these men, by their untroth, put them-
selves from the reforming of their own souls, |
and standing their untroth, shall they never
be saved nor come to the bliss of heaven.
For sooth it is, from the beginning of the
world unto the last end, there was never man
saved nor shall be saved, unless he had
troth general or special in Jesus Christ to
come or come. For right as all chosen souls
that were before the Incarnation under the
Old Testament had troth in Christ, that He
should come and reform man’s soul, either
openly as patriarchs and prophets and other
holy men had, or else privily and generally
as children and other simple and imperfect
souls had that knew not specially the privity
of the Incarnation: right so all chosen souls
under the New Testament have troth in
Christ that He is come, either openly and
feelingly as ghostly men and wise men have,
or else generally as children have that die
christened, and other simple and lewd souls
that are nourished in the bosom of Holy
Church. Since this is sooth, then methink-
eth that these men greatly and grievously err
who say that Jews and Saracens, by keeping
of their own law, may be made safe, though
they trow not in Jesus Christ as Holy Church
à KaG 12355245 ;

Ch. 3] OF PERFECTION

troweth; inasmuch as they ween that their
own troth is good and secure and sufficient
to their salvation. And in that troth they do,
as it seems, many good deeds of righteous-
ness, and peradventure if they knew that
Christian faith were better than their own is,
they would leave their own and take it, and
therefore they should be saved. Nay, it is
not enough so. For Christ, God and man,
is both way and end, and He is mediator
betwixt God and man, and without Him may
no soul be reconciled nor come to the bliss of
heaven. And they that trow not in Him
that is both God and man may never be safe
nor come to bliss. Other men also that love
not Christ nor His passion are not reformed
in the soul to the likeness of Him; and these
men are false Christian men, the which are
out of charity and live and die in deadly sin.
These men trow well, as it seemeth, that
Jesus is God’s son, and that His passion
sufficeth to salvation of man’s soul, and they
trow also all the other articles of the faith;
but it is an unhappy troth and a deadly, for
they love Him not nor choose not the fruit of
His passion, but they lie still in their sin, and
in the false love of this world unto their last
end. And so they are not reformed to the
likeness of God, but go to the pains of hell
endlessly, as Jews and Saracens do, and into
much greater pain than they, inasmuch as

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

they had the troth and kept it not. For it was
more trespass than if they never had it.

Then if thou wilt wit which souls are
reformed here in this life to the image of
God through virtue of His passion, soothly
only those that trow in Him and love Him.
In which souls the image of God, that was
forshapen as it were into a foul beast’s like-
ness, is restored and reformed to the first
shape, and to the worthiness and worship
that it had in the beginning; without which
restoring and reforming shall never soul be
saved nor come to bliss.

CHAPTER IV

OF TWO MANNER OF REFORMING OF THIS
IMAGE, ONE IN FULNESS AND ANOTHER
IN FAITH

Now, sayest thou, how may this be sooth
that the image of God, the which is man’s
soul, might be reformed here in this life to
His likeness in any creature? It seemeth
nay, it might not be so. For if it were re-
formed, then should it have stable mind,
clear sight, and clean burning love in God
and ghostly things everlastingly, as it had in
the beginning. But that is in no creature, as
thou trowest, living here in this life. For as
anent thyself thou canst well say thou think-

Ch. 4] OF PERFECTION

est thee full far therefrom. Thy mind, thy
reason, and the love of thy soul are so much
set in beholding and in love of earthly things,
that of ghostly things thou feelest right little.
Thou feelest no reforming in thyself, but
thou art so encompassed with this. black
image of sin for aught thou mayest do, that
upon what side thou turn thee thou feelest
thyself defouled and spotted with fleshly
stirrings of this foul image. Other changing
feelest thou none from fleshliness into
ghostliness; neither in privy mights of thy
soul within, nor in bodily feeling without.
Wherefore thou thinkest that it might not
be that this image might be reformed; or else
if it might be reformed, then askest thou how
it might be reformed. To this I answer and
say thus. ‘There is two manner of reforming
of the image of God which is man’s soul.
One is in fulness, another is in part. Re-
forming in fulness may not be had in this
life, but it is delayed after this life to the
bliss of heaven; where man’s soul shall be
fully reformed; not to the state that it had
at the first beginning by kind, or might have
had through grace if it had stood whole, but
it shall be restored to much more bliss and
much higher joy through the great mercy
and endless goodness of God, than it should
have had if it had never fallen. For then
shall the soul receive the whole and the full

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

feeling of God in all the mights of it, without
mingling of any other affection; and it shall
see mankind in the person of Jesus above the
kind of angels oned to the Godhead. For
then shall Jesus, both God and man, be all in
all, and only He and none other but He, as
the prophet saith: Our Lord Jesus in that day
that is everlasting day shall be highed only,*
‘and none but He. And also the body of man
shall then be glorified, for it shall receive
fully the rich dowry of undeadliness with all
that belongeth thereto. This shall a soul
have with the body, and much more than I
can say: but that shall be in the bliss of
heaven and not in this life. For though it
be so that the passion of our Lord is the cause
of all this reforming of man’s soul, neverthe-
less it was not His will to grant this full
reforming right soon after His passion to all
chosen souls that were living in time of His
passion, but He delayed it unto the last day.
And that was for this reason. Sooth it is
that our Lord Jesus Christ of His mercy
ordained a certain number of souls to salva-
tion, which number was not fulfilled in the
time of His passion. And therefore it needed
that by length of time, through kindly genera-
tion of man, it should be fulfilled. Then if
it had so been that right soon after the death
of our Lord every soul that would have
* Tsa. ii TE.

Ch. 4] OF PERFECTION

trowed in Him should by belief have been
blessed and full reformed without any other
abiding, there would have been no creature
that lived then, that would not have received
the faith to have been made blessed, and then
should generation have ceased. And so we,
chosen souls that are now living, and other

is this. For seeing that man in his first
forming of God was set in his free will and
had free choosing whether he would have
God fully or not, it was therefore reasonable
that (since he would not choose God then,
but wretchedly fell from Him), if he should
afterwards be reformed, he should be set
again in the same free choosing that he was
first in, whether he would have the profit
of his reforming or no. And this may be a
reason why man’s soul was not reformed
fully, as fast after the passion of Christ.

CHAPTER V

THAT REFORMING IN PART IS IN TWO MANNERS,
ONE IN FAITH AND ANOTHER IN FEELING

ANOTHER reforming of this image is in part;
and this reforming may be had in this life,
and unless it be had in this life, it may never
be had, nor the soul ever be saved. But this
reforming is on two manners. One is in
faith only, another is in faith and in feeling.
The first reforming in faith only sufficeth to
salvation; the second is worthy to have
passing meed in the bliss of heaven. The
first may be had lightly and in short time,
the second may not so, but through length
of time and much ghostly travail. The first
may be had with the feeling of the image of
sin; for though a man feel nothing in himself
but all stirrings of sin and fleshly desires,
notwithstanding that feeling, if he wilfully
assent thereto, he may be reformed in faith
to the likeness of God. But the second
reforming putteth out the liking in feeling of
fleshly stirrings and worldly desires, and
suffereth no such spots to abide in this image.
The first reforming is only of beginning and
profiting souls, and of active men. The
second is of perfect souls and of contemplative
men. For by the first reforming the image

Ch. 6] OF PERFECTION

of sin is not destroyed, but it is left as it were
all whole in feeling. But the second reform-
ing destroyeth the old feelings of this image
of sin, and bringeth into the soul new gracious
feelings through the working of the Holy
Ghost. The first is good, the second is
better, but the third that is in the bliss of
heaven is most best. First let us speak of
that one, and next of that other; and so shall
we come to the third.

CHAPTER’ VI

THAT THROUGH THE SACRAMENT OF BAPTISM,
THAT IS GROUNDED IN THE PASSION OF
CHRIST, THIS IMAGE IS REFORMED FROM
ORIGINAL SIN

Two manner of sins maketh a soul to lose the
shape and the likeness of God. The one is
called original, that is the first sin. The
other is called actual, that is sin wilfully done.
These two sins put away a soul from the
bliss of heaven, and damn it to the endless
pain of hell, unless through the grace of God
it be reformed to His likeness, ere it pass
hence out of this life. Nevertheless, two
remedies are there against these two sins, by
the which a forshapen soul may be restored
again. One is the sacrament of baptism
against original sin, another is the sacrament

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

of penance against actual sin. A soul of a
child, that is born and is unchristened,
because of original sin hath no likeness of
God; he is naught but an image of the fiend
and a brand of hell. But as soon as it is
christened, it is reformed to the image of
God, and through the virtue of faith of Holy
Church suddenly it is turned from the like-
ness of the fiend and made like an angel of
heaven. Also the same falleth to a Jew ora
Saracen, the which ere they are christened
are naught but manciples* of hell. But
when they forsake their errors and fall
meekly to the truth in Christ and receive the
baptism of water in the Holy Ghost, soothly
without any more tarrying they are reformed
to the likeness of God so fully, as Holy
Church troweth, that if they might as soon
after baptism pass out of this world, they
should flee straight to heaven without any
more letting, had they done never so much
sin before in the time of their untroth, and
never should they feel the pain of hell nor
of purgatory. And that privilege should
they have by the right of Christ’s passion.

* Manciples—z.e., slaves.

Ch. 7] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER VII

THAT THROUGH THE SACRAMENT OF PENANCE
THAT STANDETH IN CONTRITION, CON-
FESSION AND SATISFACTION, THIS IMAGE
IS REFORMED FROM ACTUAL SIN

ALSO what man or woman that hath lost the
likeness of God through a deadly sin, break-
ing God’s commandments, if he through
touch of grace soothfastly forsake his sin with
sorrow and contrition of heart, and be in
full will to amend him and turn him to
good living, and in this foresaid will receiveth
the sacrament of penance if he may—or if he
may not he is in will thereto—soothly I say
that this man’s soul or woman’s, that was
forshapen first to the likeness of the devil
through deadly sin, is now by the sacrament
of penance restored and shapen again to the
image of our Lord God. This is a great
courtesy of our Lord and an endless mercy
that so lightly forgiveth all manner of sin,
and so suddenly giveth plenty of grace to a
sinful soul that asketh mercy of Him. He
abideth not great penance doing nor painful
fleshly suffering ere He forgive it. But He
asketh a loathing of sin, and a full forsaking
in will of the soul for the love of Him, and a
turning of the heart to Him. This asketh

IQI

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

He, for this giveth He. And then, when He
seeth this, without any delaying He forgiveth
the sin, and reformeth the soul to His like-
ness. The sin is forgiven that the soul shall
not be damned. Nevertheless, the pain
debted for the sin is not yet fully forgiven,
unless contrition and love be the more.
And therefore shall he go and show him and
shrive him to his ghostly Father and receive
penance enjoined for his trespass and gladly
fulfil it; so that both the sin and the pain may
be done away ere he pass hence. And that
is the skilful ordinance of Holy Church for
great profit of man’s soul; that though the
sin be forgiven through the virtue of contri-
tion, nevertheless, in fulfilling of meekness
and to make whole satisfaction, he shall, if
he may, show to his priest plenary confession,
for that is his token and his warranty of
forgiveness against all his enemies, and that
is needful to have. For if a man had for-
feited his life against a king of this earth, it
were not enough to him as for a full security
to have only forgiveness of the king, unless he
have a charter the which may be his token
and his warrant against all other men.
Right so it may be said ghostly. If a man
have forfeited against the King of heaven his
life through deadly sin, it is not enough to
him for full security to have forgiveness of
God only by contrition between God and

Ch. 7] OF PERFECTION

him, unless he have a charter made by Holy
Church if he may come thereto. And that
is the sacrament of penance, the which is his
charter and his token of forgiveness.. For `
seeing that he forfeited both against God and
Holy Church, it is reasonable that he have
forgiveness for that one, and a warrant for
that other. And this is one reason why con-
fession is needful. Another reason is this:
for since this reforming of the soul standeth
in faith only and not in feeling, therefore a
fleshly man, that is rude and boisterous and
cannot lightly deem but of outward bodily
things, should not trow that his sins had been
forgiven him unless he had had some bodily
token, and that is confession, through the
which token he is made all secure of forgive-
ness if he do what in him is. This is the
troth of Holy Church as I understand it.
Also another reason is this: though the
ground of forgiveness stand not principally
in confession but in contrition of the heart
and in forethinking of sin, nevertheless I hold
that there is many a soul that should never
have felt very contrition nor had the full
forsaking of sin if confession had not.been.
For it befalleth often that in the time of con-
fession grace and compunction come to a
soul that before never felt grace, but ever was
cold and dry and far from feeling of grace.
And therefore since confession was so

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

profitable to the more part of Christian men,
Holy Church ordained, for the more security
generally to all Christian men, that every man
` and woman should once in the year at least
be shriven of all their sins that come to their
mind, to their ghostly father, though they
had never so much contrition beforetime.
Nevertheless I think that, if all men had been
as busy about the keeping of themselves in
eschewing of all manner sin and had come
to as much great knowing and feeling of God
as some men have, Holy Church should not
have ordained the token of confession as for
needful bond, for it had not needed. But
because all men are not perfect—and per-
adventure the most part of Christian men is
imperfect—therefore Holy Church ordained
confession by way of general bond to all
Christian men, that will know Holy Church
as their mother and will be buxom to her
bidding. If this be sooth, as I hope it is,
then he erreth greatly that generally saith that
confession of sins to a priest is neither needful
to a sinner nor behoveable, and that no man
is bound thereto. For by that which I have
said it is both needful and speedful to all souls
that in this wretched life are defiled through
sin, and namely to those that are through
deadly sin forshapen from the likeness of God,
the which may not be reformed to His like-
ness except by the sacrament of penance, that

Ch. 8] OF PERFECTION

principally standeth in contrition and sorrow
of heart, and secondarily in shrift of mouth
following after it, if it may be had. Upon
this manner, by this sacrament of penance, is
a sinful soul reformed to the image of God
and to His likeness.

CHAPTER VIII:

HOW IN THE SACRAMENT OF BAPTISM AND OF
PENANCE, THROUGH A PRIVY UNPERCEIV-
ABLE WORKING OF THE HOLY GHOST, THIS
IMAGE IS REFORMED THOUGH IT BE NOT
SEEN NOR FELT

BuT this reform standeth in faith and not in
feeling; for right as the property of faith is
to trow what thou seest not, right so it is to
trow what thou feelest not. But he that is
reformed in his soul by the sacrament of
penance to the image of God, he feeleth no
changing in himself, neither in his bodily
kind without, nor in the privy substance of
his soul within, other than he did. For he is
as he was unto his feeling, and he feeleth the
same stirrings of sin and the same corruption
of his flesh in passions and worldly desires
rising in his heart as he did before. And
nevertheless he shall trow that he is through
grace reformed to the likeness of God, though
he neither feel it nor see it. He may well

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

feel sorrow for his sin and a turning of his
will from sin to cleanness of living, if he have
grace and take good keep of himself. But he
may neither see nor feel the reforming of his
soul, how it is wonderly and unperceivably .
changed from the filth of the fiend unto the
fairness of an angel through the privy gracious
working of the Holy Ghost. That may he
not see, but he shall trow it; and if he trow it,
then is his soul reformed in truth. For right
as Holy Church troweth that by the sacrament
of baptism soothfastly received a Jew or a
Saracen or a child born is reformed in soul to
the likeness of God through a privy unper-
ceivable working of the Holy Ghost, not-
withstanding all the fleshly stirrings of his
body of sin, the which he shall feel after his
baptism as well as he did before; right so by
the sacrament of penance meekly and truly
received, a false Christian man that hath
been encumbered with deadly sin all his
lifetime is reformed in his soul within un-
perceivably, out-taking a turning of his will
through a privy might and a gracious working
of the Holy Ghost that suddenly worketh
and, in time of a moment or the twinkling of
an eye, righteth a froward soul and turneth it
from ghostly filth to fairness unseeable, and
of a servant of the fiend maketh a son of joy,
and of the prisoner of hell maketh a perceiver
of heavenly heritage, notwithstanding all the

Ch. 8] OF PERFECTION

fleshly feeling of this sinful image that is the
bodily kind. For thou shalt understand that
the sacrament of baptism or of penance is not
of that virtue to let and destroy utterly all
the stirrings of fleshly lusts and painful
passions, that a man’s soul may never feel no
manner rising nor stirring of them at any
time. For if it were so, then were a soul
fully reformed here to the worship of the first
making: but that may not be fully in this life.
But it is of that virtue that it cleanseth the
soul from all the sins beforedone, and if it
depart from the body saveth it from damna-
tion, and if it dwell in the body it giveth the
soul grace to withstand the stirrings of sin,
and keepeth it in grace also, that no manner
of lusty stirrings of passions that it feeleth
in the flesh, be they never so grievous, shall
hurt it nor depart it from God, as long as it
wilfully assenteth not thereto. This Saint
Paul meaned when he said thus: Those souls
that are reformed to the image of God tn faith
through the sacrament of baptism or of penance
shall not be damned for feelings of this image
of sin, if it be so that they go not after the
stirrings of the flesh by doing of deed.*

* Rom. viii I.

CHAPTER IX

THAT WE SHOULD BELIEVE STEADFASTLY IN THE
REFORMING OF THIS IMAGE, IF OUR CON-
SCIENCE WITNESS TO US A FULL FORSAKING
OF SIN AND A TRUE TURNING OF OUR WILL
TO GOOD LIVING

Or this reforming in faith speaketh Saint
Paul thus: The righteous man liveth in faith.*
That is, he that is made rightful by baptism
or by penance he liveth in faith, which
sufficeth unto salvation and to heavenly
peace, as Saint Paul saith: We that are righted
and reformed through faith in Christ have
peace and accord made between God and us,}
notwithstanding the vicious feelings of our
body of sin. For though this reforming be
privy and may not well be felt here in this
life, nevertheless whoso troweth it stead-
fastly and shapeth his works busily to accord
to his troth, and turneth not again to deadly
sin, soothly when the hour of death cometh,
and the soul is departed from this bodily life,
then he shall find it sooth that I say now.
Thus saith Saint John in comfort of chosen
souls that live here in faith under the feeling
of this painful image: My dear friends, we are
now while we live here the sons of God, for we
Le Das Se T Rom. vt.

Ch. 10] OF PERFECTION

are reformed by faith in Christ to His like-
ness; but it showeth not yet what we are, for
it is all privy. Nevertheless know well that
when our Lord shall show Him at the last day,
then shall we appear with Him like unto Him
in endlessjoy.* If thou wilt wit then whether
thy soul be reformed to the image of God or
no, by that I have said thou mayest have
entry. Ransack thy conscience and look
what thy will is, for therein standeth all. If
it be turned from all manner of deadly sin,
that thou wouldest for nothing wittingly and
wilfully break the commandments of God,
and for what thou hast misdone herebefore
against His bidding thou hast shriven thee
meekly with full heart to leave it, and art
sorry that thou didst it, I say then surely
that thy soul is reformed in faith to the like-
ness of God.

CHAPTER X

THAT ALL THE SOULS THAT LIVE MEEKLY IN THE
FAITH OF HOLY CHURCH AND HAVE THEIR
FAITH QUICKENED IN LOVE AND CHARITY
ARE REFORMED BY THIS SACRAMENT, ALBEIT
THAT THEY MAY NOT FEEL THE SPECIAL
GIFT OF DEVOTION OR OF GHOSTLY FEELING

IN this reforming that is only in faith, the
most part of chosen souls lead their life, that
* x John iii 2.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

set their will steadfastly to flee all manner of
deadly sin, and to keep in love and charity to
their even-Christians, and to keep the com-
mandments of God after their cunning. And
when it so is that wicked stirrings and evil _
wills rise in their hearts, of pride or of envy,
of ire or lechery, or of any other head of sin,
they withstand them and strive against them
by displeasing of will, so that they follow not
in deed these wicked wills. And nevertheless
if they fall lightly, as it were against their will,
through frailty or uncunning, anon their
conscience grieveth them and paineth them
so grievously, that they may have no rest till
they be shriven and have forgiveness.
Soothly all these souls that thus live in this
reforming and are found therein in the hour
of death, they shall be saved and come to the
full reforming in the bliss of heaven, though
it be so that they might never have ghostly
feeling nor inward savour nor special grace
of devotion in all their lifetime. For else,
if thou say that no soul shall be saved unless
it were reformed in ghostly feeling that it
might feel devotion and ghostly savour in
God as some souls do through special grace,
then should few souls be saved in regard of
the multitude of the others.

Nay, it is not to trow that for those souls
only that only are devout and by grace come
to ghostly feeling, and for no more, our Lord

Jesus should have taken mankind and suffered
the hard passion of death. It had been a

for so few souls. Nay, His mercy is spread
longer than so. Nevertheless, on the con-

our Lord is so precious and His mercy so
much that there shall no soul be damned, nor
any Christian man do he never so evil, as
some fools ween, soothly thou errest greatly.
Therefore go in the mean and hold thee in

And that is that the most sinful man that
liveth in earth, if he turn his will through

his soul, and if he die in that state he shall
be safe. Thus promised our Lord by His
prophet, saying thus: In what time soever it
be that the sinful man 1s turned to God from sin
and have sorrow, thenceforth he shall live and
he shall not die endlessly.* And on the other
side, whoso liveth in deadly sin and will not
leave it nor amend him thereof, nor receive
the sacrament of penance—or else if he
receive it he taketh it not soothfastly for the
love of God, that is for the love of virtue and
cleanness, but only for dread of the pains of
hell—he is not reformed to the likeness of

CHAPTER XI

THAT SOULS THAT ARE REFORMED NEED EVER
TO FIGHT AND STRIVE AGAINST THE
STIRRING OF SIN WHILE THEY LIVE HERE:
AND HOW A SOUL MAY WIT WHEN IT
ASSENTETH TO THE STIRRINGS AND WHEN
NOT

Tuis reforming in faith is lightly gotten, but |
it may not so lightly be held. And therefore
what man or woman soever, who is reformed
to the likeness of God in troth, must have
much travail and business if they will keep
this image whole and clean, that it fall not
down again through weakness of will to the
image of sin. He may not be idle nor reck-
less, for the image of sin is so near fastened
to him and so continually presseth upon him
by divers stirrings of sin, that unless he be
right wary he shall full lightly through assent
fall again thereto. And therefore he needeth
ever to be striving and fighting against
wicked stirrings of this image of sin, and to
make none accord therewith, nor take no
friendship to it to be buxom to his unskilful
biddings; for if he do, he beguileth himself.

Ch. 11] OF PERFECTION

But soothly if he strive with them, he need
not have much dread of assenting, for strife
breaketh peace and false accord. It is good
that a man have peace with all thing, save
with the fiend and with this image of sin, for
against them he needeth ever to fight, in his
thought and in his deed, till he have gotten on
them the mastery, and that shall never be
fully in this life as long as he beareth and
feeleth this image. I say not but that a soul
through grace may have the higher hand
over this image, so far forth that he shall not
follow nor assent to the unskilful stirrings
of it. But to be so clean delivered from this
image that he should feel no suggestion nor
jangling of fleshly affection nor of vain
thought at any time, may no man have in
this life. I hope that a soul that is reformed
in feeling by ravishing of love in contempla-
tion of God may be so far from sensuality
and from vain imagination, and so far drawn
out and departed from the fleshly feeling for
a time, that it shall naught feel but God; but
that lasteth not ever. And therefore say I
that it behoveth every man to strive against
this image of sin, and namely he that is
reformed in faith only that so lightly may be
deceived therewith. In the person of which
men saith Saint Paul: The flesh lusteth against
the spirit and the spirit against the flesh.*
*eGals y7:

THE SCALE 3 [Bk. 2

That is, a soul reformed to the likeness of
God fights against the fleshly stirrings of this
image of sin, and also this image of sin
striveth against the will of the spirit. This `
manner of fighting of this double image
Saint Paul knew when he said thus: I have
found two laws in myself, one law in my soul
within, and another in my fleshly limbs without
fighting with it, that oft leadeth me as a
wretched prisoner to the law of sin.* By
these two laws in a soul I understand this
double image: by the law of the spirit I
understand the reason of the soul when it is
reformed to the image of God; by the law of
the flesh I understand the sensuality which
I call the image of sin. In these two laws
a soul reformed leadeth his life as Saint Paul
saith: In my soul, that is in my will and in my
reason, I serve the law of God ; but in my
flesh, that is in my fleshly appetite, I serve the
law of sin.f Nevertheless that a soul re-
formed shall not despair, though he serve
the law of sin by feeling of the vicious
sensuality against the will of the spirit
because of corruption of the bodily kind,
Saint Paul excuseth it, saying thus of his
own person: I do not that good that I would
do, that is I would feel no fleshly stirring,
and that do I not; but I do that evil that I
* Rom. vii 23.
+ Rom. vii 25.

hate,* that is the sinful stirrings of my flesh
I hate and yet I feel them. Nevertheless
since it is so that I hate the wicked stirrings
of my flesh, and yet I feel them and often
delight in them against my will, they shall
not be rehearsed against me for damnation
as if I had done them. And why ? because
the corruption of this image of sin doth them
and not I.

So Saint Paul in his person comforteth all
souls that through grace are reformed in
faith, that they should not too much dread
the burden of this image with the unskilful
stirrings thereof, if it be so that they assent
not wilfully thereto. Nevertheless in this
point many souls that are reformed in troth
are oftentimes much tormented and troubled
in vain, as thus: when they have felt ‘fleshly
stirrings of pride or envy, of covetousness or
lechery, or of any other capital sin, they wit
not sometimes whether they assent thereto
or no, and that is no great wonder. For in
time of temptation a frail man’s thought is
so troubled and so overlaid that he hath no
clear sight nor freedom of himself, but is
taken often with liking unwarily and goeth
forth a great while before that he perceiveth
it. And therefore some fall into doubt and
dweret whether they sinned in time of
temptation or no.

As anent this point I say as methinketh,
that a soul may have assaying in this manner
whether that he assent or no. If it be so
that a man is stirred to any manner of sin, _
and the liking is so great in his fleshly feeling
that it troubleth his reason and as it were
with mastery occupieth the affection of the
soul, nevertheless he keepeth him, that he
followeth it not in deed, nor he would not
if he might, but rather is it painful to him to
feel the liking of that sin and fain he would
put it away if he might, and then when the
stirring is overpassed he is glad and well paid
that he is delivered of it; by this assay may he
wit that, were the liking never so great in the
fleshly feeling, he assented not nor sinned
not, namely deadly. Nevertheless a remedy
there is that is secure to such a simple soul
that is marred in itself and cannot help
itself: that he be not too bold in himself,
utterly weening that such fleshly stirrings
with liking are no sins, for he might so fall
into recklessness and into false security.
Also let him be not too dreadful nor too
simple in wit to deem them all as deadly
sins, nor as great venials, for neither is
sooth; but that he hold them all as sins and
great wretchedness of himself, and that he
have sorrow for them and be not too busy
to deem them neither deadly nor venial.
But if his conscience be greatly grieved let

Ch. 11] OF PERFECTION

him hastily go and show to his confessor in
general or in special such stirrings; and
namely every stirring that beginneth to
fasten any root in the heart and most occu-
pieth it, to draw it down to sin and worldly
vanity. And then, when he is thus shriven
generally or specially, let him trow stead-
fastly that they are forgiven and dispute no
more about them that are passed and forgiven
whether they were deadly or venial; but that
he be more busy to keep him better against
them that are coming. And if he do thus,
then may he come to rest in conscience.

But then some are so fleshly and so un-
cunning that they would see or feel or hear
their forgiveness of their sins as openly as
they might feel and see a bodily thing. And
forasmuch as they feel it not so, therefore
they fall. often in such dweres and doubts
of themselves, and may never come to rest,
and in that they are unwise, for faith goeth
before feeling. Our Lord said to a man that
was in the palsy when he healed him thus:
Son, trow steadfastly, thy sins are forgiven
thee.* He said not to him, See or feel how
thy sins are forgiven thee, for the forgiveness
of sins is done ghostly and unseeably through
grace of the Holy Ghost, but trow it. Right
on the same wise every man that will come
to rest in conscience, it behoveth him first,

* Mark ii 5.
209 T2

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

if he do that in him is, to trow forgiveness of
his sins without ghostly feeling. And if he
first trow it, he shall afterward through grace
feel it, and understand that it is so. Thus
saith the apostle: Unless ye first trow, ye may -
not understand.* Troth goeth before, and
understanding cometh after. The which
understanding, that I call the sight of God if
it be gracious, a soul may not have but
through great cleanness, as our Lord saith:
Blessed be clean of heart, for they shall see
God.t ‘That is, they shall see God, not with
their fleshly eye but with the inner eye, that
is the understanding, cleansed and lightened
through grace of the Holy Ghost to see sooth-
fastness. The which cleanness a soul may
not feel unless he have stable troth going
before, as the apostle saith: Our Lord
cleanseth the hearts of His chosen through
faith.t Therefore it is needful that a soul
trow first the reforming of himself made
through the sacrament of penance, though he
see it not, and that he dispose himself fully
to live rightfully and virtuously as his troth
asketh, so that he may after come to the sight
and to the reforming in feeling.

CHAPTER XII

THAT THIS IMAGE IS BOTH FAIR AND FOUL
WHILES IT IS IN THIS LIFE HERE, THOUGH
IT BE REFORMED. AND OF DIVERSITY OF
FEELINGS PRIVILY HAD BETWEEN THESE
SOULS THAT ARE REFORMED AND OTHER
THAT ARE NOT

FAIR is a man’s soul, and foul is a man’s soul.
Fair inasmuch as it is reformed in faith to the
likeness of God. But foul inasmuch as it is
mixed with fleshly feelings and unskilful
stirrings of this foul image of sin. Foul
without as it were a beast, fair within like
toanangel. Foul in feeling of the sensuality,
fair in troth of the reason. Foul for the
fleshly appetite, fair for the good will. Thus
fair and thus foul is a chosen soul, saying in
Holy Writ thus: J am black but I am fair and
shapely, ye daughters of Jerusalem, as the
tabernacles of cedar, as the skin of Solomon.*
That is, ye angels of heaven that are daughters
of the high Jerusalem, wonder not on me
nor despise me for my black shadow; for
though I be black because of my fleshly kind,
as is a tabernacle of cedar, nevertheless I
am full fair within as the skin of Solomon,
for I am reformed to the likeness of God.
* Cant! NA.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

By cedar is understood a reproved soul, the
which is a. tabernacle of the devil. By
Solomon is understood our Lord Jesus, for
He is peace and peaceable. By the skin of
Solomon is understood a blessed angel, in -
whom our Lord dwelleth and is hid, as is the
life hid in the skin of a quick body, and there-
fore is an angel likened to a skin. Then
may a chosen soul with meek trust in God
and gladness of heart say thus: Though I be
black because of my body of sin, as areproved
. soul that is one of the tabernacles of the fiend,
nevertheless I am within full fair through
troth and goodwill, like to an angel of heaven.
For so saith he in another place: Behold me
not that I am swart, for the sun hath defaced
me.* The sun maketh a skin swart only
without and not.within, and it betokeneth this
fleshly life. Therefore saith a chosen soul
thus: Reprove me not because I am swart,
for the swartness that I have is all without, of
touching and of bearing of this image of sin.
But it is nothing within. And therefore
soothly though it be so that a chosen soul
reformed in faith dwell in this body of sin,
and feel the same fleshly stirrings and use the
same bodily works as doth a tabernacle of
cedar, so far forth that in man’s doom there

that other, nevertheless within their souls

there is a full great diversity and in the sight

we shall deem no man as evil for that thing

and so unmighty for lacking of grace that he
may neither move hand nor foot to do any
good deed, nor to withstand, by displeasing
of will, the least stirring of sin when it
cometh, but he falleth down thereto as it were
a beast upon carrion. But a soul that is
reformed, though he use his fleshly wits and

fully rest in them, but he fleeth the rest as the

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

biting of an adder, and had leifer have his

is not borne in this image of sin as a sick man, `
though he feel it; but he beareth it. For

This was bodily fulfilled in the Gospel of

borne and laid in a litter and brought to our
Lord. And when our Lord saw him in
mischief, of His goodness He said to him:
Rise up and take thy bed and go into thy house.*
And so he did and was whole. And soothly
right as this man bore upon his back when
he was made whole the bed that before bare
him, right so it may be said ghostly that a soul
reformed in faith beareth this image of sin
the which bare him before. And therefore
be not adread too much of thy blackness that
thou hast of bearing of this image of sin.
But as against the shame and the discomfort

that thou hast of beholding of it, and also

CHAPTER XIII

OF THREE MANNER OF MEN, OF THE WHICH
SOME BE NOT REFORMED, AND SOME BE
REFORMED ONLY IN FAITH, AND SOME IN
FAITH AND IN FEELING

By this that I have said mayest thou see
that after divers parts of the soul are divers
states of men. Some men are not reformed
to the likeness of God, and some are reformed
only in faith, and some are reformed in faith
and in feeling. For thou shalt understand
that a soul hath two parts. That one is
* Eph. vi 16.

THE. SCALE : [Bk. 2

called the sensuality, and that is fleshly
feeling by the five outward wits, the which
is common to man and beast. Of the which
sensuality, when it is unskilfully and inordi- -
nately ruled, is made the image of sin, when
it is not ruled after reason, for then is the
sensuality sin. That other part is called
reason, and that is departed in two, in the
over part and in the nether. The over part
is likened to a man, for it should be master
and sovereign, and that is properly the image
of God. For by that only the soul knoweth
God and loveth Him. And the nether is
likened to a woman, for it should be buxom
to the other part of reason, as woman is
buxom to man. And that lieth in knowing
and ruling of earthly things, to use them
discreetly after need and to refuse them when
it is no need; for to have ever with it an eye
open to the over part of reason, with dread
and reverence to follow it. Now may I say
that a soul that liveth after the likings and
the lusts of his flesh, as it were an unskilful
beast, and neither hath knowing of God nor
desire to virtues nor to good living, but is all
blinded in pride, fretted with envy, overlaid
with covetousness, defiled with lechery and
other great sin, is not reformed to the like-
ness of God in faith. For it lieth and resteth
fully in the image of sin, that is the sensuality.
Another soul that dreadeth God and with-

Ch. 14] OF PERFECTION

standeth deadly stirrings of the sensuality
and followeth them not, but liveth skilfully
in ruling and governance of worldly things
and sets his intent and his will to please God
by his outward works, is reformed to the
likeness of God in faith. And though he
feel the same stirrings of sin that that other
doth, it shall not dis-ease him, for he resteth
not in them as doth that other. But another
soul that fleeth through grace all deadly
stirrings of the sensuality and venials also,
so far forth that he feeleth them not, is
reformed in feeling. For he followeth the
over part of reason in beholding of God and
ghostly things, as I shall tell thee afterward.

CHAPTER XIV

HOW MEN THAT BE IN SIN FORSHAPE THEM-
SELVES INTO THE LIKENESS OF DIVERS
BEASTS ; AND HOW THEY BE CALLED THE
LOVERS OF THE WORLD

A WRETCHED man is he then that knoweth
not the worthiness of his soul nor will not
know it; how it is the most worthy creature
that ever God made—save only an angel
whom it is like to—high above all other kind
bodily, to the which nothing may suffice as
full rest but only God. And therefore he
should nothing love nor like but Him only,

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

nor covet nor seek but how he might be
reformed to His likeness. But because he
knoweth not this, therefore he seeketh and
coveteth his rest and his liking outward in
bodily creatures worse than himself is.
Unkindly he doth, and unreasonably he
worketh that leaveth the sovereign good and
everlasting life that is God unsought, un-
loved, unknown and unworshipped, and
chooseth his rest and his bliss in a passing
delight of an earthly thing. Nevertheless,
this do all the lovers of this world that have
their joy and their bliss in this wretched life.
Some have it in pride and vainglory of them-
selves, so that, when they have lost the dread
of God, they travail and study night and day
how they may come to worship and praising
of the world, and make no force how it
might be, so that they might come thereto
and overpass all men either in clergy or in
craft, in name or in fame, in riches or in
reverence, in sovereignty and mastership, in
high state and lordship. Some men have
their rest in riches and in outrageous having
of earthly good, and they set their hearts so
fully to get it that they seek naught else but
how they might come thereto. Some have
their liking in fleshly lusts of gluttony and
lechery, and in other bodily uncleanness, and
some in one thing and some in another.
And thus. wretchedly these do that thus

Ch. 14] - OF PERFECTION

forshape themselves from the worthiness of
man, and turn themselves into divers beasts’
likeness.

The proud man is turned into a lion for
pride, for he would be dreaded and wor-
shipped of all men, and that no man withstand
the fulfilling of his fleshly will, neither in
word nor in deed. And if any man would
let his misproud will, he waxeth fell and
wroth, and will be wreaked of him as a lion
wreaketh him on a little beast. The man
that doth this is no man, for he doth un-
reasonably to the kind of man, and so he is
turned and transformed into a lion. Envious
and angry men through wrath and envy are
turned into hounds, so that they bark against
their even-Christians and bite them by
wicked and malicious words, and grieve them
that have not trespassed with wrongful
deeds, harming them in body and in soul
against God’s bidding. Some men are
forshapen into asses that are slow to the
service of God, and evil willed to do any
good deed to their even-Christians. They
are ready enough to run for worldly profit
and for earthly worship, or for pleasure of an
earthly man. But for ghostly meed and for
help of their own souls, or for the worship of
God, they are soon irked. They will
naught thereof, and, if they do aught, they
go but a pace and with froward will. Some

THE SCALE à [Bk. 2

are turned into swine, for they are so blind
in wit and so beastly in manners that they
have no dread of God, but follow only the
lusts and likings of their flesh, and have no
regard to the honesty of man, nor rule them-
selves after the bidding of reason, nor
refrain the unskilful stirrings of their fleshly
kind, but as soon as a fleshly stirring cometh
of sin, they are ready to fall thereto and
follow as swine do. Some men are turned
into wolves that live by ravening, as false
covetous men do, that through mastery and
overleading, rob their even-Christians of
their worldly goods. Some men are turned
into foxes, as false men and deceivable men
that live in treachery and guile. All these
and many other more that live not in dread
of God but break His commandments, for-
shape themselves from the likeness of God
and make them like to beasts, yea, and worse
than beasts, for they are like to the fiend of
hell. And therefore soothly these men that
live thus, if they be not reformed when the
hour of death cometh and their souls depart
from their bodies, then shall their eye be
opened that is now stopped with sin, and
then shall they feel and find the pain of their
wretchedness that they lived in here. And
forasmuch as the image of God was not
reformed through the sacrament of penance
in them, neither in faith nor in feeling here

Ch. 15] OF PERFECTION

in this life, they shall be as cursed cast out
from the blessed face of our Creator, and
they shall be damned with the devil into
the deepness of hell; there to be without end.
Thus saith Saint John in the Apocalypse: To
proud men and to mistrowing men, to cursed
and to manslayers, to lecherous and to covetous,
to poisoners and to worshippers of idols, and
to all false liars their dole* shall be with the
devil in the pit of hell burning with fire and
brimstone.t If the lovers of this world would
oft think on this, how all this world shall pass
and draw to an end, and how all wicked love
shall be hard punished, they should within
short time loathe all worldly lusts that now
they most like, and they should lift up their
heart to love God, and they would busily
seek and travail how they might be reformed
to His likeness, ere that they pass hence.

CHAPTER XV

HOW LOVERS OF THIS WORLD UNABLE THEM IN
DIVERS MANNERS TO THE REFORMING OF
THEIR OWN SOUL

BUT now say some of them thus: “ I would
fain love God and be a good man and forsake
the love of the world if I might, but I have
no grace thereto. If I had the same grace
* Dole—+.e., pain, punishment.
F Apoc. xxi 8.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

that a good man hath, I should do as he
doth; but because I have it not, therefore I
may not, and so it is not to me to wit, but I
am excused.” Unto these men I say thus:
Sooth it is as they say that they have no
grace, and therefore they lie still in their sin
and may not rise out. But that availeth
them not against God, for it is their own
default. They unable them by divers ways
so much that the light of grace may not shine
in them nor rest in their hearts. For some
men are so froward that they will not have
grace, nor will they be good men. For they
wit well that if they should be good men,
they must needs forbear the great liking and
lust of this world that they have in earthly
things; and that they will not. For they
think it is so sweet that they will not forego
it. And also they must needs take works of
penance, as in fasting, waking, praying and
doing many other good deeds, in chastising
of their flesh and in withdrawing of their
fleshly will, and that they may not do.
For it is made so sharp and so dreadful to
their thinking that they dread and loathe
to think upon it, and so cowardly and
wretchedly dwell they still in their sin.
Some would have grace as it seemeth, and
begin to able them thereto, but their will is
wondrous weak. For as soon as any stirring
of sin cometh, though it be contrary to the

Ch. 15] OF PERFECTION

bidding of God, they fall anon thereto, for
they are so bound through custom by oft
falling and oft assenting to sin before, that
they think it impossible for them to with-
stand it, and so the feigned hardness of
performing weakeneth their will and smiteth
it down again. Some also feel stirrings of
grace, as when they have biting of conscience
for their evil living, to the end that they
should leave it. But that is so painful to
them and heavy, that they will not suffer it
nor abide it, but they flee therefrom and forget
it if they may; so far forth that they seek
liking and comfort outward in fleshly
creatures, so that they should not feel this
biting of conscience within their soul. And
moreover some men are so blind and so
beastly that they ween that there is none
other life but this, nor that there is no soul
other than of a beast, and that the soul of a
man dieth with the body as the soul of a
beast. And therefore they say, Eat we,
drink we, and make we merry here, for of
this life we are secure: we see none other
‘heaven. Soothly such are some wretches
that say thus in their heart, though they say
it not with their mouth. Of the which men
the prophet saith thus: The unwise man said
in his heart, there 1s no God.*

This unwise man is every wretched man

that liveth, and loveth sin and chooseth the

CHAPTER XVI

A LITTLE COUNSEL HOW LOVERS OF THIS WORLD
SHOULD DO, IF THEY WOULD BE REFORMED
IN THEIR SOUL BEFORE THEIR PARTING
HENCE

THESE men, though they wit well that they
are out of grace and in deadly sin, have no
care nor sorrow nor thought therefore. But
they make fleshly mirth and worldly solace as
much as they may, and the further they be
from grace the more mirth they make. And
perchance some hold them well paid that
they have no grace, that they may, as it were,
the more freely and fully follow the liking of
fleshly lusts; as though God were asleep and
might not see them. And this is one of the
most defaults that may be. And thus by
their own frowardness they stop the light of
grace from their own soul that it may not
rest therein; the which grace, as much as in
it is, shineth to all ghostly creatures, ready
to enter in where it is received, as the sun
shineth over all creatures bodily where it is
not letted. Thus saith Saint John in the
Gospel: The light of grace shineth in darkness,
that is to men’s hearts that are dark through
sin; but the darkness taketh it not.* That is,
* Johni 5.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

these blind hearts receive not the gracious
light nor have not the profit of it. But
right as a blind man is all belapped with the
light of the sun when he standeth therein,
and yet feeleth he it not, nor hath no profit
thereof to go thereby, right so ghostly a soul
blinded with deadly sin is all belapped with
this ghostly light, and yet he is never the
better, for he is blind and will not see nor
know his blindness. And that is one of the
most lettings of grace, that a wretched man
will not acknowledge his own blindness for
pride of himself, or else if he know it, he
chargeth it not but maketh mirth and game
as he were over all secure.

Therefore unto all these men that are
blinded and bounded with the love of this
world and are foul forshapen from the fair-
ness of man, I say and counsel that they think
on their soul, and that they able them unto
grace as much as they may. And that may
they do upon this wise, if they will. When
they feel themselves out of grace and over-
laid with deadly sin, that they then think
what peril and what mischief it is to them to
be put out of grace and departed from God
as they be; for there is nothing that holdeth
them from the pit of hell that they should
not right soon fall therein, but a bare single
thread of this bodily life whereby they hang.
What lightlier may be lost than a single

Ch. 16] OF PERFECTION

thread may be broken in two? — For when
the breath stoppeth in their body—and that
may lightly befall—their soul shall pass forth
and anon be in hell without end. And if
they would think thus, they should quake
and shake for dread of the rightful dooms of
God and of the hard punishing of sin; and
they should make sorrow and mourn for
their sin and for lacking of grace, and then
should they cry and pray that they might
have grace. And if they did thus, then
should grace fall in and put out darkness and
hardness of heart and wickedness of will, and
give them might and strength to forsake the
false love of this world as much as is deadly
sin. For there is no soul so far from God
through wickedness of will in deadly sin,
that he may not through grace be righted
and reformed to cleanness of living, if he will
bow his will to God with meekness to amend
his life, and heartily ask grace and forgiveness
of Him, and excuse our Lord and fully
accuse himself. For Holy Writ, that is our
Lord, saith: J will not the death of a sinner,
but I will more that he be turned to Me and
live.* For our Lord wills that the most
froward man that liveth forshapen through
sin, if he turn his will and ask grace, that he
be reformed to His likeness.

x Ezech. xxxili LI.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

CHAPTER XVII

THAT REFORMING IN FAITH AND IN FEELING
MAY NOT SUDDENLY BE GOTTEN, BUT BY
GRACE AND MUCH BODILY AND GHOSTLY
TRAVAIL IN LENGTH OF TIME

Tuis reforming is in faith, as I have before
said, that lightly may be had. But after this
cometh reforming in faith and in feeling,
that may not lightly be gotten, but through
long travail and much business. For re-
forming in faith is common to all chosen
souls, though they be in the lowest degree of
charity. But reforming in feeling is specially
of those souls that may come to the state of
perfection, and that may not suddenly be
had. But after great plenty of grace and
much ghostly travail a soul may come
thereto; and that is when he is first helped
of his ghostly sickness, and when all bitter
passion and fleshly lusts and other old
feelings are burnt out of the heart with fire
of desire, and other new gracious feelings are
brought in with burning love and ghostly
light. Then right nigh nigheth a soul to
perfection and to reforming in feeling.

For sooth it is, right as a man is brought
nigh to death through bodily sickness,
though he receive a medicine by which he is

Ch. 17] OF PERFECTION

restored and secure of his life, he may not
therefore at once rise up and go to work as
an whole man may; for the feebleness of his
body holdeth him down, that he must abide
a good while and keep him well with medi-
cines, and diet him by measure after the
teaching of a leech, till he may fully recover
bodily health. Right so ghostly he that is
brought to ghostly death through deadly sin,
though he through medicine of the sacrament
of penance be restored to life that he shall not
be damned, nevertheless he is not anon
whole of all his passions and of all his fleshly
desires, nor able to contemplation. But it
behoveth him to abide a great while and take
good keep of himself, and rule him so that
he might recover full health of soul, for he
shall languor a great while ere that he be
fully whole. Nevertheless if he take medi-
cines of a good leech, and use them in time
with measure and discretion, he shall much
the sooner be restored to his ghostly strength,
and come to reforming in feeling. For
reforming in faith is the lowest state of all
chosen souls, for beneath that they might
not well be; but reforming in feeling is the
highest state in this life that the soul may
come to. But from the lowest to the highest
a soul may not suddenly start, no more than
a man that will climb upon a ladder high and
setteth his foot upon the lowest rung may

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

at the next fly up to the highest; but it
behoveth him to go by process one after
another till he may come to the topmost.
Right so is it ghostly; no man is made
suddenly sovereign in grace, but through
long exercise and sly working a soul may come
thereto, namely when He helpeth and teach-
eth a wretched soul in whom all grace lieth.
For without special help and inwardly
teaching of Him may no soul come thereto.

CHAPTER XVIII

THE CAUSE WHY SO FEW SOULS, COMPARED
WITH THE MULTITUDE OF THE OTHERS,
COME TO THIS REFORMING IN FAITH AND
IN FEELING

BuT now sayest thou, since our Lord is so
courteous of His goodness, and of His
gracious gifts so free, wonder it is then that
so few souls, as it seemeth in regard of the
multitude of others, may come to this
reforming in feeling. It seemeth that He
were estranged, and that is not sooth; or
that He took no regard of His creatures, the
which by taking of faith are become His
servants. Unto this I may answer and say
as methinketh that one reason is this. Many
that are reformed in faith set not their hearts
to profit in grace nor to seek none higher

Ch. 18] OF PERFECTION

estate of good living, through busy travail
in praying and thinking and other bodily and
ghostly working; but they think it enough
for them to keep themselves out of deadly
sin, and to stand still in that plight that
they are in. For they say that it is enough
for them to be saved and have the least
degree in heaven; they will covet no more.

Thus perchance do some of the chosen
souls that in the world lead active lives; and
that is little wonder of them. For they are
so occupied in worldly business that needeth
to be done, that they may not fully set their
hearts to profit in ghostly working. And
nevertheless it is perilous to them, for they
fall out and in all day, and are now up now
down, and may not come to the stableness of
. good living. But other men and women
that are free from worldly business if they
will, and may have their needful sustenance
without great bodily business—as religious
men and women specially may that bind
themselves to the state of perfection by
taking of religion, and other men also in
secular state that have much reason in great
kindly wit, and might if they would dispose
them to come to much grace—these men
are more to blame, for they stand still as they
were idle and will not profit in grace, nor no
further seek to come to the love and the
knowing of God.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

For soothly it is perilous to a soul that is
reformed only in faith, and will no more seek
profit nor give him busily to ghostly travail:
for so he may lightly lose that which he hath,
and fall again to deadly sin. Fora soul while
it is in the flesh may not stand still alway in
one state; for it is either profiting in grace
or pairing* in sin. For it fareth by him as
it doth by a man that were drawn out of a
pit, who when he were up would no further
go than the pit’s brink. Soothly he were a
much fool, for a little puff of wind or an
inwardly stirring of himself should soon cast
him down again worse than he was before.
Nevertheless if he flee from the brink as far
as he may and go forth on the earth, then
though there come a great storm he is the
more secure, for he falleth not in the pit.
Right so ghostly is he that is drawn out of the
pit of sin through reforming of faith, who
when he is out of deadly sin thinketh himself
secure enough, and therefore will not profit
but hold him still as he is by the pit’s brink
as near as he may. Soothly he is not wise;
for at the least temptation of the enemy or
of his flesh he falleth into sin again. But
nevertheless if he flee from the pit, that is if
he set his heart fully to come to more grace
and to travail busily how he may come

* Profiting—7.e., progressing; pairing—i.e., be-
coming worse, cf. impair.

Ch. 18] OF PERFECTION

thereto, and give him heartily to praying,
thinking, and doing other good works,
though great temptations rise against him,
he falleth not lightly to deadly sin again.

And soothly it is wonder to me, that since
grace is so good and profitable, why a man
when he hath but a little thereof—yea so
little that he might scarce have less—will
say, “ Hold! I will no more of this, for I
have enough.” When I see a worldly man,
though he have of worldly goods much more
than he needeth, yet he will never say,
“ Hold! I have enough, I will no more of
this.” But he will covet more and more,
and travail with all his wits and his mights,
and never will stint of his covetousness till
he may have no more. Much more then
should a chosen soul covet ghostly good that
is everlasting and maketh a soul blessed.
And he never should cease of his coveting
if he did well, get what he might. For he
that most coveteth most shall have; and
soothly if he did thus, he should profit and
wax in grace greatly.
