THE SCALE [Bk. 2

CHAPTER XIX
ANOTHER CAUSE ALSO OF THE SAME. AND HOW

WILFUL BODILY CUSTOMS INDISCREETLY |

REGARDED AND USED, SOMETIMES HINDER
SOULS FROM FEELING OF MORE GRACE

ANOTHER reason is this. Some men that are
reformed in faith at the beginning of their
turning to God, set themselves in a certain
manner of doing, whether it be bodily or
ghostly, and think ever to keep that manner
of working forth, and not to change it for
none other through grace, though it were
better. For they ween that that doing
should be best for them always to hold; and
therefore they rest them therein, and through
custom they bind them so thereto, that when
they have fulfilled it they think them won-
derly eased, for they ween that they have done
a great thing for God. And perchance if it
fall that they be letted from their custom,
though it be for a skilful cause, they be heavy
and angry and have troubling of conscience,
as if they had done a great deadly sin.
These men hinder themselves somewhat
from feeling of more grace, for they set their
perfection in a bodily work, and so they
make an end in the midst of the way, where
none end is.

Ch. 19] OF PERFECTION

For why, bodily customs that men use in
the beginning are good, but they are but
means and ways leading a soul to perfection;
and therefore he that setteth his perfection
in a bodily work or in a ghostly work that
he feeleth in the beginning of his turning to
God, and will no further seek but ever rest
therein, he hindereth himself greatly. For
that is a simple craft wherein a prentice is
ever alike wise, who can on the first day as
much of it as he can thirty winters after; or
else if the craft be good and subtle, he is of
a dull wit or else of an evil will that profiteth
not therein. But then it is sooth that of all
crafts that are, the service of God is the most
sovereign and the most subtle, the highest
and hardest to come to the perfection of it;
and also it is the most profitable and most of
Winning to him that may soothfastly per-
form it. And therefore it seemeth that the
prentices of it that are ever alike far forth in
learning, either they are dull-witted or else
evil-willed. I reprove not these customs
that men use in the state of beginning,
whether they be bodily or ghostly, for I say
that they are full good and speedful for
them to use. But I would that they held
them not but as a way and an entry towards
ghostly feeling, and that they used them as a
convenable means till better come, and that
in using of them they coveted after better.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

And then if better come that were more
ghostly and that draweth more the thought
from fleshliness and from sensuality and
vain imagination, if that should be letted —
because of their customs, then let them leave
their custom, when it may be left without
slander* or dis-ease of other men, and follow
that they feel. But if neither custom let the
other, then shall they use both if they may.
I mean not of customs needful through bond
of law, or of rule, or of penance, but other
custom wilfully taken. Thus teacheth us
the prophet in the psalter: Soothly the
bringer of the law shall give blessing, and they
shall go from virtue unto virtue, and God of
Gods shall be seen in Sion.t| ‘The bringer of
the law, that is our Lord Jesus Christ, shall
give His blessing; that is, He shall give His
grace to His chosen souls, calling them from
sin and righting them by good works to His
likeness; through which grace they shall
profit and wax from virtue to virtue till they
come to Sion, that is till they come to con-
templation, in the which they shall see God
of Gods, that is, they shall see that there is
naught but one God.

* Slander—7.e., scandal.
t Ps. Ixxxiii 8.

Ch. 20] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XX

HOW WITHOUT MUCH BODILY AND GHOSTLY
BUSINESS, AND WITHOUT MUCH GRACE AND
MEEKNESS, SOULS MAY NOT BE REFORMED
IN FEELING, NOR KEEP THEREIN AFTER
THEY ARE COME THERETO

Now sayest thou, since it is so that reforming
in faith is so low and so perilous to rest in,
because of dread of falling again, and reform-
ing in feeling is so high and so secure, whoso
might come thereto: then covetest thou to
wit what manner of travail were most speed-
ful to use by which a man might profit and
come thereto, or if there were any certain
travail or special deed by which a man might
come to that grace and that reforming in
feeling. As unto this I say thus: thou
wottest well that what man or woman will
dispose him to come to cleanness of heart
and to feeling of grace, it behoveth him to
have much travail and great fighting in will
and in work lastingly against wicked stirrings
of all the capital sins. Not only against
pride or envy, but against all others, with all
the species that come out of them, as I have
said before in the first writing. For why,
passions and fleshly desires let the cleanness
of the heart and peace in conscience. And

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

it behoveth him also to travail to get all
virtues; not only chastity and abstinence, but
also patience and mildness, charity and
meekness, and all the others. And this may
not be done by one manner of work, but by
divers works and many, after sundry dis- -
positions of men. As now praying, now
thinking, now working some good works,
now assaying themselves in divers wise; in
hunger, in thirst, in cold, in suffering of
shame and despite if need be, and in other
bodily dis-ease for love of virtue and sooth-
fastness. This knowest thou well, for this
thou readest in every book that teacheth
of good living. Thus saith every man that
will stir men’s souls to the love of God. And
so it seemeth that there is no special travail
nor certain deed through which a soul might
come to that grace; but principally through
grace of our Lord Jesus, and by many deeds
and great in all that he may do, and yet all is
little enough.

And one reason may be this: for since our
Lord Jesus Himself is special master of this
craft, and He is special leech of ghostly
sickness, for without Him all is naught, it
is therefore reasonable that after what He
teacheth and stirreth, so a man follow and
work. But he is a simple master that cannot
teach his disciple while he is in learning but
one lesson alway, and he is an unwise leech

Ch. 20] OF PERFECTION

that ever by one medicine will heal all sores.
Therefore our Lord Jesus, that is so wise and
so good, to show His wisdom and. His
goodness, teacheth divers lessons to His
disciples, after that they profit in their
learning, and giveth to divers souls divers
medicines after the feeling of their sickness.
Also another skill is this: if there were any
one certain deed by which a soul might come
to the perfect love of God, then should a
man ween that he might come thereto by his
own work and through his own travail, as a
merchant cometh to his meed by his own
travail only, and by his own work. Nay, it
is not so ghostly in the love of God. For he
that will serve God wisely and come to the
perfect love of God, he shall covet to have
none other meed but Him only. But then
to have Him may no creature deserve only
by his own travail. For though a man might
travail as much bodily and ghostly as all
creatures might that ever were, he might not
deserve only by his works to have God to
his meed; for He is sovereign bliss and
endless goodness, and passeth without com-
parison all men’s deserts, and therefore He
may not be got by no man’s special works,
as bodily meed may. For He is free, and
giveth Himself where He will and when He
will, neither for this work nor for that, nor
in this time nor after this time. For though

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

a soul work all that he can and may all his
lifetime, perfect love of Jesus shall he never
have, till our Lord Jesus will freely give it.
Nevertheless on that other side I say also, I
think that He giveth it not unless a man
travail all that he can and may, yea till he
thinketh that he ntay no more; or else be in
full will thereto if he might. And so it
seemeth that neither grace only without full
working of a soul that in it is, nor working
alone without grace, bringeth a soul to
reforming in feeling, which reforming stand-
eth in perfect love and charity. But the
one joined to the other, that is grace joined
to working, bringeth into a soul the blessed
feeling of perfect love; the which grace may
not rest fully but on meek souls that be full
of the dread of God. Therefore may I say,
he that hath no meekness nor doth his
business may not come to this reforming in
feeling. He hath not full meekness that
cannot feel of himself soothfastly as he is;
as thus, he that doth all the good deeds that
he can, as in fasting, waking, wearing of the
hair, and all other suffering of bodily penance,
or doth all the outward works of mercy to his
even-Christian, or else inward, as praying,
weeping, sighing, and thinking: if he rest
ever in them and lean so much to them, and
regardeth them so greatly in his own sight
that he presumeth of his own deserts, and

Ch. 20] OF PERFECTION

thinketh himself ever rich and good, holy
and virtuous; soothly as long as he feeleth
thus, he is not meek enough. Nor though
he say or think that all that he doth is of
God’s gift and not of himself, he is not yet
meek enough; for he may not make himself
yet naked of all his good deeds, nor make
himself poor soothfastly in spirit, nor feel
himself naught as he is. And soothly until
a soul feelably through grace can naught him-
self and bare him from all the good deeds that
he doth through beholding of the soothfast-
ness of Jesus, he is not perfectly meek. For
what is meekness but soothfastness ? Soothly
naught else. And therefore he that through
grace may see Jesus, how that He doth all,
and himself doth right naught but suffereth
Jesus to work in him what He liketh, he is
meek. But this is full hard and as it were
impossible and unreasonable to a man that
worketh all by man’s reason, and seeth no
further to do many good deeds, and then to
arrette* all to Jesus, and set himself at
naught. Nevertheless whoso might have a
ghostly sight of soothfastness, he should think
it full true and full reasonable for to do so.
And soothly he that hath this sight shall
never do the less, but he shall be stirred to
travail bodily and ghostly much the more
and with the better will. And this may be
* Arrette—7.e., account.
241 K2

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

one cause why some men _peradventure
swink and sweat and pine their wretched
body with outrageous penance all their
lifetime, and are ever saying orisons and
psalters and many other beads, and yet they
may not come to the ghostly feeling of the
love of God—as it seemeth some do in short
time with less pain—for they have not that
meekness that I speak of. Also on the other
side I say, he that doth not his business may
not come to the feeling of grace. He doth
not his business that thinketh thus: Whereto
should I travail? Whereto should I pray
or think, wake or fast, or do any other bodily
penance to come to such grace, since it may
not be gotten nor had except of the free gift
of Jesus? Therefore I will abide in fleshli-
ness as I am, and right naught do of such
works bodily nor ghostly until He give it.
For if He will give it He asketh no working
of me. Whatso that I do, and how little I
do, I shall have it. And if He will not give
it, travail I never so fast therefore, I get it
never the sooner. He that sayeth thus may
not come to this reforming, for he draweth
himself wilfully to idleness of the fleshhead,
and unableth him to the gift of grace; inas-
much as he putteth from him both inward
working that standeth in lasting desire and
in longing to Jesus, and outward working by
travail of his body in outward deeds. So may

Ch. 21] OF PERFECTION

he not have it. Therefore I say that he that
hath not true meekness nor full hearty
business; either inward only by great fervour
and lasting desire and busy prayer and
thought in God, or else both inward and
outward, he may not come to this ghostly
reforming of his image.

CHAPTER XXI

AN ENTRY HOW A SOUL THAT WILL COME TO
THIS REFORMING SHALL HAVE HER IN
MEANING AND WORKING, BY EXAMPLE OF
A PILGRIM GOING TO JERUSALEM; AND
OF TWO MANNER OF MEEKNESS

NEVERTHELESS for thou covetest to have some
manner working by which thou mightest be
rather nigh to that reforming, I shall say
thee as methinketh by the grace of our Lord
Jesus, the shortest and readiest help that I
know in this working. And how that shall
be, I shall tell thee by an example of a good
pilgrim upon this wise.

There was a man that would go to Jeru-
salem, and because he knew not the way he
came to another man that he hoped knew
better the way thither, and asked whether he
might come to that city. That other man
said to him that he might not come thither
without great dis-ease and much travail,

THE SCALE . [Bk. 2

because the way is long and perilous and full
of great thieves and robbers, and many
other lettings there be that fall to a man in
the going. And also there are many several
ways as it seemeth leading thitherward; but
men all day are slain and despoiled and may
not come to that place that they covet.
Nevertheless there is one way which, whoso
taketh it and holdeth it, he would undertake
that he should come to the city of Jerusalem,
and he should never lose his life nor be slain,
nor die for default. He should often be
robbed and evil beaten and suffer much
dis-ease in the going, but his life should be
safe. Then said the pilgrim: So that I may
have my life safe and come to that place that
I covet, I charge not what mischief I suffer
in going; and therefore say me what thou
wilt, and soothly I behote* to do after thee.
That other man answereth and saith thus:
Lo, I set thee in the right way. This is the
way, and that thou keep the learning that I
teach thee. Whatsoever thou hearest, seest
or feelest, that should let thee in the way,
abide not with it wilfully, tarry not for it
restfully, behold it not, like it not, dread it
not; but ever go forth in thy way, and think
thou wouldest be at Jerusalem. For that
thou covetest, and that thou desirest, and
naught else but that. And if men rob thee
* Behote—i.e., promise.

Ch. 21] OF PERFECTION

and despoil thee, beat thee, scorn thee,
despise thee, strive not again if thou wilt have
thy life, but hold thee with the harm that thou
hast and go forth as if naught were, that thou
take no more harm. And also if men would
tarry thee with tales and feed thee with
leasings, to draw thee to mirths and to leave
thy pilgrimage, make a deaf ear and answer
not again, and say naught else but that thou
wouldest be at Jerusalem. And if men
proffer thee gifts and will make thee rich
with worldly good, attend not to them;
think ever on Jerusalem. And if thou wilt
hold this way and do that I have said, I
undertake thy life that thou shalt not be
slain, but thou shalt come to that place that
thou covetest.

Ghostly to our purpose, Jerusalem is as
much as to say a sight of peace, and beto-
keneth contemplation in perfect love of
God. For contemplation is naught else but
a sight of God, which is very peace. Then
if thou covet to come to this blessed sight of
very peace and be a true pilgrim to Jeru-
salem-ward, though it be so that I were
never there, nevertheless as far forth as I
can I shall set thee in the way thitherward.
The beginning of the high way in the which
thou shalt go is reforming in faith, grounded
meekly in the faith and in the laws of Holy
Church, as I have said before; for trust

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

securely, that though thou have sinned here-
before, if thou be now reformed by the sacra-
ment of penance after the law of Holy
Church, thou art in the right way. Now
then, since thou art in the secure way, if thou
wilt speed in thy going and make good
journeys, it behoveth thee to hold these two
things often in thy mind: meekness and love.
And that is; I am naught, I have naught, I
covet naught, but one. Thou shalt have the
meaning of these words in thine intent and
in habit of thy soul lastingly, though thou
have them not specially in thy thought, for
that needeth not. Meekness saith, I am
naught, I have naught. Love saith, I covet
naught but one, and that is Jesus. These
two strings, well fastened with the mind of
Jesus, make good accord in the harp of the
soul, when they be craftily touched with the
finger of reason; for the lower thou smitest
upon the one, the higher soundeth the other.
The less thou feelest that thou art or that
thou hast of thyself through meekness, the
more thou covetest to have of Jesus in desire
of love. I mean not only of that meekness
that a soul feeleth in the sight of his own sin,
for frailness and wretchedness of this life,
or of the worthiness of his even-Christian.
For though this meekness be soothfast and
medicinable, nevertheless it is boisterous
and fleshly as in regard, not clean nor soft nor

Ch. 21] OF PERFECTION

lovely. But I mean also this meekness that
the soul feeleth through grace, in sight and
beholding of the endless being and the
wonderful goodness of Jesus. And if thou
may not see it yet with thy ghostly eye, that
thoutrowit. For through sight of His being,
either in full faith or in feeling, thou shalt
hold thyself not only the most wretch that
is, but also as naught in substance of thy
soul, though thou hadst never done sin.
And that is lovely meekness; for in regard of
Jesus that is soothfastly all, thou art right
naught. And also that thou think that thou
hast right naught, but art as a vessel that
standeth ever empty as if naught were
therein, as of thyself. For do thou never
so many good deeds outward or inward, till
thou have and feel that thou hast the love of
Jesus, thou has right naught.. For with that
precious liquor only, may thy soul be ful-
filled, and with none other. And forasmuch
as that thing alone is so precious and so
worthy, therefore what thou hast or what
thou dost, hold it as naught to rest in, with-
out the sight of the love of Jesus. Cast it
all behind thee and forget it, that thou
mightest have that that is best of all. Right
as a true pilgrim going to Jerusalem leaveth
behind him house and land, wife and child,
and maketh himself poor and bare from all
that he hath, that he might go lightly without

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

letting: right so if thou wilt be a ghostly
pilgrim, thou shalt make thyself naked from
all that thou hast, that are both good deeds
and bad, and cast them all behind thee, that
thou be so poor in thine own feeling that
there is nothing of thine own working that
- thou wilt lean upon restingly, but ever desir-
ing more grace and love, and ever seeking
the ghostly presence of Jesus. And if thou
do thus, then shalt thou set in thine heart
fully that thou wouldst be at Jerusalem and
at none other place but there. And that is,
thou shalt set in thine heart wholly and fully
that thou wouldest nothing have but the love
of Jesus, and the ghostly sight of Him as He
will show Him; for to that only art thou made
and bought, and that is thy beginning and
thine ending, thy joy and thy bliss. And
therefore whatsoever thou have, be thou
never so rich in other deeds bodily and
ghostly, unless thou have that, and know
and feel that thou hast it, hold that thou hast
right naught. Print well this reason in the
meaning of thy heart, and cleave sadly
thereto; and it shall save thee from all perils
in thy going, that thou shalt never perish,
and it shall save thee from thieves and
robbers, the which I call unclean spirits, that
though they spoil thee and beat thee through
divers temptations, thy life shall ever be safe.
And shortly if thou keep it as I shall tell thee, .

Ch. 21] OF PERFECTION

thou shalt escape all perils and mischiefs and
come to the city of Jerusalem within short time.

Now thou art in the way and knowest
what the place is called whither thou shalt
draw to. Begin then to go in thy journey.
Thy forthgoing is naught else but ghostly
working, and bodily also when need is;
which thou shalt use by discretion upon this
wise. What work that it be that thou shalt
do after the degree and the state that thou
standest in bodily or ghostly, if it help this
gracious desire that thou hast to love Jesus,
and make it more easy, more whole and
more mighty to all virtues and to all goodness,
that work hold I best; be it preaching, be it
thinking, be it reading, be it working. And
as long as that work strengtheneth most thy
heart and thy will to the love of Jesus, and
furthest draweth thine affection and thy
thought from worldly vanity, it is good to
use it. And if it be so that, through use, the
savour of that work letteth, and thou thinkest
that another work savoureth thee more, and
thou feelest more grace in another, take
another and leave that. For though thy
desire and the yearning of thine heart to
Jesus should be ever unchangeable, neverthe-
less thy ghostly works, that thou shalt use
in praying or thinking to feed and nourish
thy desire, may be divers, and may well be
changed after that thou feelest thee disposed

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

through grace in applying of thine own heart.
For it fareth by works and by desire as it
doth by sticks and by a fire. For the more
sticks are laid to the fire, the greater is the
fire; right so the more divers ghostly working
that a man hath in his thought to keep whole
his desire, the mightier and more burning
shall his desire be to God. And therefore
look wisely what work thou canst best do,
and that most helpeth thee to save whole this
desire to Jesus, if thou be free, and art not
bound save by the common law; and do that.
Bind thee not to wilful customs unchange-
ably, that should let the freedom of thine
heart to love Jesus if grace would visit thee
specially. For I shall tell thee which customs
are ever good and needful to be kept. Lo,
such custom is ever good to hold that
standeth in getting of virtue and letting of
sin, and that custom should never be left,
for thou shalt ever be meek, patient, sober and
chaste if thou do well, and so of all other
virtues. But the custom of another thing
that letteth a better, it is good to leave when
time is, where a man may. As thus, ifa man
have in custom to say thus-many beads, or
to think this manner of thought this long
time, or to wake or kneel thus long, or any”
other such bodily deed, this custom he may
leave sometimes when reasonable cause
letteth, or else if more grace come otherwise.

Ch. 22] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XXII

OF TARRYINGS AND TEMPTATIONS BY THEIR
GHOSTLY ENEMIES THAT SOULS FEEL IN
THEIR GHOSTLY KNOWING AND GOING
TO JERUSALEM; AND OF REMEDIES AGAINST
THEM

Now thou art in the way, and wottest how
thou shalt go. Now beware of enemies that
will be busy to let thee if they may. For
their intent is to put out of thine heart that
desire and the longing that thou hast to the
love of Jesus, and to drive thee home again
to the love of worldy vanities, for there is
nothing that grieveth them so much. These
enemies are principally fleshly desires and
vain dreads, that rise out of thy heart through
corruption of thy fleshly kind, and would let
thy desire of the love of God, that they might
fully and restfully occupy thine heart; these
are thy next enemies. Also other enemies
there be, as unclean spirits that are busy with
sleights and wiles to deceive thee. But one
remedy shalt thou have that I said before;
what so ever it be that they say, trow them
not, but hold forth thy way, and only desire
the love of Jesus. Answer ever thus: I am
naught, I have naught, I covet naught, but
only the love of Jesus. If thine enemies say
to thee first thus, by stirrings in heart that

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

thou art not shriven aright, or that there is
some old sin hid in thy heart that thou
knowest not, nor ‘never were shriven aright,
and therefore thou must turn home again and
leave thy desire, and go shrive thee better:
trow not this saying, for it is false, for thou
art shriven. Trust securely that thou art
in the way, and needest no more ransacking
of shrift for that which is past. Hold forth
thy way and think on Jerusalem. Also if
they say that thou art not worthy to have the
love of God, whereto shalt thou covet that
thou might not have, nor art not worthy
thereto; trow them not but go forth and say
thus: “Not because I am worthy, but
because I am unworthy, therefore would I
love God; for, if I had it, that should make
me worthy. And since I was made thereto,
though I should never have it, yet will I
covet it, and therefore will I pray and think
that I might get it.” And then if thine
enemies see that thou beginnest to wax bold
and well willed to thy work, they begin to
wax afraid of thee. Nevertheless they will
not cease of tarrying thee whenever they
may, as long as thou art going in the way,
what with dread and menacing on the one
side, what with flattering and false pleasing
on the other side, to make thee break thy
purpose and turn home again. They will
say thus: If thou hold forth thy desire to

Ch, 22] OF PERFECTION

Jesus so fully travailing as thou beginnest,
thou shalt fall into sickness or into fantasies,
or into frenzies, as thou seest that some do;
or thou shalt fall into poverty and bodily
mischief, and no man shall well help thee;
or thou might fall into privy temptations of
the fiend, that thou shalt not help thyself.
For it is wonder perilous to any man to give
him fully to the love of God, and leave all
the world, and covet nothing but only the
love of Him; for so many perils may befall
that a man knoweth not of. And therefore
turn home again and leave this desire, for
thou shalt never bring it to the end; and do
as other worldly men do.

Thus say thine enemies, but trow them
not, but hold forth thy desire, and say naught
else but thou wouldest have Jesus, and be at
Jerusalem. And if they perceive then thy
will so strong that thou wilt not spare neither
for sin nor for sickness, for fantasies nor for
frenzies, for doubts nor for dreads of ghostly
temptations, for mischief nor for poverty,
for life nor for death; but ever forth thou
wilt one thing and nothing but one, and
makest deaf ear to them as though thou
heard them not, and holdest thee forth
stiffy in thy prayer and in thy other ghostly
works without stinting, with discretion after
counsel of thy sovereign* or of thy ghostly

* Sovereign—i.e., superior.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

father, then begin they to be wroth and go a
little nearer thee. Then they begin to rob
thee and beat thee, and do thee all the shame
that they can. And that is when they bring
it to pass that all the deeds that thou dost,
be they never so well done, are deemed of
other men as ill and turned into the worst
part. And whatsoever it be that thou
wouldest have done in help of thy body or
of thy soul, it shall be letted or hindered by
other men; so that thou shalt be put from thy
will in all things that thou skilfully covetest.
And all this they do that thou shouldest be
stirred to ire or melancholy or evil will against
thy even-Christian. But against all these
dis-eases, and all other that thou mayest feel,
use this remedy. Take Jesus in thy mind
and anger thee not with them, tarry not with
them but think on thy lesson, that thou art
naught, that thou hast naught, that thou
mayest naught lose of earthly good, thou
covetest naught but the love of Jesus, and
hold forth thy way to Jerusalem with thy
occupation. And nevertheless if thou be
tarried sometime through frailty of thyself
with such uneases as fall to thy bodily life;
through evil will of man or malice of the
fiend, as soon as thou mayest come again to
thyself, leave off the thinking of thy dis-ease
and go forth to thy work. Abide not too
long with them for dread of thine enemies.

Ch. 23] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XXIII

OF A GENERAL REMEDY AGAINST WICKED
STIRRINGS AND PAINFUL TARRYINGS THAT
FALL TO THEIR HEARTS, OF THE, FLESH,
OF THE WORLD, AND OF THE FIEND

AND after this, when thine enemies see that
thou art so well willed, that thou art not angry
nor heavy, nor wroth, nor much. stirred
against any creature for aught that they may
do or speak against thee; but settest thy heart
fully to suffer all that may befall, ease or
unease, praise or lacking, and that thou wilt
charge nothing with thee that thou mightest
keep thy thought and thy desire whole to the
love of God, then are they much abashed.
But then will they assay thee with flattering
and vain pleasing. And that is when they
bring to the sight of thy soul all thy good
deeds and virtues, and bear upon thee that
all men praise thee and speak good of thy
holiness and how all men love thee and
worship thee for thy holy living. Thus do
thine enemies that thou shouldest think their
saying sooth, and have delight in this vain
joy and rest thee therein. But if thou do
well, thou shalt hold all such jangling «as
falsehood and flattery of thine enemy, that
proffereth thee to drink venom tempered with

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

honey. And therefore refuse it and say thou
wilt not thereof, but thou wouldest be at
Jerusalem.

Such lettings thou shalt feel or else other
like unto them, what of thy flesh, what of
the world, what of the fiend, more than I
may rehearse now. For a man as long as he
suffereth his thought wilfully to run about
the world in beholding of sundry things, he
perceiveth few lettings, but as soon as he
draweth all his thought and his yearning to
one thing only, to have that, to know that,
to love that, and that is only Jesus, then shall
he well feel many painful lettings, for every-
thing that he feeleth which is not that that he
coveteth, is letting to him. Therefore I
have told thee of some specially, as for
example; and overmore I say generally that
what stirring that thou feelest of thy flesh
or of the fiend, pleasant or painful, bitter or
sweet, liking or dreadful, gladsome or sorrow-
ful, that would draw down thy thought and
thy desire from the love of Jesus to worldly
vanity, and would let utterly thy ghostly
covetousness that thou hast to the love of
Him, and that thy heart should be occupied
with that stirring restingly, set it at naught,
receive it not wilfully, tarry not therewith
too long. But if it be of some worldly thing
that behoveth needs to be done to thyself or
to thine even-Christian, speed thee soon of

Ch. 23] OF PERFECTION

it and bring it to an end, that it hang not on
thine heart. If it be another thing that
needeth not, or else that toucheth thee not,
charge it not, jangle not therewith, nor anger
thee not, dread it not, like it not, but smite
it out of thine heart readily, and say thus:
“ I am naught; I have naught; naught I seek
nor covet but the love of Jesus.” Knit thy
thought to this desire, and strengthen it and
maintain it with prayer and with other
ghostly works that thou forget it not, and it
shall lead thee in the right way and save thee
from all perils, and though thou feel them
thou shalt not perish, and I hope that it shall
bring thee to perfect love of our Lord Jesus.
Nevertheless on the other side I say also,
what work or what stirring it may be that
may help thy desire, and make thy thought
furthest from lust and mind of the world,
more whole and more burning to the love of
God, strength it and nourish it; whether it
be praying or thinking, stillness or speaking,
reading or hearing, loneliness or communing,
going or sitting; keep it for the time and work
therein as long as the savour lasteth, if it so
be that thou take therewith meat and drink
and sleep as a pilgrim doth, and keep dis-
cretion in thy working after counsel and
ordinance of thy sovereign. For have he
never so great haste in his going, yet will he
eat and drink and sleep. Do thou likewise.

For though it let thee one time, it shall
further thee another time.

CHAPTER XXIV

OF AN EVIL DAY AND A GOOD NIGHT, AND WHAT
IT MEANETH; AND HOW THE LOVE OF THE
WORLD IS LIKENED TO AN EVIL DAY AND
THE LOVE OF GOD TO A GOOD NIGHT

IF thou wilt wit then what this desire is,
soothly it is Jesus. For He maketh this
desire in thee and He giveth it thee, and He
it is that desireth in thee, and He it is that
is desired. He is all and He doth all, if thou
might see Him. Thou dost naught but
sufferest Him to work in thy soul, and
assentest to Him with great gladness of heart
that He vouchsafeth so to do in thee. Thou
art naught else but a reasonable instrument
wherein that He worketh. And therefore
when thou feelest thy thought by touching
of His grace taketh up with desire to Jesus
with a mighty devout will to please Him and
love Him, then think that thou hast Jesus;
for it is He that thou desirest. Behold Him
well for He goeth before thee, not in bodily
likeness but unseeably by privy presence of
His might. Therefore see Him ghostly if
thou may, and fasten all thy thought and
thine affection to Him, and follow Him

Ch. 24] OF PERFECTION

wheresoever He goeth; for He shall lead thee
the right way to Jerusalem that is the sight
of peace in contemplation. Thus prayed
the prophet to the Father of heaven, saying
thus: Father of Heaven, send out Thy light
and Thy soothfastness, that is Thy Son Jesus;
and He shall lead me by desire in me to Thy
holy hill and to Thy tabernacles,* that is to
the feeling of perfect love and height in con-
templation. Of this desire speaketh the
prophet thus: Lord Jesus, the mind of Thee
1s printed in desire of my soul, for my soul hath
desired Thee in the night and my spirit hath
coveted thee in all my thinkings.t| And why
the prophet saith he hath desired God all
in the night, and what he meaneth thereby,
I shall tell thee. Thou wost that the night
is a timeful space betwixt two days; for when
one day is ended, another cometh not at
once, but first cometh night and parteth the
days, sometimes long and sometimes short,
and then after cometh another day. The
prophet meant not only this manner of
night, but he meant a ghostly night. Thou
shalt understand that there be two days or
two lights; the first is a false light, the second
is a true light. The false light is the love of
this world that a man hath in himself of
corruption of his flesh; the true light is the
perfect love of Jesus felt through grace in a
oF S tba Tt Isa. xxvi 8, 9.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

man’s soul. The love of this world is a false
light, for it passeth away and lasteth not, and
so it performeth not what it promiseth. This
light promised the fiend to Adam when he
stirred him to sin and said: Your eyes shall
be opened and ye shall be as gods.* And he
said sooth, for when Adam had sinned, anon
his inner eye was closed and ghostly light
withdrawn, and his outer eyes were opened,
and he felt and saw a new light of fleshly
liking and worldly love that he saw not
before. And so saw he a new day, but this
was an evil day; for this was it that Job
cursed when he said thus: Perish the day in
which I was born.t He cursed not the day
running in the year that God made, but he
cursed this day that man made, that is the
concupiscence and the love of this world in
the which he was born, though he felt it not.
Then this day and this light he asked of God
that it should perish and no longer last.

But the everlasting love of Jesus is a true
day and a blessed light. For God is both
love and light, and He is everlasting as Saint
John saith: He that loveth God dwelleth in
the light.{ ‘Then when man perceiveth and
seeth the love of this world false and failing,
and for that he will forsake it and seek the
love of Jesus, he may not at once feel the love

of Him, but he must abide awhile in the night,

naught else but a forbearing and a with-
drawing of the thought of the soul from

right so a man that setteth him fully to think

ing and perceiving and his affection from

worse than himself. And if he may do so,
then it is night with him, for then he is in
darkness. But this is a good night and a
light darkness, for it is a stopping out of the
false love of this world, and it is a nighing to
the true day. And soothly the darker that
this night is, the nearer is the true day of the
love of Jesus. For the more that a soul may,
through longing to God, be hid from noise
and din of fleshly affections and unclean
thoughts, the nearer it is to feel the light of
the love of Him, for it is even at it. Thus,

THE SCALE ; [Bk. 2

it seemeth, the prophet meant when he said:
When I sit in darkness our Lord is my light.*
That is when my soul is hid from all stirrings
of sin as it were in sleep, then is our Lord
my light, for then nigheth He of His grace
for to show me of His light. Nevertheless
this night is sometimes painful, first when a
man is much foul, and is not used through
grace to be often in this darkness but would
fain have it, and therefore he setteth his
thought and his desire to Godward as much

lightly have it, therefore it is painful. For
the custom and the homeliness that he hath
had with sins before, of the world and of
fleshly affections and earthly things, and his
fleshly deeds, press so upon him and ever
smite in by mastery and draw down all the
soul to them, that he may not well be hid
from them as soon as he would. Therefore
this darkness is painful to him, and namely
when grace toucheth not abundantly. Never-
theless if it be so with thee, be not too heavy
nor strive not much, as though thou wouldest
through mastery put them out of thy
thought; for thou mayest not do so. But
abide grace, suffer easily, and break not
thyself too much. And slyly if thou mayest,
draw thy desire and thy ghostly beholding to

Jesus if thou wouldst not charge them. For

This naught I mean thus. ‘ That a soul may

is a great ease for the soul that desireth the
love of Jesus. It is in ease as for thought
of any earthly thing, nevertheless it is full
busy to think on him. What thing then
maketh this darkness? Soothly naught else
but a gracious desire to have the love of

it hath that time to the love of God, to see

painful, then He is in the soul as travailing
in desire and longing to light; but He is not
as yet resting in love nor as showing His light.
And therefore it is called night and darkness,
inasmuch as the soul is hid from the false
light of the world and hath not yet fully
feeling of true light, but is in abiding of that
blessed love of God which it desireth. Then
if thou wilt wit when thou art in this obscure

thou feelest thine intent and thy will fully
set to desire God and think only on Him,
thou mayest as it were first ask thyself in

any creature. And then if thine eye answer

that way come to restful darkness. And then
is this darkness restful, when the soul is hid
for a time from the painful feeling of all such
vain thought and only is rested in desire and
longing to Jesus with a ghostly beholding to
Him, as it shall be said afterward. But that
lasteth but a while whole; nevertheless though
it be but a short time, it is full profitable.

CHAPTER XXV

HOW THE DESIRE OF JESUS FELT IN THIS LIGHT-
SOME DARKNESS SLAYETH ALL STIRRINGS
OF SIN, AND ENABLETH THE SOUL TO
PERCEIVE GHOSTLY LIGHTNINGS FROM
THE HEAVENLY JERUSALEM THAT IS JESUS

THEN since this darkness and this night is
so good and so restful, though it be but
short, that standeth only in desire and longing
to the love of Jesus with a blind thinking on
Him; how good then and how blessed is it
to feel His love and to be illumined with His
blessed unseeable present light to see sooth-
fastness ; the which light a soul receiveth when
the night passeth and the day springeth.
This I think was the night that the prophet
meant when he said: My soul hath desired
Thee in the night.* As I have before said it
is much better to be hid in this dark night
from beholding of the world, though it is
more painful, than to be out in false liking of
this world, that seemeth so shining and so
comfortable to them that are blind in knowing
of ghostly light. For when thou art in this
darkness thou art much nearer Jerusalem
than when thou art in the midst of the false
light. Therefore apply thine heart fully to

the stirring of grace and use to dwell in this

visions. He saith that he saw a city set upon

CHAPTER XXVI

HOW A MAN SHALL KNOW FALSE ILLUMINATIONS
FEIGNED BY THE FIEND FROM THE TRUE
LIGHT OF KNOWING THAT COMETH OUT
OF JERUSALEM; AND BY WHAT TOKENS

BuT now beware of the midday fiend, that
feigneth light as if it came out of Jerusalem
but is not so. For the fiend seeth that our
Lord Jesus showeth light to His lovers of
soothfastness, therefore in deceiving of them
that are unwise he showeth a light, that is not
true, under colour of a true light, and decei-
veth them. Nevertheless how a soul may
know the light of soothfastness, when it
shineth from God, and when it is feigned
through the enemy, I shall say as methinketh
by an example of the firmament.

Sometime the firmament showeth a light
from the sun which seemeth the sun and is
not, and sometime the sun showeth truly.
A knowing of that one from that other is this.
The feigned sun showeth him not but
between two black rainy clouds; then,
because the sun is near, there shineth out
from the clouds as it were a sun, yet is none.
But the true sun showeth him when the
firmament is clear or much cleared from the
black clouds. Now to our purpose. Some

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

men, as it seemeth, forsake the love of the
world and would come to the love of God
and to the light of understanding of Him, but _
they will not come through this darkness that
Ihave spoken of before. They will not know
themselves truly nor meekly, what they have
been before, nor what they are yet through
sin, nor how naught they are in their own
kind against God. They are not busy to
enter into themselves, all other things
outward being left, and slay all wicked
stirrings of sin that rise in their hearts, of
pride, envy or other sins, through lasting
desire to Jesus in praying and in thinking,
in silence and in weeping, and in other bodily
and ghostly exercise as devout men and holy
men have done. But as soon as they have
forsaken the world as it were outward in
likeness, or else soon after, they ween they
are holy and able to have the ghostly under-
standing of the Gospel and of Holy Writ.
And namely if they may fulfil letterly* the
commandments of God and keep them from
bodily sins, then they ween that they love
God perfectly. And therefore they will anon
preach and teach all other men, as if they had
received grace of understanding in perfection
of charity through special gift of the Holy
Ghost. And also they are much more
stirred thereto, forasmuch as they feel some-
x- Letterly—i.e., literally.

Ch. 26] OF PERFECTION

time much knowing, as it were suddenly given
to them without great study before had, and
also much fervour of love, as it seemeth, to
preach truth and righteousness to their even-
Christian. Therefore they hold it as a grace
of God who visiteth them with His blessed
light before other souls. Nevertheless if
they will look well about them, they shall
well see that this light of knowing and the
heat that they feel cometh not of the true sun,
that is our Lord Jesus, but it cometh from
the midday fiend that feigneth light and
likeneth him to the soul. And therefore shall
he be known by example before said.

Light of cunning* that is feigned by the
fiend to a dark soul is showed between two
black raining clouds. The overcloud is
presumption and highing of himself, the
nether cloud is down-putting and a lowering
of his even-Christian. Then what light of
knowing or feeling of fervour that it be that
shineth to a soul, with presumption and
highing of itself and disdain of his even-
Christian the same time felt, it is not light
of grace given of the Holy Ghost though the
knowing in itself were soothfast; but it is
either of the fiend if it come suddenly, or else
of man’s own wit if it come by study. And
so it may well be known if this feigned light
of knowing is not the light of the true sun.

* Cunning—v.e., knowledge, ability.

THE SCALE [Bk 2

Therefore they that have this knowing on
this manner are full of ghostly pride and see
it not. They are so blind with this feigned
light that they hold the highness of their own
heart and unbuxomness to the laws of Holy
Church as it were perfect meekness to the
Gospel and to the laws of God; and they ween
that the following of their own will were
freedom of spirit, and therefore as black

there is unstableness and all evil work.f And
therefore that cunning that bringeth forth
such sins cometh not from the Father of
Light that is God, but is earthly, beastly and
fiendly. And so by these tokens, that are
pride, presumption, unbuxomness, indigna-

these follow after—may the feigned light be
known from the true. For the true sun
shineth and showeth him not by special
visitation to give light of understanding or
perfect charity to a soul, unless the firma-

CHAPTER XXVII

HOW GREAT PROFIT IT IS TO THE SOUL TO BE
BROUGHT THROUGH GRACE INTO LIGHT-
SOME DARKNESS, AND HOW A MAN SHALL
DISPOSE HIM IF HE WILL COME THERETO

THERE are many devout souls that through
grace come into this darkness and feel the
knowing of themselves, and yet wot they not
fully what it is; and that unknowing in part
hindereth them. They feel often their
thought and their affection well drawn out
and departed from the mind of earthly things,
and brought into a great rest of a delectable
softness without painful troubling of vain
thoughts or of their bodily wits; and they
feel that time so great freedom of spirit that
they may think on Jesus peaceably, and offer
their psalms and prayers mightily savourly
and sweetly to Him as long as frailty of the
bodily kind may suffer it. They wot well
that this feeling is good, but they wot not
what it is. Therefore unto all such souls I
say as methinketh, that this manner of

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

feeling—though it be but short and but
seldom—it is soothfastly this darkness that
I speak of; for it is a feeling of themselves
first, and a rising above themselves through
burning desire to the sight of Jesus. Or .
else, if I shall say more soothly, this gracious
feeling is a ghostly sight of Jesus, and if they
may keep them in that rest or bring it through
grace into a custom, so that they might
lightly and freely have it when they list, and
hold them in it, they should never be over-
come by temptation of the fiend nor of the
flesh, nor by errors nor heresies; for they are
set in the gate of contemplation, able and
ready to receive the perfect love of Jesus.
Therefore he that hath it, it is good that he
know it meekly, keep it tenderly, and pursue
it fervently, that no creature let him utterly
from it that he follow it not when he may, and
that he forget and set at naught all things
that should put him from this, if he be free
of himself and may do what he will without
slander or dis-ease of his even-Christian.
For methinketh that he may not come to this
rest lightly, unless he have great plenty of
grace and set himself to follow after the
stirring of grace; and that ought he to do.
For grace would ever be free, namely from
sin and worldly business, and from all other
things that let the working of it, though they
be no sin.

Ch. 27] OF PERFECTION

Nevertheless another soul that hath not
yet received this fulness of grace, if he desire
to come to this ghostly knowing of Jesus, as
much as in him is he must able himself to it,
and put away all lettings that stop grace as
much as he may. He must learn to die to
the world, and forsake the love of it truly.
First pride, bodily and ghostly, that he
desire no worship, worldly cunning nor
worldly craft, benefices nor riches, precious
clothing nor worldly array, nor nothing
where-through he should be worshipped
above other men. He shall covet none of all
these, but, if they be put upon him, take
them with dread, so that he be poor both
outward and inward, or else fully inward in
heart. And that he covet to be forgotten of
the world, that men regard him no more—
be he never so rich nor so cunning—than
the poorest man that liveth. Also that he
suffer not his heart to rest in beholding of
his own deeds or his virtues, weening that he
doth: better than another, because he for-
saketh the world and others do not so, and
therefore setteth well by himself. Also he
must leave all rising of heart and evil will of
ire and envy against his even-Christian, and
that he dis-ease no man, nor anger him un-
skilfully in word nor in deed, nor give any
man matter where-through he might skil-
fully be angered or stirred; so that he might

2T

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

be free from every man. And also that he
forsake covetousness, that he covet right
naught of earthly good but only ask his
bodily sustenance that he needeth, and hold
him paid when God stirreth other men to .
give him aught. And that he put no manner
of trust in any worldly good, nor in help or
favour of any worldly friend, but principally
and fully in God; for if he do otherwise he
bindeth himself to the world, and he may not
therefore be free to think on Jesus. And
also’ gluttony and lechery, and also all other
fleshly uncleanness utterly he must leave,
that the affection be bound to no woman by
fleshly homeliness. For it is no doubt that
such blind love that is sometimes atwixt a
man and a woman, and seemeth good and
honest—for that they would not sin in deed—
in the sight of God is full unclean and well
great sin.. For it is a great sin that a man
shall suffer his affection—that should be
fastened to Jesus and to all virtues and to all
ghostly cleanness—to be bound with any
fleshly love of any creature wilfully, namely
if it be so much that it beareth down the
thought and maketh it unrestful, that he may
have no savour in God. Thus I hold it to be
wilful if a man doth it and saith it is no sin,
or else is so blinded with it that he will not
see it. And also that a man covet no deli-
cacies of meats and drinks only for lust of

278 f:

Ch. 27] OF PERFECTION

his flesh, but hold him paid with such meats
as he may easily have without great business
—namely, if he be whole, what meat soever
that will do away hunger and keep the body
in common strength unto the service of God
—and that he murmur not nor strive not,
nor anger him not for his meat, though he be
sometime not served as the flesh would.

All these sins and all other must he forsake
utterly in his will and in deed when he may,
and other things that let him, so that he may
dispose him to think freely on Jesus. For as
long as these lettings and such other hang
upon him he may not die to the world, nor
come into this darkness of knowing of
himself, and therefore, that he might come
thereto, he must do all this as Saint Paul did,

to me, and I to the world.* That is, he that
hath forsaken the love of the world in
worships and riches and all other worldly
things beforesaid for the love of God, and
loveth it not nor pursueth it not, but is well
paid that he hath right naught of it, nor would
have though he might, soothly to him the
world is dead, for he hath no savour nor
delight therein. Also if the world set him
at naught and hath no regard to him, nor
favour nor worship, nor set no price by him,
but forgetteth him as a dead man, then is he

dead to the world. And in this plight was
Saint Paul set perfectly, and so must any other
man in part that would follow and come to
the perfect love of God; for he may not live
to God fully, unless he die first to the world.
This dying to the world is this darkness,
and it is the gate to contemplation and to
reforming in feeling, and none other than
this. There may be sundry ways and divers
works letting and leading sundry souls to
contemplation; for after divers disposings of
men and after sundry states—as are religious
and secular that they are in—are divers
exercises in working. Nevertheless there is
no gate but one; for what exercise it be that
a soul hath, unless he may come by that
exercise to this knowing and to a meek feeling
of himself—and that is that he be mortified
and dead to the world as in his: love, and that
he may feel himself sometimes in this restful
darkness by which he may be hid from vanity
of the world as in his love, and that he may
feel himself what he is—soothly he is not yet
come to reforming in feeling nor hath not
fully contemplation. He is full far there-
from, and if he will come by any other gate,
he is but a thief and a breaker of the wall,
and therefore as unworthy shall he be cast
out. But he that can bring himself first to
naught by grace of meekness, and die on this
manner, he is in the gate, for he is dead to the

world and he liveth to God. Of which Saint

of Jesus, but your life is hid from worldly
men as Christ liveth and is hid in His god-
head from the love and the sight of fleshly
lovers.

This gate our Lord Himself showed in the

naught else but the profit of this lightsome
darkness, the which I call the gate of con-
templation. For he that is in this darkness,
and is hid through grace from worldly vanity,
he coveteth not of worldly good, he seeketh
it not, he is not tarried therewith, he looketh
not after it, he loveth it not, and therefore
hath he an hundredfold more than the king
or than he hath that coveteth most of earthly
good. For he that coveteth naught but
Jesus hath an hundredfold, for he hath more
rest, more peace in heart, more very love
and delight in soul in one day, than he that
most coveteth of the world and hath all the

wealth of it under his will, hath in all his

a rich naught that bringeth a soul to so much

Whoso goeth in darkness and hath no light,

as it doth if a man be a great while in the sun,

that art a daughter of Chaldee for love of the

CHAPTER XXVIII

THAT IN REFORMING OF A SOUL THE WORKING
OF OUR LORD JESUS IS DIVIDED INTO FOUR
PARTS; THAT IS, CALLING, RIGHTING,
MAGNIFYING AND GLORIFYING

Lo I have said to thee a little how, if thou
covet to be reformed in feeling, thou shalt
dispose thee -toward in thy forthgoing.
Nevertheless I say not that thou mayest do
thus of thyself, for I wot well that our Lord
Jesus bringeth all this to the end whereso He
will. For He only through His grace
stirreth a soul, and bringeth it into this dark-
x Isa. lviii 10, 11. + Isa. xlvii 5.

Ch. 28] OF PERFECTION

ness first, and sithen into light, as the prophet
saith: Right as the light of knowing and the
feeling of ghostly love is of Jesus, right so the
darkness that is the forsaking of worldly love is
of Him,* for He doth all. He formeth and
reformeth; He formeth only of Himself, but
He reformeth us with us; for grace given, and
applying of our will to grace, worketh all
this. And on what manner wise He doth
that, Saint Paul rehearseth thus: These that
God knew before should be made shapely to the
image of His Son, these He called, these He
righted, these He magnified, these He glorified.

Though these words may be said of all
chosen souls in the lowest degree of charity,
that are reformed only in faith; nevertheless
they may be understood more specially of
those souls that are reformed in feeling, to
the which our Lord God showeth much plenty
of grace, and doth much more business about
them. For they are His own sons specially,
that bear the full shape and the likeness of
His Son Jesus. In the which words Saint
Paul departeth the working of our Lord into
four times. First is the time of calling to a
soul from worldly vanity, and that time is
often easy and comfortable. For in begin-
ning of turning such a man, that is disposed
to much grace, is so quickly and so feelingly
inspired and feeleth often so great sweetness

* Ps. cxxxviii I2. t Rom. viii 29, 30.

WHE! SCALE [Bk. 2

of devotion, and hath so many tears in com-
punction, that he thinketh him sometimes
as he were half in heaven; but this softness
passeth after a time. And then cometh the
second time, that is the time of righting, that
is travailous. For when he beginneth to go
forth mightily in the way of righteousness,
and setteth his will full against all sin without
and within, and stretcheth out his desire to
virtues and to the love of Jesus, then feeleth
he much letting—both within himself of
frowardness and hardness of his own will, and
from without through temptation of his
enemy—that he is often in full great torment.
And that is no wonder, for he hath so long
been crooked to the false love of this world,
that he may not be made right, as a crooked
staff may not be made even, unless he be cast
and baked in the fire. Therefore our Lord
Jesus, seeing what thing is behovable to a
froward soul, suffereth it to be travailed and
tarried by sundry temptations, and to be well
exercised through ghostly temptations that
all the rust of uncleanness might be burnt
out of it. And that shall be both within, of
dreads and doubts and perplexities that it
shall nearly fall into despair; and it shall
seem as it were forsaken of God and left all
in the hands of the fiend, save for a little
privy trust that it shall have in the goodness
of God and in His mercy.. For that privy

Ch. 28] OF PERFECTION

trust our Lord leaveth in such a soul, go He
never so far from it; by the which the soul is
borne up from despair and saved from
ghostly mischief. And also without itself it
shall be mortified and pained in the sen-
suality, either by divers sickness, or by
feelable torment of the fiend, or else through
a privy might of God, the silly soul by feeling
and busying of the wretched body shall be
so pained, and it shall not wit how it shall
suffer longer to be in the body, unless it were
that our Lord Jesus kept it therein. And
yet nevertheless had the soul liefer to be in
all this pain than to be blinded with the false
love of the world. For that were hell to
such a soul; but the suffering of this manner
of pain is naught but purgatory, and therefore
he suffereth it gladly, and he would not put
it away though he might, for it is so profit-
able. All this doth our Lord in great profit
of the soul, to drive it out of the sensuality,
that it might receive ghostly light.

For after this, when the soul is thus
mortified and brought from worldly love into
this darkness, so that it hath no more savour
nor delight of worldly liking than of a straw
but thinketh it bitter as wormwood, then
cometh the third time of magnifying. And
that is when the soul is reformed in feeling
in part, and receiveth the gift of perfection
and the grace of contemplation; and that is

THE! SCALE [Bk. 2

a time of great rest, for then is Jesus more
homely with a soul. And after this cometh
the fourth time, of glorifying. That is when
the soul shall be fully reformed in the bliss of
heaven. For these souls that are thus called
from sin and thus righted (or else on other
manner like) by divers assaying through
fire and water, and afterwards are thus
magnified, they shall be glorified. For our
Lord shall then give them fully that which
they here coveted, and more than they could
covet. For He shall raise them above all
other chosen souls to the evenhead of
cherubim and seraphim, seeing they passed
all others in knowing and loving of God
here in this life. Therefore he that will
come to this magnifying let him not dread
this righting, for that is the way. For our
Lord saith by His prophet a word of great
comfort to all such souls that are examined
with the fire of tribulation, thus: My child,
if thou pass through fire, dread not, for the
flame shall not harm thee.* It shall cleanse
thee from all fleshly filth and make thee able
to receive ghostly fire of the love of God.
And that needeth to be done first. For as
I have before said, it may not else be reformed
in feeling.

CHAPTER XXIX

HOW IT FALLETH SOMETIMES THAT SOULS BE-
GINNING AND PROFITING IN GRACE SEEM TO
HAVE MORE LOVE, AS BY OUTWARD TOKENS,
THAN SOME HAVE THAT BE PERFECT; AND
YET IT IS NOT SO IN SOOTH WITHIN

BuT now sayest thou: How may this be
sooth? For there are many souls new
turned to God that have many ghostly
feelings. Some have great compunction for
their sins, and some have great devotions
and fervours in their prayers, and often have
sundry teachings of ghostly light in under-
standing, and some men have other manner
feelings of comfortable heat and great sweet-
ness, and nevertheless these souls come never
fully into this restful darkness that I speak of,
with fervent desire and lasting love and
thought in God. ‘Then askest thou whether
these souls be reformed in feeling or not?
It seemeth yes, inasmuch as they have such
great ghostly feelings that other men, that
stand only in faith, feel not of.

Unto this I may say as methinketh, that
these ghostly feelings, whether they stand
in compunction or devotion, or in ghostly
imagination, are not the feelings which a soul
shall have and feel in the grace of contempla-

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

tion. I say not but that they are soothfast
and graciously given of God. But these
souls that feel such are not yet reformed in
feeling, nor they have not yet the gift of
perfection nor ghostly such burning love of
Jesus as they may come to. And neverthe-
less often it seemeth otherwise that such
souls feel more of the love of God than
others that have the gift of perfection, inas-
much as the feeling showeth more outward
by great fervour of bodily tokens, in weeping,
praying, kneeling and speaking, and other
bodily stirring; so far forth that it seemeth
to another man that they were ever ravished
in love. And though methinketh it is not
so, well I wot that this manner of feelings
and fervours of devotion and compunction
that these men feel are gracious gifts of God
sent into chosen souls, to draw them out of
worldly love and fleshly lust that have been
long time rooted in their heart, from which
love they should not be drawn out but by
such seeable stirring of great fervours.
Nevertheless that the fervour is so much
in outward showing, it is not only for much-
ness of love that they have it, but for littleness
and weakness of their soul, that may not bear
a little touching of God. For it is as yet as
it were fastened to the flesh, and never was
yet departed from it by ghostly mortifying;
and therefore the least touching of love and

Ch. 29] OF PERFECTION

the least sparkle of ghostly light sent from
heaven into such a soul is so much and so
comfortable, so sweet and so delectable, over
all the liking that it ever felt before in fleshly `
love of earthly things, that it is overtaken with
it. And also it is so new and sudden and so
uncouth* that it may not suffer to bear it,
but bursteth and showeth out in weeping,
sobbing, and other bodily stirring. Right
as the costrel} that is old, when it receiveth
new wine that is fresh and mighty, the costrel
bolnetht out and is in point to cleave and
burst, until the wine hath boiled and purged
out all uncleanness. But as soon as the wine
is fined and cleared, then it standeth still and
the costrel whole. Right so a soul that is old
through sin, when it receiveth a little of the
love of God, that is so fresh and so mighty
that the body is in point to cleave and to
break, were it not that God keepeth it whole.
But yet it bursteth out at the eyes by weeping
and at the mouth by speaking, and that is
more for weakness and feebleness of soul than
for muchness of love. For afterwards, when
love hath boiled all the uncleanness out of
the soul by such great fervours, then is love
clear and standeth still; and then is both the
body and the soul much more in peace, and

* Uncouth—7.e., strange, unknown.
+ Costrel—7.e., barrel, wine skin.
+ Bolneth—7.e., swelieth.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

yet hath the soul much more love than it had
before, though it show less outward. For it
is now all whole in rest within, and naught
but little in outward showing of fervour.
And therefore I say that these souls that feel
such great bodily fervours, though they be
in much grace, are not yet reformed in feeling,
but they are greatly disposed toward. For I
trow that such a man, namely one that hath
been greatly defiled in sin, shall not be
reformed in feeling, unless he be first burnt
and purified with such great compunctions
going before. Another soul that never was
much defiled with love of the world, but
hath ever been kept from great sins in
innocence, may lightlier and more privily,
without great fervour showed outward, come
to this reforming.

Then is this sooth, as I think, that such
comforts and fervours that a soul feeleth in a
state of beginning or of profiting are as it
were his ghostly food sent from heaven, to
strengthen him in his travail. Right as a
pilgrim travaileth all day meatless and drink-
less, and is nearly overcome with weariness,
falleth at the last to a good inn, and there
hath he meat and drink and is well refreshed
for the time; right so ghostly a devout soul
that will forsake the love of the world, and
would fain love God and setteth all his
business thereto, prayeth and travaileth all

day bodily and ghostly, and sometime feeleth
no comfort nor savour in devotion. Then
our Lord, having pity over all His creatures,

him well paid for all his travail and all the

ali such feelings come to them in that state

lasting desire in Jesus, nor the eye of their
soul is not opened to the beholding of ghostly
things, but they nigh fast toward. And
therefore they are not yet reformed in feeling,

CHAPTER XXX _

IN WHAT MANNER A MAN SHALL HAVE KNOWING
“OF HIS OWN SOUL; AND HOW A MAN
SHOULD SET HIS LOVE IN JESUS, GOD AND
MAN, ONE PERSON

IT needeth a soul that would have knowledge
of ghostly things, to have first knowing of
itself. For it may not have knowing of a kind
above itself unless it have knowing of itself;
and that is when the soul is so gathered into
itself and departed from beholding of all
earthly things and from the use of bodily
wits, that it feeleth itself as it is in its own
kind without a body. Then if thou covet to
know and see thy soul what it is, thou shalt
not turn thy thought with imagination into
thy body to seek it and feel it, as it were hid
within thy heart as thy heart is hid and
holden within thy body. If thou seek so,
thou shalt never find it in itself. The more
thou seekest to find and feel it as thou
wouldest a bodily thing, the further thou art
therefrom. For thy soul is no body but a
life unseeable; not hid and holden within thy
body as a less thing is hid and holden within
a more, but it is holding and quickening thy
body, much more than thy body is in might
and virtue.

Ch. 30] OF PERFECTION

. Then if thou wilt find it, withdraw thy
thought from all bodily thing outward, and
from mind of thine own body also, and from
thy five wits as much as thou may; and think
on the kind of a reasonable soul ghostly, as
thou wouldest think to know any virtue, as
soothfastness or meekness or any other.
Right so think that a soul is a life, immortal
and unseeable, and hath might in itself to
see and know the sovereign soothfastness,
and to love the sovereign goodness that is
` God. When thou seest this, then feelest
thou somewhat of thyself. Seek thyself in
none other place; but the more fully and the
more clearly that thou mayest think of the
kind and the worthiness of a reasonable soul
—what it is, and what is the kindly working
of it—the better seest thou thyself. It is
full hard for a soul that is rude and much in
the flesh to have sight and knowing of it, or
of an angel, or of God; it falleth anon into
imagination of bodily shape, and it weeneth
thereby to have the sight of itself, and so of
God, and so of ghostly things. And that
may. not be; for all ghostly things are seen
and known by understanding of the soul, not
by imagination. Right as a soul seeth by
understanding that the virtue of righteous-
ness is to give to everything that which it
ought to have, right so and on such a manner
may the soul see itself by understanding.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

Nevertheless I say not that thy soul shall
rest still in this knowing, but it shall by this
seek higher knowing above itself, and that is
the kind of God. For a soul is but a mirror,
in the which thou shalt see God ghostly.
And therefore thou shalt first find thy mirror
and keep it bright and clean from fleshly
filth and worldly vanity, and hold it well up
from the earth that thou mayest see it, and
our Lord therein also. For to this end travail
all chosen souls in this life, in their meaning
and their intent; though they have not
specially the feeling of this. And therefore
it is, as I have said before, that many souls
beginning and profiting have many great
fervours and much sweet devotion, and as it
seemeth burn all in love, and yet they have
not perfectly love nor ghostly knowing of
God. For wit thou well, feel a soul never so
much fervour, so much that he thinketh that
the body may not bear it, or though he melt
all into weeping, as long as his thinking and
his beholding of God is most or all in imagina-
tion and not in understanding, he cometh not
yet to perfect love nor to contemplation.

For thou shalt understand that the love of
God is on three manner wise. All are good,
but each one is better than other. The first
cometh only through faith, without gracious
imagination or ghostly knowing of God.
This love is in the least soul that is reformed

Ch. 30] OF PERFECTION

in faith, in the lowest degree of charity; and
it is good, for it sufficeth to salvation. The
second love is that which a soul feeleth
through faith and imagination of Jesus in His
manhood. This love is better than the first,
when the imagination is stirred by grace; for
why, then the ghostly eye is opened in
beholding of our Lord’s manhood. The
third love that the soul feeleth through
ghostly sight of the Godhead in the manhood,
as it may be seen here, is best and most
worthy, and that is perfect love. This love
a soul feeleth not until it be reformed in
feeling. Souls beginning and profiting have
not this love, for they cannot think on Jesus
nor love Him godly, but as it were all manly
and fleshly after the conditions and likeness
of man. And upon that regard they shape
all their working in their thoughts and in
their affections. They dread Him as a man
and worship Him and love Him principally
in manly imagination, and go no further.
As thus: if they have done amiss and tres-
passed against God, they think that God is
wroth with them as a man should be if they
had trespassed against him, and therefore
they fall down as it were to the feet of our
Lord with sorrow of heart and cry mercy.
And when they do thus they have a good
trust that our Lord of His mercy will forgive
them their trespass. This manner of doing

is right good, but it is not ghostly as it might

For in our Lord Jesus are two kinds, the

worthier, ghostlier and more meedful than

CHAPTER XXXI

HOW THIS MANNER OF SPEAKING OF REFORMING
IN FEELING OF A SOUL SHALL BE TAKEN,
AND ON WHAT WISE IT IS REFORMED; AND
HOW IT IS FOUND IN SAINT PAUL’S WORDS

Now I have said to thee a little of reforming
in faith, and also I have touched to thee a
little of the forthgoing from that reforming
to the higher reforming that is in feeling.
Not in the intent that I would by these words
set God’s working under a law of my
speaking; as if to say, thus worketh God in a
soul and none otherwise. Nay, I mean not
so: but I say, after my simple feeling, that
our Lord worketh thus in some creatures as
I think. And I know well that He worketh
otherwise also, that passeth my wit and my
feeling. Nevertheless, whether He work thus
or otherwise, by sundry means, in longer
time or shorter time, with much travail or
with little travail, if all come to one end—
that is to the perfect love of Him—then it is
good enough. For if He will give one soul
on one day the full grace of contemplation
and without any travail, as He well may, as
good is it to that soul as if he had been
examined, pained, mortified and purified for
twenty winters. And therefore upon this

Ch. 31] OF PERFECTION

manner take thou my saying, as I have said
and as I think to say. For now by the grace
of our Lord Jesus shall I speak a little as
methinketh more openly of reforming in
feeling; what it is, and how it is made, and
which are ghostly feelings that a soul
receiveth. Nevertheless first, that I take
not this manner of speaking of reforming of
a soul in feeling as feigning or fantasy, there-
fore I shall ground it in Saint Paul’s words,
when he saith thus: Ye that are through grace
reformed in faith, conform you not hence-
forward to the manners of the world, in pride,
in covetousness, and in other sins ; but be ye
reformed in newhead of feeling.* Lo here
thou mayest see that Saint Paul speaketh of
reforming in feeling. And what that new
feeling is he expoundeth in another place
thus: We pray God that ye may be fulfilled in
knowing of God’s will, in all understanding and
in all manner of ghostly wisdom.t This is
reforming in feeling. For thou shalt under-
stand that the soul hath two manner of
feelings: one without of the five bodily wits,
another within of the ghostly wits; the which
are properly the mights of the soul, mind,
reason, and will. When these mights are
through grace fulfilled in all understanding
of the will of God and ghostly wisdom, then
hath the soul new gracious feelings. That

know God and love Him all manly not ghostly,

CHAPTER XXXII

HOW GOD OPENETH THE INNER EYE OF THE
SOUL TO SEE HIM, NOT ALL AT ONCE BUT
BY DIVERS TIMES. AND OF THREE MANNERS
OF REFORMING OF A SOUL BY ENSAMPLE

A SOUL that is called from the love of the
world, and after that is righted and assayed,
mortified and purified, as I have before said,
our Lord Jesus of His merciful goodness
reformeth it in feeling when He vouchsafeth.
He openeth the inner eye of the soul when He
lighteneth the reason through touching and
shining of His blessed light, to see Him and
know Him; not all fully at once, but little and
little by divers times as the soul may suffer

THE! SCALE [Bk. 2

Him. He seeth Him not what He is, for
that may no creature do in heaven nor in
earth; nor he seeth Him not as He is, for
that sight is only in the bliss of heaven.
But he seeth Him that He is an unchangeable
being; sovereign might, sovereign soothfast-
ness, sovereign goodness, a blessed life, an
endless bliss. This seeth the soul, and much
more that cometh withal; not blindly and
nakedly and unsavourly, as doth a clerk that
seeth Him by his clergy only through might
of his naked reason, but he seeth Him in
understanding that is comforted and lighted
by the gift of the Holy Ghost with a wonder-
ful reverence and a privy burning love and
with ghostly savour and heavenly delight,
more clearly and more fully than it may be
written or said.

This sight, though it be but shortly and
little, is so worthy and so mighty that it
draweth and ravisheth all the affection of the
soul from beholding and mind of all earthly
things thereto, to rest therein evermore if it
might. And on this manner of sight and
knowing the soul groundeth all his inward
working in all the affections. For then it
dreadeth God in man as soothfastness,
wondereth Him as might, loveth Him as
goodness. This sight and this knowing of
Jesus, with the blessed love that cometh out
of it, may be called reforming of a soul in-

Ch. 32] OF PERFECTION

faith and in feeling that I speak of. It is in
faith, for it is dark as yet in regard of that full
knowing of Jesus, with the blessed love that
cometh out of it, that shall be in heaven.
For then shall we see Him, not only that He
is, but as He is; as Saint John saith: Then
shall we see Him as He is.* Nevertheless it
is in feeling also as in regard of that blind
knowing that a soul hath standing only in
faith. For this soul knoweth somewhat of
the very kind of Jesus God through this
gracious sight, but that other knoweth not,
but only troweth it is sooth.

Nevertheless that thou the better mayest
conceive what I mean, I shall show these
three manners of reforming of a soul, by
ensample of three men standing in the light
of the sun. Of the which three one is blind,
another may see but he hath his eyes stopped,
the third looketh forth full sight. The blind
man hath no-manner of knowing that he is
in the ‘sun, but he troweth it if a true man
tell him; and he betokeneth a soul that is only
reformed in faith, and troweth in God as
Holy Church teacheth, and wots not what.
This sufficeth as for salvation. That other
man seeth a light of the sun, but he seeth it
not clearly what it is; for the lid of his eye
letteth him that he may not see. But he
seeth through the lid of his eye a glimmering

* John iii 2.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

of great light; and he betokeneth a soul that
is reformed in faith and in feeling, and so is
he contemplative. For he seeth somewhat
of the Godhead of Jesus through grace; not
clearly nor fully, because the lid, that is his
bodily kind, is yet a wall betwixt his kind and
the kind of Jesus God, and letteth him from
the clear sight. But he seeth through this
wall, after that grace toucheth him more or
less, that Jesus is God and that Jesus is
sovereign goodness and sovereign being and
a blessed life, and that all other goodness
cometh of Him. ‘This seeth the soul by
grace, notwithstanding the bodily kind, and
the more clear and subtle that the soul is
made, and the more it is departed from
fleshlihead, the sharper sight it hath and the
mightier love of the godhead of Jesus. This
sight is so mighty that, though none other
man living would trow in Jesus nor love Him,
he would never trow the less nor love Him
the less; for he seeth it soothfastly that he
may not untrow it. The third man, that hath
full sight of the sun, he troweth it not for he
seeth it fully; and he betokeneth a full blessed
soul that, without any wall of body or of sin,
seeth openly the face of Jesus in the bliss of
heaven. ‘There is no faith, and therefore he
is fully reformed in feeling. There is no
state above the second reforming that a
soul may come to here in this life; for this is

Ch. 32] OF PERFECTION

the state of perfection and the way heaven-
ward. Nevertheless all the souls that are
in this state are not all alike farforth. For
some have it little, shortly and seldom; and
some have it longer, clearer and oftener; and
some have it best, clearest and longest, after
the abounding of grace; and yet all these have
the gift of contemplation. For the soul hath
not perfect sight of Jesus all at once, but first
a little and a little, and after that it profiteth
and cometh to more feeling. And as long
as it is in this life it may wax more in knowing
and in this love of Jesus. And soothly I wot
not what were more lief to such a soul that
hath felt a little of it than utterly—all other
things being left and set at naught—to tend
only to have clearer sight and cleaner love of
Jesus in whom is all the blessed Trinity.
This manner knowing of Jesus, as I under-
stand, is the opening of heaven to the eye of
a clean soul, of the which holy men speak in.
their writings. Not as some ween, that the
opening of heaven is as if a soul might see
by imagination through the skies above the
firmament how our Lord Jesus sitteth in His
majesty in a bodily light as much as a hundred
suns. Nay, it is not so; nor though he see
never so high on that manner, soothly he
seeth not the ghostly heaven. The higher
he ascendeth above the sun to see Jesus, the
lower he falleth beneath the sun. Never-

theless this manner of sight is sufferable to
