CHAPTER XXXIII

HOW JESUS IS HEAVEN TO THE SOUL; AND WHY
HE IS CALLED FIRE

WHAT is heaven to a reasonable soul?
Soothly naught else but Jesus God. For if
that be heaven only that is above all earthly
thing, then is God only heaven to man’s
soul, for He only is above the kind of a soul.
Then if a soul may through grace have know-
ing of that blessed kind of Jesus, soothly he
seeth heaven for he seeth God. ‘Therefore
there are many men that err in understanding
of some words that are said of God, for they
understand them not ghostly. Holy writing
saith that a soul that will find God shall lift
upward the inner eye and seek God above
itself. Then some men, that will do after
this saying, understand this word ‘“‘ above
themselves ” as for higher setting in stead
and worthiness of place, as one element and
one planet is above another in setting and
worthiness of bodily place; but it is not so
ghostly. For a soul is above all bodily
things, not by setting of stead but by sub-
stance and worthiness of kind. Right so on

Ch. 33] OF PERFECTION

the self wise God is above all bodily and
ghostly creatures, not by setting of stead but
through subtlety and worthiness of His
unchangeable blessed kind. And therefore
he that will wisely seek God and find Him,
he shall not run out with his thought, as if
he would climb above the sun and part the
firmament and imagine the majesty as it were
of a hundred suns; he shall rather draw down
the sun and all the firmament, and forget it
and cast it beneath him where he is, and set
all this and all bodily thing also at naught,
and think then, if he can ghostly, both of
himself and of God also. And if he do thus,
then seeth the soul above itself, then seeth
it into heaven.

Upon this same manner shall this word
“ within ” be understood. It is commonly
said that a soul shall see our Lord within all
thing and within itself. Sooth it is that our
Lord is within all creatures, but not on that
manner that a kernel is hid within the shell
of a nut, or as a little bodily thing is holden
within another such. But He is within all
creatures as holding and keeping them in
their being, through subtlety and might of
His own blessed kind and cleanness un-
seeable. For right as a thing which is most
precious and most clean is laid innermost,
right so by that likeness it is said that the
kind of God, that is most precious, most clean

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

and most ghostly, furthest from bodihead,
is hid within all things. And therefore he
that will seek God within, he shall forget
first all bodily things—for all that is without
—and his own body; and he shall forget
thinking of his own soul and think on the
unmade kind that is Jesus, that made him,
and giveth him reason and mind and love;
the which is within him through His might
and sovereign subtlety. Upon this manner
shall the soul do when grace toucheth it, or
else it will but little avail to seek Jesus and to
find Him within itself and within all creatures
as methinketh.

Also it is said in Holy Writ that God 1s
light. So saith Saint John.* This light
shail not be understood as for bodily light,
but it is understood thus, that God is truth
and soothfastness, for soothfastness is ghostly
light. Then he that most graciously knoweth
soothfastness, best knoweth God. And
nevertheless it is likened to the bodily light
for this reason. Right as the sun to the
bodily eye showeth itself and all bodily things
by it: right so soothfastness, that is God,
showeth to the reason of the soul itself first,
and by itself all other ghostly thing that
needeth to the knowing of a soul. Thus
saith the prophet: Lord, we shall see thy light
by Thy light.t ‘That is, we shall see Thee that

A TJohn i5. Tt Ps. xxxv 9.

Ch. 33] OF PERFECTION

art soothfastness by Thyself. On the same
wise it is said that God is fire: Our Lord is
fire wasting.* That is to say, God is not
fire elementary, that jheateth the body and
burneth it, but God is love and charity. For
as fire wasteth all bodily thing that may be
wasted, right so the love of God burneth and
wasteth all sin out of the soul and maketh it
clean, as fire maketh clean all manner metal.

These words, and all other that are spoken
of our Lord in Holy Writ by bodily likeness,
must needs be understood ghostly, else there
is no savour in them. Nevertheless the cause
why such manner words are said of our Lord
in Holy Writ is this. We are so fleshly that
we cannot speak of God nor understand of
Him, unless we by such words first be
entered in. Nevertheless when the inner eye
is opened through grace to have a little sight
of Jesus, then shall the soul turn lightly
enough all such words of bodily things into
ghostly understanding. ‘This ghostly open-
ing of the inner eye into knowing of the God-
head I call reforming in faith and feeling.
For then the soul somewhat feeleth in under-
standing of that thing that it had before in
naked trowing. And that is the beginning
of contemplation, of which Saint Paul saith
thus: Our contemplation 1s not in things that
are seen, but it is in things unseeable. For

* Heb. xii 29.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

things that are seen are passing, but unseeable
things are everlasting.* To which sight every
soul should desire to come, both here in part
and in the bliss of heaven fully. For in that
sight and in that knowing of Jesus is fully the
bliss of a reasonable soul, and endless life.
Thus saith our Lord: Father, this is endless
life ; that Thy chosen souls know Thee and Thy
Son whom Thou hast sent, one soothfast God.

CHAPTER XXXIV

OF TWO MANNERS OF LOVE, REFORMED AND
UNREFORMED, WHAT IT MEANETH. AND
HOW WE SHOULD BE BEHOLDEN TO LOVE
JESUS MUCH FOR OUR MAKING, BUT MORE
FOR OUR AGAIN-BUYING, £ AND MOST OF
ALL FOR OUR SAVING THROUGH THE GIFTS
OF HIS LOVE

BUT now wonderest thou, since this knowing
of God is the bliss and the end of a soul, why
then have I said here before that a soul should
covet naught else but only the love of God;
and spake nothing of this sight, that a soul
should covet this. Unto this I may say thus,
that the sight of Jesus is full bliss of a soul,
and that is not only for the sight, but also for
the blessed love that cometh out of that sight.

+ Ayen-buying—7.e., ransoming, redemption.

Ch. 34] OF PERFECTION

Nevertheless because love cometh out of
knowing, and not knowing out of love, there-
fore it is said that in knowing and in sight
principally of God with love is the bliss of
a soul, and the more He is known the better
is He loved. But forasmuch as to this
knowing, or to this love that cometh of it,
the soul may not come without love, there-
fore I said that thou shouldest covet love, for
love is cause why a soul cometh to this
knowing and to this love that cometh of it.
And on what manner that is I shall tell thee
more openly.

Holy writers say, and sooth it is, that there
is two manner of ghostly love. One is called
formed, another is called unformed. Love
unformed is God Himself, the third Person in
the Trinity—that is, the Holy Ghost. He is
love unformed and unmade as Saint John
saith: God 1s love*—that is, the Holy Ghost.
Love formed is the affection of the soul,
made by the Holy Ghost of the sight and
knowing of soothfastness, that is God only,
stirred and set in Him. This love is called
formed, for it is made by the Holy Ghost.
This love is not God in Himself, for it is
made, but it is the love of the soul, felt of
the sight of Jesus and stirred to Him only.
Now mayest thou see that love formed is
not cause why a soul cometh to the ghostly

* x John iv 8.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

sight of Jesus, as some men would think that
they would love God so burningly as it were —
by their own might, that they were worthy
to have the ghostly knowing of Him. Nay
it is not so. But love unformed, that is,
God Himself, is cause of all this knowing.
For a blind wretched soul is so far from the
clear knowing and the blessed feeling of His
love through sin and frailty of the bodily
kind, that it might never come to it, were it
not for the endless meekness of the love of
God. But then because He loved us so
much, therefore He giveth us His love, that
is the Holy Ghost. He is the giver and the
gift, and maketh us then by that gift to know
and love Him. Lo, this is the love that I
spake of, that thou shouldest only covet and
desire this unformed love that is the Holy
Ghost. For soothly a less thing or a less
gift than He is may not avail to bring us to
the blessed sight of Jesus. And therefore
shall we fully desire and ask of Jesus only
this gift of love, that He would for the much-
ness of His love so blessed touch our hearts
with His unseeable light to the knowing of
Him, and depart with us of His love, that as
He loveth us we might love Him again.
Thus saith Saint John: Love we God now,
for He first loved us.* He loved us much
when He made us to His likeness, but He
*. t John iv 19.

Ch. 34] OF PERFECTION

loved us more when He bought us with His
precious blood, through wilful taking of
death in His manhood, from the power of

loveth us most when He giveth us the gift

making and our buying? Soothly right
naught.

Therefore the most token of love showed
to us, as methinketh, is this; that He giveth
Himself in His Godhead to our souls. He
gave Himself first in His manhead to us for
our ransom, when He offered Himself to the
Father of heaven upon the altar of the Cross.
This was a right fair gift and a right great
token of love. But what time He giveth
Himself in His Godhead to our souls for our
salvation and maketh us to know Him and
to love Him, then loveth He us fully, for
then giveth He Himself to us; and more
might He not give us, nor less might not
suffice tous. And for this skill is it said that
the righting; of a sinful soul through for-

THE! SGAEE [Bk. 2

giveness of sins is aretted and appropered*
principally to the working of the Holy Ghost;
for the Holy Ghost is love, and in the righting
of a soul our Lord Jesus showeth to a soul
most of His love, for He doth away all sin
and oneth it to Him. And that is the best
thing that He may do to a soul, and therefore
it is appropered to the Holy Ghost. The
making of a soul is appropered to the Father,

wit and wisdom that He showed in His
manhood. For He overcame the fiend
principally through wisdom and not through

appropered to the third Person, that is the
Holy Ghost. For therein showed Jesus
most love unto man’s soul,and for that thing
shall He be most loved again. His making
is common to us and to all unreasonable
creatures, for as He ‘made us of might, so
made He them; and therefore is this work
greatest of might but not most of love. Also
the buying is common to us and to all
reasonable souls, as to Jews and Saracens
and to false Christian men. For He died
for all souls alike and bought them, if their

will have the perfect love of it. And also

the righting and the hallowing of our souls
through the gift of the Holy Ghost, that is

This is the love of God that I spake of,
which thou shouldest covet and desire; for
this love is God Himself and the Holy Ghost.
This love unformed, when it is given to us,
worketh in our soul all that is good, and all

loveth us ere that we love Him. For it
cleanseth us first of our sins, and maketh us

world; it slayeth in us all wicked stirrings

dreads; it keepeth us from malicious tempta-
tions of the fiend; and driveth us out from
the business and vanity of the world and from
conversation of worldly lovers, All this doth
the love of God unformed when He giveth
Himself to us. We do right naught but

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

suffer Him and assent to Him; for that is the
most that we do, that we assent wilfully to
His gracious working in us. And yet is not
that will of us, but of His making, so that
methinketh that He doth in us all that is
well done, and yet we see it not. And not ~

And then beginneth the soul to know Him

blessed godhead of Jesus, how that He is all
and that He worketh all, and that all good
deeds that are done and good thoughts are
only of Him; for He is all sovereign might
and all sovereign soothfastness and all
sovereign goodness. And therefore every
good deed is done of Him and by Him, and
He only shall have the worship and the thanks
for all good deeds, and nothing but He.
For though wretched men steal His worship
here for a while, nevertheless at the last end
shall soothfastness show well that Jesus did
all and that man did right naught of himself.
And then shall thieves of God’s good, that
are not accorded with Him here in this life,

Ch. 35] OF PERFECTION

for their trespass be deemed to death, and
Jesus shall be fully worshipped and thanked
of all blessed creatures for His working.
This love is naught else but Jesus Himself,
that for love worketh all this in man’s soul
and reformeth it in feeling to His likeness
as I have before said, and somewhat as I shall
say. This love bringeth into the soul the
fulhead of all virtues, and maketh him all
clean and true, soft and easy, and turneth
him into all love and into liking. And on

thee a little afterward. This love draweth
the soul from vain beholding of worldly
things into contemplation of ghostly creatures
and of God’s privities, from fleshlihead into

CHAPTER XXXV

HOW SOME SOULS LOVE JESUS BY BODILY
FERVOURS, AND BY THEIR OWN MANLY
AFFECTIONS THAT BE STIRRED BY GRACE
AND BY REASON. AND HOW SOME LOVE
JESUS MORE RESTFULLY, BY GHOSTLY
AFFECTIONS ONLY, STIRRED INWARDLY
THROUGH SPECIAL -GRACE OF THE HOLY
GHOST

‘THEN may I say that he that hath most of
this love here in this life most pleaseth God,

THE SCALE [Bk, 2

and most clear sight. shall have of Him and
most fully love Him in the bliss of heaven:
for he hath the most gift of love here in
earth. This love may not be had by a man’s
own travail, as some ween. It is fully had ©
of the gracious gift of Jesus, after much
bodily and ghostly travail going before.
For there are some lovers of God that make
themselves to love God as it were by their
own might; for they strain themselves
through great violence and pant so strongly
that they burst into bodily fervours as if
they would draw down God from heaven
to them. And they say in their hearts and
with their mouth, ‘‘ Ah, Lord, I love Thee,
and I would love Thee. I would for this
love suffer death.” And in this manner of
working they feel great fervour and much
grace. And sooth it is, as methinketh, this
working is good and meedful, if it be well
tempered with meekness and with discretion.
Nevertheless these men love not nor have
not the gift of love on that manner as I speak
of, nor they ask it not so. For a soul that
hath the gift of love through gracious behold-
ing of Jesus as I mean—or else if he have it
not yet but would have it—he is not busy to
strain himself over his might, as it were by
bodily strength, to have it by bodily fervours
and so to feel and love God. But he
thinketh that he is right naught of himself

Ch. 35] OF PERFECTION

and that he can do right naught; but as it
were a dead thing only, hanging and borne
up by the mercy of God. He seeth well
that Jesus is all and doth all, and therefore
he asketh naught else but the gift of love.
For since the soul seeth that his own love is
naught, therefore it would have His love,
for that is enough. Therefore prayeth he
and that desireth he, that the love of God
would touch him with His blessed light,
that he might see a little of Him by His
gracious presence, for then should he love
Him. And so by this way cometh the gift
of love that is God into a soul.

The more that a soul naughteth itself
through grace by sight of this soothfastness
—sometimes without any fervour outward
showed—and the less it thinketh that it
loveth or seeth God, the nearer it nigheth to
perceive the gift of blessed love. For then
is love master and worketh in the soul and
maketh it forget itself, and see and behold
only how love doth. And then is the soul
more suffering than doing, and that is clean
love. Thus Saint Paul meaned when he
said: All these that are wrought with the spirit
of God, are God’s sons.* ‘That is, souls that
are made so meek and so buxom to God that
they work not of themselves but suffer the
Holy Ghost to stir them and work in them

* Rom. viii 14.

THE! SCALE [Bk. 2

the feelings of love with a full sweet accord
to His stirrings, these are specially God’s
sons, most like unto Him.

Other souls that cannot love thus, but
travail themselves by their own affections and |
stir themselves by their own thinking of God
and bodily exercise to draw out of themselves
by mastery the feeling of love by fervours
and other bodily signs, love not ghostly.
They do well and meedfully, if so be they
will know meekly that their working is not
the kindly gracious feeling of love, but is
manly doing by a soul at the bidding of
reason. And nevertheless through the good-
ness of God, because the soul doth what in
it is, these manly affections of the soul,
stirred unto God by man’s workings, are
turned into ghostly affections, and are
meedful as if they had been done ghostly in
the first beginning. And this is a great
courtesy of our Lord showed to meek souls,
that turneth all these manly affections of
kindly love into the affection and into the
meed of His own love; as if He had wrought
them all fully by Himself. And so these
manly affections so turned may be called
affections of ghostly love through purchase,
not through kindly bringing forth of the
Holy Ghost. I say not that a soul may work
such manly affections only of itself without
grace; for I wot well that Saint Paul saith

Ch. 35] OF PERFECTION

that we may do right naught nor think
aught that is good of ourselves without grace.
We that love God ween not that we suffice to
love or to think good of ourselves only, but our
sufficing is of God.* For God worketh in us
all, both good will and good work, as Saint
Paul saith: Itis God that worketh in us good
will and fulfilling of good will.t But I say
that such affections are good, made by the
will and mean of a soul after the general
grace that He giveth to all chosen souls; not
of special grace made ghostly by touching of
His gracious presence as He worketh in His
perfect lovers, as I said before. For in
unperfect lovers love worketh farly by the
affections of man; but in perfect lovers love
worketh nearly by His own ghostly affections,
and slayeth in a soul for the time all other
affections both fleshly, kindly and manly.
And that is properly the working of love by
Himself. This love may be had a little in
part here in a clean soul, through the ghostly
sight of Jesus; but in the bliss of heaven it is
fulfilled by clear sight in His godhead, for
there shall none affections be felt in a soul
but godly and ghostly.

CHAPTER XXXVI

THAT AMONG ALL OTHER GIFTS OF JESUS THE
GIFT OF LOVE IS WORTHIEST AND MOST.
PROFITABLE. AND HOW JESUS DOTH ALL
THAT IS WELL DONE IN HIS LOVERS ONLY
FOR LOVE. AND HOW LOVE MAKETH THE
USING OF ALL VIRTUES AND OF ALL GOOD
DEEDS LIGHT AND EASY

ASK thou then of God nothing but this gift
of love, that is, the Holy Ghost. For among
all the gifts that our Lord giveth there is none
so good nor so profitable, so worthy nor so `
excellent, as this is. For there is no gift of
God that is both the giver and the gift but
this gift of love; and therefore it is the best
and the worthiest.. The gift of prophecy,
the gift of miracles-working, the gift of great
knowing and counselling, and the gift of
great fasting or of great penance-doing or
any other such, are great gifts of the Holy
Ghost; but they are not the Holy Ghost, for
a reproved soul and a damnable might have
all those gifts as well as a chosen soul. And
therefore all these manner gifts are not greatly
to desire nor much to charge.

But the gift of love is the Holy Ghost, God
Himself; and Him may no soul have and be
damned withal. For that gift only saveth it
from damnation, and maketh it God’s son,

Ch. 36] OF PERFECTION

perceiver* of heavenly heritage. And that
love, as I have before said, is not the affection
of love that is formed in a soul, but it is the
Holy Ghost Himself, that is love unformed,
that saveth a soul. For He giveth Himself
to a soul first, ere yet the soul loveth Him;
and He formeth the affection in the soul and
maketh the soul to love Him only for Him-
self. And not only that, but also by this gift
the soul loveth itself, and all his even-
Christians as itself, only for God; and this is
the gift of love that maketh division betwixt
chosen souls and reproved. And this gift
maketh full peace atwixt God and a soul
and oneth all blessed creatures wholly in
God; for it maketh Jesus to love us, and us
Him also, and every one of us to love each
other in Him.

Covet this gift of love principally as I have
said. For if He will of His grace give it
on that manner wise, it shall open and light
the reason of thy soul to see soothfastness,
that is God and ghostly things. And it shall
stir thine affection wholly and fully to love
Him, and it shall work in thy soul only as He
will, and thou shalt behold Jesus reverently
with softness of love and see how He doth.
Thus biddeth He by His prophet that we
should do, saying: Cease ye, and see that I
am God.t ‘That is, ye that are reformed in

to the feeling of His love. Then bringeth

higher hand. Nevertheless they shall have

CHAPTER XXXVII

HOW LOVE, THROUGH GRACIOUS BEHOLDING
OF JESUS, SLAYETH ALL STIRRINGS OF
PRIDE, AND MAKETH THE SOUL TO LOSE
SAVOUR AND DELIGHT IN ALL EARTHLY
WORSHIP

NEVERTHELESS how love slayeth sins and
reformeth virtues in a soul more specially
shall I say; and first of pride, and of meekness
that is contrary virtue thereto. Thou shalt
understand that there is two manner of
meekness. One is had by working of reason,
another is felt of the special gift of love.
Both are of love; the one love worketh by
reason of the soul, but the other He worketh
by Himself. The first is imperfect, that
other is perfect.

The first meekness that a man feeleth of
beholding of his own sins and of his own
wretchedness, through the which beholding
he thinketh himself unworthy to have any
gift of grace or any mercy of God; but he
thinketh it enough that He would of His
great mercy grant him forgiveness of his
sins. And also he thinketh that, because
of his own sins, he is worse than the most
sinner that liveth, and that every man doth
better than he. And so by such beholding

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

thrusteth he himself down in his thought
under all men; and he is busy to withstand
the stirrings of pride as much as he may,
both bodily pride and ghostly, and despiseth
himself so that he assenteth not to the feelings _
of pride. And if his heart be taken sometime
with it, that it be defiled with vain joy of
worship, or of cunning or of praising or of
any other thing, as soon as he may perceive
it he is evil paid with himself, and hath
sorrow for it in heart, and asketh forgiveness
for it of God and showeth him to his con-
fessor and accuseth himself meekly, and
receiveth his penance. This is good meek-
ness, but it is not yet perfect meekness, for
it is of souls that are beginning and profiting
in grace, caused of beholding of sins. Love
worketh this meekness by reason of the soul.

Perfect meekness a soul feeleth of the sight
and the ghostly knowing of Jesus. For when
the Holy Ghost lighteneth the reason into
the sight of soothfastness, how Jesus is all
and doth all, the soul hath so great love and
so great joy in that ghostly sight, for it is so
soothfast, that it forgetteth itself and fully
leaneth to Jesus with all the love that it hath
to behold Him. It taketh no keep of un-,
worthiness of itself nor of sins beforedone,
but setteth itself at naught, with all the sins
and all the good deeds that ever it did, as if
there were nothing but Jesus. Thus meek

Ch. 37] OF PERFECTION

was David when he said thus: Lord Fesus;
the sight of Thy blessed unmade substance and
Thine endless being showeth well unto me that
my substance and the being of my soul ts as
naught anent Thee.* Also anent his even-
Christians he hath no regard to them, nor
deeming of them whether they be better or
worse than he himself is. For he holdeth
himself and all other men as it were even,
alike naught of themselves anent God, whom
he beholdeth as all. And therefore setteth
he all other creatures at naught, as he doth
himself. Thus meek was the prophet when
he said thus: All men are before the Lord as
naught, and as unnotefult and naught are they
accounted to Him.£ That is, anent the end-
less being and the unchangeable kind of God,
mankind is as naught. For of naught iis it
made, and to naught should it turn, unless
He keep it in being that made it of naught.
This is soothfastness, and this should make
a soul meek if it might see through grace
this soothfastness. Therefore, when love
openeth the inner eye of the soul to see this
soothfastness with other circumstances that
come withal, then beginneth the soul to be
soothfastly meek. For then, by the sight
of God, it feeleth and seeth itself as it is; and

then forsaketh the soul the beholding and

And when it doth so, then setteth the soul
at naught all the joy and the worship of the

little and so naught, in the regard of that joy

that though it might have it without any sin
he would naught of it. For though men
would worship him, praise him, favour him
or set him at great state, it liketh him
naught: nor though he had cunning of all the
seven arts, of clergy, and of all crafts under

nor no more savour of them than to gnaw on
a dry stick. He had well liefer forget all
this and be alone out of the sight of the
world, than to think on them and to be
worshipped of all men. For the heart of a
true lover of Jesus is made so much and so
large through a little sight of Him and a little
feeling of His love, that all the liking and all
the joy of all earth may not suffice to fill a
corner of it. And then seemeth it well that
these wretched worldly lovers—that are as
it were ravished in love of their own worship,
and pursue after it to have it with all their
might and all their wit—have no savour in

Ch. 37] OF PERFECTION

this meekness, for they are wondrous far
therefrom. But the lover of Jesus hath this

bécause he forsaketh the worship of the
world—for that were a proud meekness that
belongeth to an hypocrite—but because he
hath a sight and ghostly knowing of sooth-

gift of the Holy Ghost.

That reverent sight and that lovely behold-
ing of Jesus comforteth the soul so wonder-
fully and beareth it up so mightily and so
softly, that it may not like nor fully rest in
any earthly joy, nor will it. He taketh no

He setteth it not at heart neither to be well
paid if men despise him—as for more meek-

worship him or praise him. He had liefer
to forget both that one and that other, and
think only on Jesus, and get meekness that
way; and that is much the more secure way
for whoso might come thereto. Thus did
David when he said: Mine eyes are ever open
to Fesus our Lord, for why, He shall pluck my
feet from snares of sins.* For when he doth
so, then forsaketh he utterly himself and

of other men, that it should, by that, fall
into heaviness and sorrow. It cometh also
as an arrow flying in the day, when a man is
worshipped and praised of all men, whether
it be for worldly doing or for ghostly, that
he should have vain joy in himself restingly
and false gladness in a passing thing. This is
a sharp arrow and a perilous; it flieth swiftly,
it striketh softly, but it woundeth deadly.
But the lover of Jesus, that stably beholdeth
Him by devout prayers and busy thinking
on, Him, is so belapped with the secure
shield of soothfastness that He dreadeth not,
for this arrow may not enter into the soul.

For though it come it hurteth not, but glinteth
away and passeth forth. And thus is the
soul made meek as I understand, by the
working of the Holy Ghost that is the gift of
love. For He openeth the eye of the soul
to see the love of Jesus, and He keepeth the
soul in that sight restfully and securely, and
He slayeth all the stirrings of pride wonder-
fully and privily and softly, and the soul wots
never how. And also He bringeth in by that
way soothfastly and lovely the virtue of
meekness. All this doth love, but not in
all His lovers alike full. For some have this
grace but shortly and little, as it were in the
beginning of it, and a little assaying towards;
for their conscience is not yet cleansed fully
through grace. And some have it more
fully, for they have clearer sight of Jesus, and
they feel more of His love. And some have
it most fully, for they have the full gift of
contemplation. Nevertheless he that least
hath on this manner as I have said, I hope
soothly he hath the gift of perfect meekness,
for he hath the gift of perfect love.

CHAPTER XXXVIII

HOW LOVE SLAYETH ALL STIRRINGS OF WRATH
AND ENVY SOFTLY, AND REFORMETH IN
THE SOUL THE VIRTUES OF PEACE AND
PATIENCE AND OF PERFECT CHARITY TO HIS
EVEN-CHRISTIAN, AS HE DID SPECIALLY IN
THE APOSTLES

Love worketh wisely and softly in a soul
where He will, for He slayeth mightily ire
and envy and all passions of angriness and
melancholy in it, and bringeth into the soul
virtues of patience and mildness, peace-
ability and love to his even-Christians. It
is full hard and great mastery to a man that
standeth only in working of his own reason
to keep patience, holy rest and softness in
heart, and charity to his even-Christians, if
they dis-ease him unskilfully and do him
wrong, that he shall not somewhat do again
to them through stirring of ire or of melan-
choly, either in speaking or in working or
in both. Nevertheless though a man be
stirred and troubled in himself and made
unrestful, so that it be not too much passing
over the bounds of reason, and that he keep
his hand and his tongue and be ready to
forgive the trespass when mercy is asked,
yet this man hath the virtue of patience,

Ch. 38] OF PERFECTION

though it be but weak and nakedly; foras-
much as he would have it, and travaileth
busily in refraining of his unskilful passions
that he might have it, and also is sorry that
he hath it not as he should. But to a true
lover of Jesus it is no great mastery to suffer
all this, for why, love fighteth for him, and
slayeth wonder softly such stirrings of wrath
and of melancholy, and maketh his soul so
easy and so peaceable, so suffering and so
godly—through the ghostly sight of Jesus
with the feeling of His blessed love—that
though he be despised and reproved of other
men, or take wrong or harm, shame or
villainy, he chargeth it not. He is not much
stirred against them, he will not be angered
nor stirred against. them; for if he were much
stirred he should forbear the comfort that
he feeleth within his soul, but that will he
not. He may lightlier forget all the wrong
that is done to him, than another may forgive
it though mercy were asked. And so he
had well liefer forget it; for he thinketh it
most ease to him.

- And love doth all this, for love openeth
the eyes of the soul to the sight of Jesus,
stableth it with the liking of love that it
feeleth by that sight, and comforteth it so
mightily that it taketh no heed; whatsoever
men jangle or do against him it hangeth
nothing upon them. The most harm that

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

he might have were a forbearing of the ghostly
sight of Jesus. And therefore it is liefer to
him to suffer all harms than that alone. All
this may the soul do well and easily without .
great troubling of the ghostly sight, when
dis-ease falleth all without forth and toucheth
not the body: as is backbiting and scorning
or spoiling of such as he hath. All this
grieveth not. But it goeth somewhat nearer
when the flesh is touched and he feeleth
smart; then is it harder. Nevertheless
though it be hard and impossible to the
frail kind of man to suffer bodily penance
gladly and patiently, without bitter stirrings
of ire, anger and melancholy, it is not
impossible to love—that is the Holy Ghost
—to work this in a soul where He toucheth
with the blessed gift of love. But He giveth
a soul that is in that plight mighty feelings
of love, and wonderfully fasteneth it to
Jesus, and departeth it wonderfully far from
the sensuality through His privy might, and
comforteth it so sweetly by His blessed
presence that the soul feeleth little pain or
else none of the sensuality; and this is a
special grace given to the holy martyrs.
This grace had the apostles, as Holy Writ
saith of them thus: The apostles came joying
from the counsel of the Jews, when they were
beaten with scourges, and they were glad that
they were worthy to suffer any bodily dis-ease

Ch. 38] OF PERFECTION

for the love of Fesus.* They were not stirred
to ire nor to felness, to be avenged on the
Jews that beat them; as a worldly man would
be when he suffered a little harm—be it
never so little—of his even-Christians. Nor
were they stirred to pride, nor to highness of
themselves, nor to disdain and to deeming
of the Jews, as hypocrites and heretics are,
that will suffer much bodily pain, and are
sometimes ready to suffer death with great
gladness and with mighty will, as it were in
the name of Jesus and for love of Him.
Soothly that love and that gladness that they
have in suffering of bodily mischief is not of
the Holy Ghost; it cometh not from the fire
that burneth in the high altar of heaven, but.
it is feigned by the fiend enflamed of hell.
For it is fully mixed with the height of pride
and the presumption of themselves, and
despite and deeming and disdain of them that
thus punish them. ‘They ween that all this
is charity, and that they suffer all that for
the love of God, but they are beguiled of the
midday fiend. A true lover of Jesus, when
he suffereth harm of his even-Christian, is
so strengthened of the Holy Ghost, and is
made so meek, so patient, so peaceable, and
that soothfastly, that what wrong or harm it
be that he suffer of his even-Christian he
keepeth ever meekness. He despiseth him
KACE V ATS

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

not, he deemeth him not, but he prayeth
for him in his heart and hath of him pity
and compassion, much more tenderly than
of another man that never did him harm;
and soothly better loveth him, and more .
fervently desireth the salvation of his soul,
because he seeth that he shall have so much
ghostly profit of the evil deed of that other
man, though it be against his will.. But this
love and this meekness worketh only the
Holy Ghost, above the kind of man, in them
that He maketh the true lovers of Jesus.

CHAPTER XXXIX

HOW LOVE SLAYETH COVETOUSNESS, LECHERY
AND GLUTTONY, AND SLAYETH THE FLESHLY
SAVOUR AND DELIGHT IN ALL THE BODILY
WITS, SOFTLY AND EASILY, THROUGH A
GRACIOUS BEHOLDING OF JESUS :

COVETOUSNESS also is slain in a soul by the
working of love, for it maketh the soul so
covetous of ghostly good, and to heavenly
riches so ardent, that it setteth right naught
by all earthly things. It hath no more dainty
in having of a precious stone than of a chalk
stone; nor no more love hath he in a hundred
pound of gold than in a pound of lead. It
setteth all things that shall perish at one price,
nor chargeth that one more than that other

Ch, 39] _ OF PERFECTION

as in his love. For it seemeth well that all
these earthly things—that worldly men set
so great price upon and love so daintily—
should pass away and turn to naught, both
the thing in itself and the love of it. And
therefore he bringeth it in his thought
betimes into the plight that it shall be in
after, and so accounteth it as naught. And
when worldly lovers strive and fight and plead
for earthly good, who may first have it, the
lover of Jesus striveth with no man, but
keepeth himself in peace and holdeth him
paid with that that he hath, and will strive
for no more; for he thinketh that he needeth
no more of all the riches in earth than a scant.
bodily sustenance to save the bodily life
withal, as long as God will: and that he
may lightly have. And therefore will he have
no more than scantily he needeth for the
time, that he may freely be discharged from
business about the keeping and the dispend-:
ing of it, and fully give his. heart and his
business about the seeking of Jesus, to find
Him in cleanness of spirit. For that is all
his covetousness, for why, only the clean of
heart shall see Him. Also fleshly love of
father and mother and other worldly friends,
hangeth not upon, him; it is even cut from
his heart with the sword of ghostly love, that
he hath no more affection to father or mother
or to any worldly friend than he hath to

THE! SCALE [Bk. 2

another man, unless he see or feel in him more
grace or more virtue than in other men.
Save only this, that he had liefer his father
and mother had the same grace that some
other men have; but nevertheless if they be -
not so, then loveth he other men better than
them, and that is charity. And so God’s
love slayeth covetousness of the world, and
bringeth poverty in spirit into the soul. And
that doth love not only in them that have
right naught of worldly goods, but also in
some creatures that are in great worldly
state and have dispending of earthly riches.
Love slayeth in some of them covetousness
so far forth that they have no more liking nor
savour in having of them than of a straw. .
Nor though they be lost, for default of
them that should keep them, they set naught:
thereby; for why, the heart of God’s lover,
through the gift of the Holy Ghost, is taken
so fully with the sight and the love of another
thing, that is Jesus: and that is so precious
and so worthy, that it will receive none other
love restingly that is contrary thereto.
And not only doth love this, but also it
slayeth the liking of lechery and all other
bodily uncleanness, and bringeth into the
soul very chastity, and turneth it into liking.
For the soul feeleth so great delight in the
sight of Jesus that it liketh to be chaste, and
it is no great hardness to it to keep chastity

Ch. 39] OF PERFECTION

for it is so most easy and most at rest. And
upon the same wise the gift of love slayeth
lusts of gluttony and maketh the soul sober
and temperate, and beareth it up so mightily
that it may not rest in liking of meat and
drink, but it taketh meat and drink, what it
be that least grieveth the bodily complexion,
if it may lightly come thereto; not for love
of itself but for the love of God. On this
manner wise the lover of God seeth well that
he needeth to keep his bodily life with meat
and drink as long as God will suffer them to
be together.

Then shall this be the discretion of the
lover of Jesus, as I understand, that hath
feeling and working in the love; that upon
what manner that he may most keep His
grace whole and least be letted from working
in it through taking of bodily sustenance,
so shall he do. ‘That manner of meat that
least letteth and least troubleth the heart,
and may keep the body in strength—be it
flesh, be it fish, be it bread or ale—I trow
the soul chooseth to have it, if it may come
thereby. For all the business of the soul is
to think on Jesus with reverent love ever,
without letting of anything, if it might.
And therefore, since it must needs somewhat
be letted and hindered, the less it is letted
and hindered by meat and drink or any other
thing the liefer it is. It had liefer use the

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

best meat and most of price under the sun,
if it less letted the keeping of his heart, than
to take bread and water if it letted him more;
for he hath no regard to get him great meed
for the pain of fasting and be put thereby
from softness in heart, but all his business
is to keep his heart as stably as he may in
the sight of Jesus and in the feeling of His
love. And soothly as I trow, he might with
less liking use the best meat that is good in
its own kind, than another man that worketh
all in reason without the special gift of love
should more use the worst, save only meat
that through craft of curiosity is only made
for lust. ‘That manner of meat may he not
well accord withal. And also on that other
side, if little meat, as only bread and ale, most
helpeth and easeth his heart and keepeth it
most in peace, it is most lief then to him to
use it so: and namely if he feel bodily strength
only of the gift of love withal. And yet doth
love more, for it slayeth accidie and fleshly
idleness, and maketh the soul lively and
speedy to the service of Jesus; so far forth
that it coveteth ever to be occupied in good-
ness, and in inward beholding of Him by
the virtue of which the soul hath savour and
ghostly delight in praying and thinking, and
in all other manner of doing that needeth to
be done after the state that he is in,, without
heaviness or painful bitterness, whether he

Ch. 39] OF PERFECTION

be religious or secular. Also it slayeth the
vain liking of the five bodily wits. First the
sight of the eye, that the soul hath no liking
in the sight of any worldly thing; but it
feeleth rather pain and dis-ease in beholding
of it, be it never so fair, never so precious,
never so wonderful. And therefore as
worldly lovers run out sometimes to see new
things, to wonder on them, and so to feed
their heart with the vain sight of them; right
so a lover of Jesus is ready to run away and
withdraw him from the sight of such manner
things, that the inner sight be not letted, for
he seeth ghostly another manner thing that
is fairer and more wonderful, and that would
he not forbear.

Right on the same wise is it of speaking
and hearing. It is a pain to the soul of a
lover of Jesus to speak or to hear anything
that might let the freedom of his heart to
think on Jesus. What song or melody or
minstrelsy outward that it be, if it let the
thought that it may not freely and restfully
pray or think on Him, it liketh him right
naught; and the more delectable it is to other
men the more unsavoury it is to him. And
also to hear any manner of speaking of other
men—unless it be somewhat touching the
working of his soul in the love of Jesus—it
liketh him right naught; he is else right soon
irked of it. He had well liefer be in peace

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

and hear right naught nor speak right naught,
than to hear the speaking and the teaching
of the greatest clerk of earth, with all the
reasons that he can say to him through man’s
wits, unless he can speak feelingly and `
stirringly of the love of Jesus; for that is his
craft principally. And therefore would he
naught else speak, hear nor see, but what
might help him and further him into more
knowing and to better feeling of Him. Of
worldly speech it is no doubt that he hath
no savour in speaking nor in hearing of it,
nor in worldly tales nor in tidings, nor in
none such vain jangling that belongeth not to
Him. And so it is of smelling and savouring.
The more that the thought should be dis-
tracted and broken from ghostly rest by the
use either of smelling or savouring or of
any of the bodily wits, the more he fleeth it.
The less he feeleth of them, the liefer it is to
him; and if he might live in the body without
the feeling of any of them, he would never
feel them. For they trouble the heart oft-
times and put it from rest, and they may not
fully be eschewed. Nevertheless the love
of Jesus is sometimes so mighty in a soul that
it overcometh and slayeth all that is contrary
thereto for a time.

Ch. 40] OF PERFECTION

CHAPTER XL

WHAT VIRTUES AND GRACES A SOUL RECEIVETH
THROUGH OPENING. OF THE INNER EYE
UNTO THE GRACIOUS BEHOLDING OF JESUS;
AND HOW IT MAY NOT BE GOT THROUGH
MAN’S TRAVAIL ONLY BUT THROUGH
SPECIAL GRACE AND TRAVAIL ALSO .

Tuus worketh love in a soul, opening the
ghostly eye into beholding of Jesus by
inspiration of special grace, and maketh it
clean, subtle and able to the work of con-
templation. What this opening of the ghostly
eye is the greatest clerk on earth cannot
imagine by his wit, nor show fully by his
tongue. For it may not be got by study nor
through man’s travail only, but principally
by grace of the Holy Ghost and with travail
of man. I dread much to speak aught of it,
for methinketh I cannot; it passeth mine
assay, and my lips are unclean. Neverthe-
less, because I think that love asketh and love
biddeth, therefore I shall say a little more of
it as I think love teacheth. ‘This opening
of the ghostly eye is that lightsome darkness
and rich naught that I spake of before, and
it may be called purity of spirit and ghostly
rest, inward stillness and peace of conscience,
highness of thought and onl-iness of soul, a

THE ! SCALE [Bk. 2

lively feeling of grace and purity of heart,
the waking sleep of the spouse and tasting
of heavenly savour, burning in love and
shining in light, entry of contemplation and
reforming in feeling. All these reasons are
said in Holy Writ by divers men, for each of -
them spake of it after his feeling in grace.
And though all these be divers in the showing `
of words, nevertheless they are all one in
sentence of soothfastness.

For a soul that through visiting of grace
hath one, hath all; for why, a soul sigħing to
see the face of Jesus, when it is touched
through special grace of the Holy Ghost, is
suddenly changed and turned from the plight
that it was in to another manner of feeling.
It is wonderfully departed and drawn first
into itself from the love and the liking of all
worldly thing; so much that it hath lost the
savour of the bodily life and of all thing that
is, save only Jesus. And then it is clean
from all the filth of sin, so far forth that the
mind of it and all unordained affection of any
creature is suddenly washed and wiped
away, that there is no mean letting betwixt
Jesus and the soul, but only the bodily life.
And then it is in ghostly rest; for why, all
painful doubts and dreads and all other
temptations of ghostly enemies are driven
out of the heart, that they trouble not nor
sink therein for the time. It is in rest from

Ch. 40] OF PERFECTION

the noise of worldly business and painful
tarryings of wicked stirrings; but it is full
busy in the free ghostly working of love. And
the more it travaileth so, the more rest he
feeleth. This restful travail is full far from
fleshly idleness and from blind security. It
is full of ghostly work; but it is called rest,
because grace looseth the heavy yoke of
fleshly love from the soul, and maketh it
mighty and free through the gift of ghostly
love, to work gladly, softly and delectably
in all things that grace stirreth it to work in.
And therefore it is called a holy idleness and
a rest most busy; and so is it in inwardness
from the great crying of the beastly noise of
fleshly desires and unclean thoughts.

This stillness maketh inspiration of the
Holy Ghost in beholding of Jesus. For why,
His voice is so sweet and so mighty that it
putteth to silence in a soul all jangling of
other speakers; for it is a voice of virtue,
softly sounded in a soul, of the which the
prophet saith thus: The voice of our Lord
Jesus is in virtue.* This voice is a lively
word and a speedy, as the apostle saith:
Quick is the word of Jesus and speedy, more
piercing than any sword isf Through speak-
ing of this word is fleshly love slain, and the
soul kept in silence from all wicked stirrings.
Of this silence it is said in the Apocalypse

* Ps, xxviii 4. + Heb.iv 12.

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

thus: Silence was made in heaven as it were
an half hour.* Heaven is a clean soul,
through grace lifted up from earthly love to
heavenly conversation, so that it is in silence;
but forasmuch as that silence may not last
' whole continually for corruption of the bodily
kind, therefore it is likened to the time of
half an hour. A full short time the soul
thinketh it is, be it never so long; and there-
fore it is but halfan hour. And then hath it
peace in conscience; for why, grace putteth
out gnawing, pricking, stirring and flitingt
of sins, and bringeth in peace and accord,
and maketh Jesus and a soul both one in
full accordance of will. There is none up-
braiding of sins nor sharp reproving of
defaults made at that time in a soul, for they
have kissed and made friends and all is
forgiven that was misdone.

Thus feeleth the soul with full meek like-
ness and great ghostly gladness, and. it
conceiveth a full great boldness of salvation
by this accord-making, for it heareth a privy
witnessing in conscience of the Holy Ghost,
that it is a chosen son to a heavenly heritage.
Thus Saint Paul saith: The Holy Ghost
beareth witness to our spirit that we- are
God’s sons.£ This witnessing of conscience,

* Apoc. viii I.
+ Fliting—7.e., contention, scolding.
+ Rom. viii 16.

Ch. 40} OF PERFECTION

soothfastly felt through grace, is the very joy
of the soul, as the apostle saith: My joy ts the
witness of my conscience,* and that is when
it witnesseth peace and accord, true‘ love
and friendship betwixt Jesus and a “soul.
And when it is in this peace, then it is in
highness of thought.

When the soul is bound with love of the
world, then is it beneath all creatures; for
everything overgoeth it and beareth it down
by mastery, that it may not see Jesus nor love
Him. For right as the love of the world is
vain and fleshly, right so the beholding and
thinking and the using of all creatures is
fleshly; and that is a thraldom of the soul.
But then, through opening of the ghostly eye
into Jesus, the love is turned and the soul is
raised up after his own kind above all bodily
creatures; and then the beholding and think-
ing and the using of them is ghostly, for the
love is ghostly. The soul hath then full
great disdain to be buxom to love of worldly
things; for it is high set above them through
grace. It setteth naught by all the world,
for why, all shall pass and perish. Unto this
highness of heart, while the soul is kept
therein, cometh none error nor deceit of the
fiend, for Jesus is soothfastly in sight of the
soul at that time and all things are beneath
Him. Of this the prophet pears thus:

* 2 Cog. 12;

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

Come man to high heart and God shall be
highed.*That is, a man that through grace
cometh to the highness of thought, shall see
that Jesus only is highed above all creatures
and he in Him.

And then is the soul alone, much estranged
from friendship of worldly lovers, though
her body be in the midst among them; full
far departed from fleshly affections of

Jesus. So that the same grace that turneth

perfect; and that grace is called a lively
feeling of grace, for he that hath it feeleth
well by experience that he is in grace. It

is full lively to him, for it quickeneth the soul

CHAPTER XLI

HOW SPECIAL GRACE IN BEHOLDING OF OUR
LORD JESUS WITHDRAWETH SOMETIMES
FROM A SOUL. AND HOW A SOUL SHALL
BEHAVE HER IN THE ABSENCE AND PRESENCE
OF JESUS. AND HOW A SOUL SHALL DESIRE
THAT THE GRACIOUS PRESENCE OF JESUS
SHALL BE IN IT ALWAYS

SHOW me then a soul that, through inspira-
tion of grace, hath opening of the ghostly
sight into beholding of Jesus; that is departed
and drawn out from the love of the world, so
far forth that it hath purity and poverty of
spirit, ghostly rest, inward silence and peace
in conscience, highness of thought, onli-ness
and privity of heart, wakeful sleep of the
spouse; that hath lost liking and joy of the
world and is taken with delight of heavenly
savour, ever thirsting and softly seeking the
blessed presence of Jesus; and I dare hardily
pronounce that this soul burneth all in love
and shineth in ghostly light, worthy to come
to the name and to the worship of the spouse,
for it is reformed in feeling, made able and
ready to contemplation. These are the
tokens of inspiration in opening of the ghostly
eye. For why, when the eye is opened, the
soul is in full feeling of all these virtues
361 o

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

beforesaid for that time. Nevertheless it
falleth oftentimes that grace withdraweth
in part, through corruption of man’s frailty,
and suffereth then the soul to fall into itself in
fleshlihead, as it was before; and then is the
soul in pain and in sorrow, for it is blind and -
unsavoury and can do no good. It is weak
and unmighty, encumbered with the body
and with all the bodily wits; it seeketh and
desireth after the grace of Jesus again and
may not find it. For Holy Writ saith of our
Lord thus: After when our Lord Jesus hath
hid His face, there 1s none that may behold
Him.* When He showeth Him, the soul
may not unsee Him for He is light; and when
He hideth Him it may not see Him, for the
soul is dark. His hiding is but a subtle
assaying of the soul, His showing is wondrous
merciful goodness in comfort of the soul.
Have ye no wonder though the feeling of
grace be withdrawn sometimes from a lover
of Jesus. For Holy Writ saith the same of
the spouse that she fareth thus: J sought and
I found Him not: I called and He answered
not. That is, when I fall down to my
frailty then grace withdraweth, for my
falling is cause thereof and not His fleeing.
But then feel I pain of my wretchedness in

feelable answering. And then I cried with

comforted in love and in light with ghostly

This changeability of absence and presence
of Jesus that a soul feeleth is not perfection
of the soul, nor is it not against the grace of
perfection or of contemplation;. but in so
much perfection is the less. For the more
letting that a soul hath of itself from continual
feeling of grace, the less is the grace; and
yet nevertheless is the grace in itself grace
of contemplation. This changeability of
absence and presence falleth as well in the
state of perfection as in state of beginning,
but in another manner. For right as there
is diversity of feeling in the presence of grac

Ch. 41] OF PERFECTION

betwixt these two states, right so is there in
the absence of grace. And therefore he that
knoweth not the absence of grace is ready
to be deceived; and he that observeth not the
presence of grace is unkind to the visiting,
whether he is in the state of beginners or
perfect. Nevertheless the more stableness
that there is in grace, unhurt and unbroken,
the lovelier is the soul and the more like unto
Him in whom is no changeability, as the
apostle saith. And it is full seemly that the
soul-spouse be like to Jesu-spouse in manners
and in virtue, full according to Him in
stableness of perfect love. But that falleth
seldom here, except in the special spouse.
For he that perceiveth no changeability in
feeling of his grace, but is ever alike whole
and stable, unbroken and unhurt as he
thinketh, he is either full perfect or full blind.
He is perfect that is sequestered from all
fleshly affections and communing of all
creatures, and all means of corruption and of
sin are broken away betwixt Jesus and his
soul, fully oned to Him with softness of love.
But this is only grace above man’s kind.
He is full blind that feigneth him in grace
without ghostly feeling of God’s inspiration,
and setteth himself in a manner of stableness
as he were ever in feeling and in working of
special grace, deeming that all is grace that
he doth and feeleth without and within,

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

thinking that whatsoever he do or speak is
grace, holding himself unchangeable in
speciality of grace. If there be any such—
as I hope there be none—he is full blind in
feeling of grace.

But then might thou say thus: that we .
should live only in troth and not covet

bodily feelings, be they never so comfortable,
we shall not covet, nor regard them much if

speak of, if they come in the manner that
I have said before, we should ever desire;
the which are slaying of all worldly love,
opening of the ghostly eye, purity of spirit,
peace of conscience, and all other beforesaid.
We should covet to feel ever the lively
inspiration of grace made by the ghostly
presence of Jesus in our soul, if we might;
and to have Him in our sight with reverence,
and ever feel the sweetness of His love by a
wonderful homeliness of His presence. This
should be our life and our feeling in grace,
after the measure of His gift in whom all
grace is, to some more and to some less; for
His presence is felt in divers wise as He
vouchsafeth. And in this we should live,
and work what belongeth for us to work; for

CHAPTER XLII

A COMMENDATION OF. PRAYER OFFERED TO.
JESUS BY A CONTEMPLATIVE SOUL. AND
HOW STABLENESS IN PRAYER IS A SECURE
WORK TO STAND IN. AND HOW EVERY
FEELING OF GRACE IN A CHOSEN SOUL MAY
BE CALLED JESUS, BUT THE MORE CLEANER
THE SOUL IS, THE WORTHIER IS THE GRACE

THE soul of man, while it is not touched with
special grace, is blunt and boisterous to
ghostly: work, and can naught therein. It
may not thereof for weakness of itself; it is
both cold and dry, undevout and unsavoury
in itself. But then cometh the light of grace,
and through touching maketh it sharp and
subtle, ready and able to ghostly work, and
giveth it a great freedom and an whole
readiness in will to be buxom to all the stirring
of grace, ready to work after that grace
stirreth the soul. For by opening of the
ghostly eye it is applied all fully to grace,
ready to pray. And how the soul prayeth
then shall I tell thee.

The most special prayer that the soul
useth and hath most comfort in I trow is the
Pater noster, or else psalms of the psalter;
the Pater noster for lewd men, and psalms
and hymns and other service of Holy Church

Ch. 42] OF PERFECTION

for lettered men. The soul prayeth then
not in manner as it did before, nor in common
manner of men in highness of voice or by
reasonable speaking out; but in full great
stillness of voice and softness of heart.
For why, his mind is not troubled nor
tarried with outward things, but whole
gathered together in itself, and the soul is
set as it were in a ghostly presence of Jesus;
and therefore every word and every syllable
is sounded savourly, sweetly and delectably,
with full accord of mouth and of heart. For
why, the soul is turned then all into fire of
love, and therefore every word it privily
prayeth is like to a sparkle springing out of a
fire, that burneth and chafeth all the mights
of the soul, and turneth them into love, and
lighteneth them so comfortably that the soul
listeth ever to pray and do none other thing.
The more it prayeth, the better it may, the
mightier it is. For grace helpeth the soul
well and maketh all things light and easy, that
it list right well to psalm and sing the laudings
of God with ghostly mirth in heavenly delight.

This ghostly work is food of the soul, and
this prayer is of much virtue. For it wasteth
and bringeth to naught all temptations of the
fiend, privy and open, it slayeth all the mind
and the liking of the world and of fleshly
sins, it beareth up the body and the soul
from painful feeling of wretchedness of this

THE SCALE [Bk. 2

life, it keepeth the soul in feeling of grace
and working of love, and nourisheth it ever
alike hot and fresh, as sticks nourish fire.
It putteth away all irking and heaviness of
heart, and holdeth it in might and in ghostly -
gladness. Of this prayer speaketh David
‘thus: Dressed be my prayer, Lord, as incense
in Thy sight.* For right as incense that is
cast in the fire maketh sweet smell by the
reek ascending up into the air; right so a
psalm, savourly and softly sung or said in a
burning heart, yieldeth up a sweet smell to
the face of our Lord Jesus and to all the
court of heaven. There dare no flesh-fly
rest upon the pot’s brink boiling on the fire;
right so there may no fleshly delight rest
upon a clean soul, that is wrapped and
warmed all in the fire of love, boiling and
blowing psalms and laudings to Jesus. This
prayer is ever heard of Jesus; it yieldeth
grace to Jesus, and receiveth grace again.
It maketh a soul homely and fellowly with
Jesus, and with all the angels of heaven.
Use it whoso may, the work is good and
gracious in itself. And though it be not all
fully contemplation in itself, nor the working
of love by itself, nevertheless it is a part of
contemplation. For why, it may not be
done on this manner wise save in plenty of
grace through opening of the ghostly eyes,
“VPsitex tat

Ch. 42] OF PERFECTION

and therefore a soul that hath this freedom
and this gracious feeling in prayer, with
ghostly savour and heavenly delight, hath
the grace of contemplation in manner as it is.

This prayer is a rich offering filled with all
fatness of devotion, received by angels and
presented to the face of Jesus. The prayer
of other men, that are busy in active works,
is made of two words. For they ofttimes
form in their heart one word through thinking
of worldly business, and sound in their
mouth another word of the psalm sung or
said; yet nevertheless, if their intent be true,
their prayer is good and speedful, though it
lack savour and sweetness. But this manner
offered of a man contemplative is made but
of one word. For as it is formed in the
heart, right so wholly it soundeth in the
mouth, as it were naught but one thing that
formeth and soundeth. And soothly no
more it is, for the soul through grace is made
whole in itself, so far. departed from the
fleshlihead that it is master of the body; and
then is the body naught else, but an instru-
ment and a trump of the soul, in which the
soul bloweth sweet notes of ghostly laudings
to Jesus.

This is the trump that David speaketh of
thus: Blow ye with the trump in the new
moon.* That is, ye souls that are reformed

in ghostly life, through opening of the

And therefore, for this prayer is so pleasing
to Jesus and so profitable to the soul, then |
is it good to him that is new turned to God
—whatsoever he be that would please Him
and coveteth to have some quaint feeling of

This is a secure feeling and a soothfast.
If thou may come thereto and hold it, thou
needest not therefore to run about here and
there, and ask questions of each ghostly man
what thou shalt do, how thou shalt love God
and how thou shalt serve God, and speak of
ghostly matters that pass thy knowing, as
perchance some do. That manner is not
full profitable, unless more need make it.
Keep thee to thy prayers stiffly, first with
travail that thou might come afterwards to
this restful feeling of this ghostly prayer, and
that shall teach wisdom enough to thee
soothfastly without feigning or fantasy. And
keep it forth if thou have it, and leave it not;
unless grace come otherwise and will reave it

Ch. 42] OF PERFECTION

another manner; then mayest thou leave it

why, the soul is turned into the eyes and

soul holden in body, after the cleanness that
it hath. For wit thou well, every feeling of
grace is Jesus and may be called Jesus; and,
after that the grace is more or less, so feeleth

for his sins, is verily Jesus. For why, He
maketh that contrition in a soul by His

and rudely felt, full far from His godly
subtlety, for then the soul can no better nor
may no better for uncleanness of itself.
Nevertheless afterward, if the soul profit

same Jesus and none other is seen and felt
of the same soul when it is touched with

THE! SCAEED % [Bk. 2

grace. But that is more ghostly, near to the
godly kind of Jesus.

And soothly that is the most thing that
Jesus loveth in a soul, that it might be made -
godly and ghostly in sight and in love, like
to Him in grace, to that that He is by kind;
for that shall be the end of ail lovers. Then
